# The Great Works Course: full reading plan

Generated from README.md by learn/build.py. Do not edit by hand.

An eleven-phase course through every work in README.md. Each phase has a core sequence (read in order) and a deeper shelf (the rest of that phase's sections, read as interest and time allow). Hour figures are rough reading-time estimates for the suggested portion of each work, not for complete editions.

Core sequence: 206 works, about 2,502 hours. Deeper shelves: the remaining works of each phase's sections.

Each core work lists a recommended edition and places to find it. The search links are queries, not specific copies, so check the translator named before you borrow or buy.

## Contents

1. [Phase I: Tools of Thought](#phase-i) (6 core works, ~63 h)
2. [Phase II: The Greek World](#phase-ii) (11 core works, ~225 h)
3. [Phase III: Rome](#phase-iii) (15 core works, ~195 h)
4. [Phase IV: Scripture and the Early Church](#phase-iv) (19 core works, ~201 h)
5. [Phase V: The Middle Ages](#phase-v) (22 core works, ~232 h)
6. [Phase VI: Judaism and Islam](#phase-vi) (24 core works, ~277 h)
7. [Phase VII: India, China, and Japan](#phase-vii) (30 core works, ~257 h)
8. [Phase VIII: Renaissance and the New Science](#phase-viii) (20 core works, ~251 h)
9. [Phase IX: Enlightenment to the Twentieth Century](#phase-ix) (26 core works, ~335 h)
10. [Phase X: The Modern Imagination](#phase-x) (25 core works, ~397 h)
11. [Phase XI: Capstone: The Forbidden Shelf](#phase-xi) (8 core works, ~69 h)

<a id="phase-i"></a>

## Phase I: Tools of Thought

*4th c. BC to 13th c.. About 63 hours of core reading.*

The medieval curriculum began with the language arts and the mathematical arts before any major author. This phase is short on purpose: learn the vocabulary of logic (genus, species, predication, syllogism), the parts of rhetoric, and the method of a geometric proof. Every later phase uses them.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. What is the difference between a valid argument and a true conclusion?
2. What are the five canons of rhetoric, and which one does modern writing neglect?
3. Why did Euclid's method become the model of certain knowledge for later philosophers?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Porphyry — Isagoge** (~3 h)
   - Read: Read it first; it was the standard primer before Aristotle. Learn the five predicables and note the unanswered question about universals.
   - Edition: Tr. Jonathan Barnes, Porphyry: Introduction (Oxford Clarendon Later Ancient Philosophers)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Porphyry+Isagoge) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Porphyry+Isagoge) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Porphyry+Isagoge) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Porphyry+Isagoge) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
2. [ ] **Aristotle — Categories** (~5 h)
   - Read: The ten categories, with close attention to the account of primary and secondary substance in chapter 5.
   - Edition: Tr. J.L. Ackrill, Categories and De Interpretatione (Oxford Clarendon Aristotle Series); also in The Complete Works of Aristotle, ed. Barnes (Princeton)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Categories) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aristotle+Categories) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aristotle+Categories) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Categories) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
3. [ ] **Aristotle — the rest of the Organon (On Interpretation, Prior and Posterior Analytics, Topics, Sophistical Refutations)** (~20 h)
   - Read: On Interpretation 1-9, Prior Analytics I.1-7 (the syllogism), Posterior Analytics I.1-2, and Sophistical Refutations. Skim the Topics.
   - Edition: The Complete Works of Aristotle, ed. Jonathan Barnes, vol. 1 (Princeton); Prior Analytics tr. Robin Smith (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Organon+Owen) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aristotle+Organon) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aristotle+Organon) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Organon) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
4. [ ] **Anon. (Pseudo-Cicero) — Rhetorica ad Herennium** (~8 h)
   - Read: The five canons of rhetoric; Book III on the memory palace is the most cited section.
   - Edition: Tr. Harry Caplan (Loeb Classical Library 403)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Rhetorica+ad+Herennium) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Rhetorica+ad+Herennium) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Rhetorica+ad+Herennium) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
5. [ ] **Cicero — De Oratore; Orator; Topica; Brutus** (~12 h)
   - Read: De Oratore Book I on the education of the orator. Brutus can wait for Phase 3.
   - Edition: Tr. James M. May and Jakob Wisse, Cicero: On the Ideal Orator (Oxford)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+De+Oratore) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cicero+De+Oratore) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cicero+De+Oratore) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+De+Oratore) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
6. [ ] **Euclid — Elements** (~15 h)
   - Read: Book I in full: definitions, postulates, common notions, then work each proposition through to I.47 (the Pythagorean theorem).
   - Edition: Tr. T.L. Heath, The Thirteen Books of the Elements (Dover); Green Lion Press one-volume edition
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Euclid+Elements) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Euclid+Elements) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Euclid+Elements) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Euclid+Elements) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

**Synthesis.** In one page, explain how a medieval student would move from grammar to logic to rhetoric, and what each step equips them to do.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Cicero — On Invention (De Inventione)**: An early, incomplete treatise by Cicero on rhetorical invention, the process of finding arguments for a speech. Written in his youth, it draws on earlier Greek rhetorical theory. It was widely studied as a rhetorical handbook in medieval Europe.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cicero+On+Invention) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cicero+On+Invention) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+On+Invention) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Priscian — Institutes of Grammar (Institutiones Grammaticae)**: A comprehensive Latin grammar composed in the early sixth century AD by the grammarian Priscian of Caesarea. It systematically covers Latin morphology and syntax. It served as a standard grammatical authority throughout the medieval period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Priscian+Institutes+of+Grammar) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Priscian+Institutes+of+Grammar) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Priscian+Institutes+of+Grammar) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Servius the Grammarian — Commentary on Virgil**: A line-by-line commentary on Virgil's works, especially the Aeneid, written by the late fourth-century grammarian Servius. It provides historical, mythological, and linguistic notes and preserves information about Roman antiquity not found elsewhere.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Servius+the+Grammarian+Commentary+on+Virgil) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Servius+the+Grammarian+Commentary+on+Virgil) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Servius+the+Grammarian+Commentary+on+Virgil) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **St. Isidore — Etymologies (Etymologiae)**: A twenty-book encyclopedia compiled by Isidore of Seville in the early seventh century, organizing knowledge by the etymology of words. It covers subjects including grammar, law, medicine, and natural history, drawing on earlier classical sources. It was widely copied and used as a reference work throughout the Middle Ages.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Isidore+Etymologies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Isidore+Etymologies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Isidore+Etymologies) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Alcuin of York — Disputatio with Pippin**: A short Latin dialogue in question-and-answer form, attributed to Alcuin, between a teacher and Pippin, son of Charlemagne. It presents definitions of common concepts through riddles and metaphors, such as defining winter as "the exile of summer." It was used as a pedagogical text in the Carolingian period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Alcuin+of+York+Disputatio+with+Pippin) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Alcuin+of+York+Disputatio+with+Pippin) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Alcuin+of+York+Disputatio+with+Pippin) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Donatus — Ars Minor; Ars Maior**: Two Latin grammar textbooks written by the fourth-century grammarian Aelius Donatus. The Ars Minor covers the parts of speech in question-and-answer form for beginners, while the Ars Maior treats grammar and rhetorical figures in more depth. They were the standard introductory grammars of medieval Europe.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Donatus+Ars+Minor) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Donatus+Ars+Minor) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Donatus+Ars+Minor) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Boethius — On Topical Differences; the logical commentaries**: Sixth-century Latin works by Boethius, including translations of and commentaries on Aristotle's logical treatises and Porphyry's Isagoge. On Topical Differences catalogs types of argument, or topics, used in dialectic and rhetoric. These texts transmitted much of Greek logic to the Latin-speaking West before most of Aristotle's original works were available in Latin.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Boethius+On+Topical+Differences) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Boethius+On+Topical+Differences) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Boethius+On+Topical+Differences) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Quintilian — Institutio Oratoria**: A twelve-book treatise on rhetorical education by the first-century Roman teacher Quintilian. It outlines a curriculum for training an orator from childhood through mature practice, covering grammar, style, delivery, and ethical formation. It was rediscovered in complete form in 1416 and influenced Renaissance humanist education.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Quintilian+Institutio+Oratoria) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Quintilian+Institutio+Oratoria) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Quintilian+Institutio+Oratoria) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Martianus Capella — The Marriage of Philology and Mercury**: A fifth-century Latin allegorical work in prose and verse, in which the marriage of Mercury and the maiden Philology is attended by personifications of the seven liberal arts, each delivering a speech summarizing its content. The work established the sevenfold division of the liberal arts, the trivium and quadrivium, used throughout medieval education.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Martianus+Capella+Marriage+of+Philology+and+Mercury) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Martianus+Capella+Marriage+of+Philology+and+Mercury) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Martianus+Capella+Marriage+of+Philology+and+Mercury) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Longinus — On the Sublime**: A Greek treatise on literary style, of disputed authorship (traditionally attributed to "Longinus"), probably composed in the first century AD. It analyzes the sources of sublimity, or grandeur, in writing, including elevated thought, strong emotion, and figures of speech, illustrated with examples from Homer and other authors.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Longinus+On+the+Sublime) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Longinus+On+the+Sublime) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Longinus+On+the+Sublime) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Dionysius Thrax — The Art of Grammar**: A short Greek grammatical treatise attributed to Dionysius Thrax, active in the second century BC, considered the earliest systematic grammar of Greek. It defines the parts of speech and basic grammatical categories and influenced later Greek and Latin grammatical writing.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Dionysius+Thrax+Art+of+Grammar) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Dionysius+Thrax+Art+of+Grammar) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Dionysius+Thrax+Art+of+Grammar) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Isocrates — Against the Sophists; Antidosis**: Prose works by the Athenian rhetorician Isocrates from the fourth century BC. Against the Sophists criticizes the teaching methods of rival rhetoric teachers and outlines his own approach; the Antidosis defends his career and educational philosophy, which combined rhetorical training with civic and ethical formation.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Isocrates+Against+the+Sophists) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Isocrates+Against+the+Sophists) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Isocrates+Against+the+Sophists) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Hermogenes of Tarsus — On Types of Style**: A Greek rhetorical treatise, probably by the second-century rhetorician Hermogenes of Tarsus, analyzing qualities of prose style such as clarity, grandeur, and force, and how they are achieved through diction, word order, and figures. It became a standard textbook in Byzantine rhetorical education.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hermogenes+of+Tarsus+On+Types+of+Style) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hermogenes+of+Tarsus+On+Types+of+Style) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hermogenes+of+Tarsus+On+Types+of+Style) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Bede — On the Art of Metre and On Figures of Speech**: Two short pedagogical works by the eighth-century Northumbrian monk Bede, treating Latin poetic meter and the rhetorical figures found in the Bible and classical texts. They were composed for use in monastic schools in Anglo-Saxon England.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bede+On+the+Art+of+Metre+and+On+Figures+of+Speech) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bede+On+the+Art+of+Metre+and+On+Figures+of+Speech) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bede+On+the+Art+of+Metre+and+On+Figures+of+Speech) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **St. Boethius — On Arithmetic; On Music**: Latin treatises by the sixth-century philosopher Boethius, adapting earlier Greek works, including that of Nicomachus of Gerasa, on number theory and musical theory. On Music discusses the mathematical ratios underlying musical intervals and distinguishes several categories of music, including music of the celestial spheres. The works served as standard textbooks for the medieval quadrivium.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Boethius+On+Arithmetic) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Boethius+On+Arithmetic) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Boethius+On+Arithmetic) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **St. Bede — On the Reckoning of Time (De Temporum Ratione)**: An eighth-century treatise by the Northumbrian monk Bede on computus, the calculation of the date of Easter, and chronology, including the dating of years from the birth of Christ. It also discusses the physical nature of time and the structure of the calendar.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bede+On+the+Reckoning+of+Time) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bede+On+the+Reckoning+of+Time) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bede+On+the+Reckoning+of+Time) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Pliny the Elder — Natural History**: A thirty-seven-book encyclopedia compiled by the Roman author Pliny the Elder in the first century AD, covering subjects including astronomy, geography, zoology, botany, and mineralogy. Pliny died in 79 AD during the eruption of Mount Vesuvius. The work was widely copied and consulted throughout the medieval period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pliny+the+Elder+Natural+History) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pliny+the+Elder+Natural+History) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pliny+the+Elder+Natural+History) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Alcuin of York — Problems to Sharpen the Young (Propositiones ad Acuendos Juvenes)**: A collection of mathematical puzzles attributed to Alcuin of York, an eighth-century scholar at the court of Charlemagne. It includes problems such as river-crossing puzzles and questions about measuring quantities, and is considered among the earliest recreational mathematics texts in the Latin tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Alcuin+of+York+Problems+to+Sharpen+the+Young) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Alcuin+of+York+Problems+to+Sharpen+the+Young) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Alcuin+of+York+Problems+to+Sharpen+the+Young) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Nicomachus of Gerasa — Introduction to Arithmetic**: A Greek treatise on number theory by Nicomachus, active around 100 AD, covering properties of numbers such as odd, even, prime, and figurate numbers within a Pythagorean framework. It was translated into Latin by Boethius and became a standard source for arithmetic in medieval education.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Nicomachus+of+Gerasa+Introduction+to+Arithmetic) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Nicomachus+of+Gerasa+Introduction+to+Arithmetic) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Nicomachus+of+Gerasa+Introduction+to+Arithmetic) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Ptolemy — Almagest; Harmonics**: The Almagest, composed by Claudius Ptolemy in the second century AD, presents a mathematical model of the geocentric universe and remained the standard astronomical reference until the Copernican revolution. His Harmonics addresses the mathematics of musical scales and intervals.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ptolemy+Almagest) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ptolemy+Almagest) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ptolemy+Almagest) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Macrobius — Commentary on the Dream of Scipio**: A Latin commentary written in the early fifth century by Macrobius on the concluding section of Cicero's De Republica. It expands on themes including the harmony of the celestial spheres, the nature of the soul, and cosmology, and was a major channel through which Neoplatonic ideas reached the medieval West.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Macrobius+Commentary+on+the+Dream+of+Scipio) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Macrobius+Commentary+on+the+Dream+of+Scipio) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Macrobius+Commentary+on+the+Dream+of+Scipio) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Aratus — Phaenomena**: A Greek didactic poem composed in the third century BC by Aratus, describing the constellations and weather signs in verse. It was translated into Latin several times, including by Cicero, and a line from it is quoted in the Acts of the Apostles.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aratus+Phaenomena) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aratus+Phaenomena) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aratus+Phaenomena) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Hyginus — Poeticon Astronomicon**: A Latin work traditionally attributed to Hyginus, cataloging the mythological stories associated with each constellation. It served as a reference for classical star-myths in later periods.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hyginus+Poeticon+Astronomicon) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hyginus+Poeticon+Astronomicon) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hyginus+Poeticon+Astronomicon) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Bede — On the Nature of Things (De Natura Rerum)**: A short treatise by Bede describing the physical world, including the elements, weather phenomena, and the heavens, drawing on earlier sources such as Pliny and Isidore of Seville. It complements his later work on time-reckoning, De Temporum Ratione.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bede+On+the+Nature+of+Things) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bede+On+the+Nature+of+Things) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bede+On+the+Nature+of+Things) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Archimedes — The Works**: The surviving treatises of the Greek mathematician Archimedes, active in the third century BC, covering topics including the law of buoyancy, the measurement of the circle, the areas and volumes of geometric solids, and, in the Sand-Reckoner, a system for expressing very large numbers. His method of exhaustion anticipated later techniques of integral calculus.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Archimedes+Works) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Archimedes+Works) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Archimedes+Works) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Vitruvius — On Architecture**: A Latin treatise on architecture and engineering by the Roman architect Vitruvius, written in the first century BC. It is the only complete work on architecture to survive from antiquity, covering building materials, construction methods, proportion, and machines, and includes a description of human bodily proportion later illustrated as "Vitruvian Man."
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Vitruvius+On+Architecture) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Vitruvius+On+Architecture) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Vitruvius+On+Architecture) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Apollonius of Perga — Conics**: A Greek mathematical treatise by Apollonius of Perga, active in the third century BC, on conic sections, defining and naming the ellipse, parabola, and hyperbola. Its results were later applied by Johannes Kepler in describing planetary orbits.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Apollonius+of+Perga+Conics) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Apollonius+of+Perga+Conics) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Apollonius+of+Perga+Conics) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Diophantus — Arithmetica**: A Greek mathematical text by Diophantus of Alexandria, probably composed in the third century AD, addressing algebraic equations, particularly those seeking integer or rational solutions. A marginal note by Pierre de Fermat on a copy of this work states what became known as Fermat's Last Theorem.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Diophantus+Arithmetica) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Diophantus+Arithmetica) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Diophantus+Arithmetica) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Aristoxenus — Elements of Harmonics**: A Greek treatise on music theory by Aristoxenus, a student of Aristotle active in the fourth century BC. It analyzes musical scales and intervals based on the perception of trained hearing, in contrast to the purely numerical approach of earlier Pythagorean music theory.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aristoxenus+Elements+of+Harmonics) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aristoxenus+Elements+of+Harmonics) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristoxenus+Elements+of+Harmonics) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Sacrobosco — On the Sphere (De Sphaera)**: A thirteenth-century Latin astronomical textbook by Johannes de Sacrobosco, describing the spherical earth, the celestial spheres, and basic astronomical phenomena. It was a standard university text on astronomy in Europe for several centuries.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sacrobosco+On+the+Sphere) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sacrobosco+On+the+Sphere) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sacrobosco+On+the+Sphere) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

<a id="phase-ii"></a>

## Phase II: The Greek World

*8th to 4th c. BC. About 225 hours of core reading.*

Homer is the shared reference point for everything that follows in the Western sections. Read the poets, then the historians, then the philosophers, in roughly the order they wrote. Plato and Aristotle answer questions the tragedians and historians raised.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. What does Achilles want, and does he get it?
2. How do the tragedians treat the conflict between family obligation and the law of the city?
3. Thucydides and Plato both lived through the Peloponnesian War. What did each conclude from it?
4. How does Aristotle's account of the good life differ from Plato's?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Homer — The Iliad; The Odyssey** (~40 h)
   - Read: Iliad first. Mark Book 9 (the embassy), Book 18 (the shield), and Book 24 (Priam and Achilles). Then the Odyssey, tracking hospitality and homecoming.
   - Edition: Iliad tr. Richmond Lattimore (Chicago) or Robert Fagles (Penguin); Odyssey tr. Lattimore (Harper) or Emily Wilson (Norton)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Homer+Iliad+Butler) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Homer+Iliad) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Homer+Iliad) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Homer+Iliad) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
2. [ ] **Hesiod — Theogony; Works and Days** (~4 h)
   - Read: Theogony for the genealogy of the gods; Works and Days for the ethic of labor and justice.
   - Edition: Tr. M.L. West, Theogony and Works and Days (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hesiod+Evelyn-White) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hesiod+Theogony) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hesiod+Theogony) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hesiod+Theogony) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
3. [ ] **Aeschylus — The Oresteia; Prometheus Bound** (~8 h)
   - Read: Read the Oresteia as one trilogy. The Eumenides ends a cycle of vengeance with a court at Athens.
   - Edition: Tr. Richmond Lattimore, in The Complete Greek Tragedies (Chicago); or tr. Robert Fagles (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aeschylus+Agamemnon) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aeschylus+Oresteia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aeschylus+Oresteia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aeschylus+Oresteia) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
4. [ ] **Sophocles — The Theban Plays; Ajax** (~8 h)
   - Read: Oedipus the King, Oedipus at Colonus, Antigone. Antigone sets divine law against the decree of the city.
   - Edition: Tr. Robert Fagles, The Three Theban Plays (Penguin); or David Grene, Sophocles I (Chicago)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sophocles+Oedipus) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sophocles+Theban+Plays) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sophocles+Theban+Plays) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sophocles+Theban+Plays) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
5. [ ] **Euripides — The Tragedies** (~10 h)
   - Read: Medea, Hippolytus, The Trojan Women, The Bacchae.
   - Edition: The Complete Greek Tragedies: Euripides, ed. Grene and Lattimore (Chicago)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Euripides+Murray) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Euripides+Tragedies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Euripides+Tragedies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Euripides+Tragedies) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
6. [ ] **Aristophanes — The Comedies** (~5 h)
   - Read: The Clouds first; its caricature of Socrates is the counterpart to Plato's portrait. Then The Frogs and Lysistrata.
   - Edition: Tr. Peter Meineck, Clouds (Hackett); or Alan Sommerstein, Lysistrata and Other Plays (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristophanes+Clouds) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aristophanes+Comedies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aristophanes+Comedies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristophanes+Comedies) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
7. [ ] **Herodotus — The Histories** (~30 h)
   - Read: Books I-II for method and digression, VII-IX for the Persian invasion.
   - Edition: The Landmark Herodotus, ed. Robert Strassler, tr. Andrea Purvis (Anchor)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Herodotus+Macaulay) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Herodotus+Histories) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Herodotus+Histories) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Herodotus+Histories) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
8. [ ] **Thucydides — The Peloponnesian War** (~30 h)
   - Read: Pericles' Funeral Oration (II.35-46), the plague (II.47-54), the Mytilenean debate (III.36-50), the Melian dialogue (V.84-116), the Sicilian expedition (VI-VII).
   - Edition: The Landmark Thucydides, ed. Robert Strassler, tr. Richard Crawley (Free Press)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Thucydides+Crawley) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Thucydides+Peloponnesian+War) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Thucydides+Peloponnesian+War) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Thucydides+Peloponnesian+War) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
9. [ ] **The Pre-Socratics (the fragments)** (~5 h)
   - Read: Heraclitus and Parmenides above all; the problem of change and permanence sets up Plato.
   - Edition: Ed. and tr. Jonathan Barnes, Early Greek Philosophy (Penguin); or Kirk, Raven and Schofield, The Presocratic Philosophers (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Burnet+Early+Greek+Philosophy) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pre-Socratics) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pre-Socratics) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pre-Socratics) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
10. [ ] **Plato — Republic; Symposium; Phaedo; Phaedrus; Timaeus; Gorgias; Laws** (~45 h)
   - Read: Suggested order: Gorgias, Phaedo, Symposium, Republic, Phaedrus, Timaeus. Leave the Laws for a later pass. The Apology and Crito, though not listed, are a short useful preface.
   - Edition: Plato: Complete Works, ed. John M. Cooper (Hackett). Republic tr. Allan Bloom (Basic Books) or Grube/Reeve (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plato+Jowett) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Plato+Republic) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Plato+Republic) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plato+Republic) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
11. [ ] **Aristotle — Nicomachean Ethics; Politics; Physics; Metaphysics; On the Soul; Poetics; Rhetoric** (~40 h)
   - Read: Nicomachean Ethics in full; then Politics I-III, Poetics, On the Soul II-III, Physics II, Metaphysics I and XII.
   - Edition: Nicomachean Ethics tr. Terence Irwin (Hackett) or Bartlett and Collins (Chicago); Politics tr. C.D.C. Reeve (Hackett); Poetics tr. Joe Sachs (Focus)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Ethics) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aristotle+Nicomachean+Ethics) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aristotle+Nicomachean+Ethics) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Nicomachean+Ethics) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

**Synthesis.** Trace one idea (justice, fate, or the good life) from Homer through the tragedians to Plato and Aristotle.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **The Homeric Hymns**: A collection of anonymous Greek hymns in epic style, addressed to various gods, composed between roughly the seventh and fourth centuries BC. They include narrative episodes such as the abduction of Persephone in the Hymn to Demeter and the infancy of Hermes.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Homeric+Hymns) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Homeric+Hymns) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Homeric+Hymns) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Apollonius of Rhodes — Argonautica**: A Hellenistic Greek epic poem by Apollonius of Rhodes, composed in the third century BC, recounting Jason's quest for the Golden Fleece with the Argonauts and his relationship with the sorceress Medea. It is noted for its detailed portrayal of Medea's internal conflict and later influenced Roman epic, including Virgil's Aeneid.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Apollonius+of+Rhodes+Argonautica) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Apollonius+of+Rhodes+Argonautica) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Apollonius+of+Rhodes+Argonautica) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Pindar — Odes**: Choral odes composed by the Greek poet Pindar in the fifth century BC, celebrating victors in athletic competitions including the Olympic and Pythian games. They combine praise of the victor with mythological narrative and reflections on human achievement and mortality.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pindar+Odes) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pindar+Odes) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pindar+Odes) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Sappho — Fragments**: Surviving fragments of lyric poetry by the Greek poet Sappho, active on the island of Lesbos around 600 BC. Her poems, largely preserved incompletely through quotation and papyrus finds, deal chiefly with themes of love and desire, often directed toward women.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sappho+Fragments) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sappho+Fragments) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sappho+Fragments) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Aesop — The Fables**: A collection of short prose fables traditionally attributed to Aesop, said to have lived in Greece in the sixth century BC, though the surviving collections were compiled and expanded by later authors. Each fable typically features animal characters and concludes with a moral lesson.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aesop+Fables) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aesop+Fables) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aesop+Fables) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Theocritus — Idylls**: A collection of Greek poems by Theocritus, active in the third century BC, largely set in rural Sicily and featuring shepherds and herdsmen. The Idylls are considered the founding works of the pastoral poetry genre, later imitated by Virgil and other poets.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Theocritus+Idylls) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Theocritus+Idylls) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Theocritus+Idylls) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Callimachus — Hymns; Aetia**: Works by the Alexandrian poet and scholar Callimachus, third century BC. The Hymns are poems addressed to various gods in the epic tradition; the Aetia is a fragmentary elegiac poem exploring the origins of various customs, rituals, and place names. Callimachus favored short, highly polished poetic forms over long epic.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Callimachus+Hymns) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Callimachus+Hymns) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Callimachus+Hymns) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Menander — The Grouch; the comedies**: Plays by the Athenian playwright Menander, fourth-third centuries BC, the principal representative of Greek New Comedy, of which The Grouch (Dyskolos) survives complete. His comedies center on domestic situations, romantic misunderstandings, and stock character types, and influenced Roman comic playwrights such as Plautus and Terence.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Menander+Grouch) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Menander+Grouch) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Menander+Grouch) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Greek Anthology**: A large collection of Greek epigrams and short poems by numerous authors, compiled and expanded over roughly a thousand years, from the classical period through the Byzantine era. Its poems address subjects including love, death, art, and everyday life.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Greek+Anthology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Greek+Anthology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Greek+Anthology) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Sextus Empiricus — Outlines of Pyrrhonism**: A 2nd-century AD survey of Pyrrhonist skepticism, outlining methods for suspending judgment on matters that cannot be conclusively determined. The text presents opposing arguments on a range of philosophical and scientific questions to demonstrate the case for withholding assent. It is a primary source for ancient skeptical philosophy and was studied during the European Renaissance.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sextus+Empiricus+Outlines+of+Pyrrhonism) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sextus+Empiricus+Outlines+of+Pyrrhonism) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sextus+Empiricus+Outlines+of+Pyrrhonism) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Diogenes Laertius — Lives of the Eminent Philosophers**: A 3rd-century AD compilation of biographical information, sayings, and doctrinal summaries of Greek philosophers from Thales to the Hellenistic schools. It draws on earlier sources, many no longer extant. It remains a primary source of information for several ancient philosophical schools.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Diogenes+Laertius+Lives+of+the+Eminent+Philosophers) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Diogenes+Laertius+Lives+of+the+Eminent+Philosophers) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Diogenes+Laertius+Lives+of+the+Eminent+Philosophers) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Xenophon — Memorabilia; Cyropaedia**: Works by a student of Socrates who was also a soldier and historian of the 4th century BC. The Memorabilia is a defense and recollection of Socrates' teaching and character, presenting a more practically oriented portrait than Plato's dialogues. The Cyropaedia is a partly fictionalized account of the education and rule of Cyrus the Great, addressing leadership and governance.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Xenophon+Memorabilia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Xenophon+Memorabilia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Xenophon+Memorabilia) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Epicurus — Letters and Principal Doctrines**: Surviving letters and maxims of the philosopher (341-270 BC), founder of Epicureanism. The teachings hold that the goal of life is tranquility (ataraxia), achieved through the moderate pursuit of pleasure and the removal of fear, particularly fear of death and the gods. Much of Epicurus's original output is lost, and these fragments are preserved largely through later authors such as Diogenes Laertius.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Epicurus+Letters+and+Principal+Doctrines) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Epicurus+Letters+and+Principal+Doctrines) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Epicurus+Letters+and+Principal+Doctrines) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Theophrastus — Characters; the botanical works**: Writings by Aristotle's successor as head of the Lyceum (c. 371-287 BC). The Characters is a short collection of sketches describing thirty personality types, such as the flatterer and the boastful man. His botanical treatises, Enquiry into Plants and On the Causes of Plants, are among the earliest systematic works on plant science.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Theophrastus+Characters) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Theophrastus+Characters) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Theophrastus+Characters) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Philo of Alexandria — On the Creation; Allegorical Interpretation**: Writings of a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher (c. 20 BC-50 AD) who interpreted the Hebrew Bible using categories drawn from Greek philosophy, particularly Platonism. His allegorical method of biblical interpretation influenced later Christian exegetes, including Origen and Clement of Alexandria.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Philo+of+Alexandria+On+the+Creation) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Philo+of+Alexandria+On+the+Creation) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Philo+of+Alexandria+On+the+Creation) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

<a id="phase-iii"></a>

## Phase III: Rome

*3rd c. BC to 2nd c. AD. About 195 hours of core reading.*

Rome absorbed Greek learning and turned it toward law, duty, and empire. Read the historians of the Republic, then Cicero and the Augustan poets, then the imperial historians and the Stoics. Virgil and Cicero were the two Latin authors every later European student knew.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. What does Roman virtus mean, and how does it differ from Greek arete?
2. Is Aeneas admirable? What does Virgil suggest Rome cost?
3. How do Seneca, Epictetus, and Marcus Aurelius each practice Stoicism differently?
4. What does Tacitus think happened to Roman liberty?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Polybius — The Histories** (~10 h)
   - Read: Book I for the method, Book VI for the mixed constitution that later shaped Montesquieu and the American founders.
   - Edition: Tr. Robin Waterfield, The Histories (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Polybius+Shuckburgh) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Polybius+Histories) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Polybius+Histories) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Polybius+Histories) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
2. [ ] **Livy — The History of Rome (Ab Urbe Condita)** (~25 h)
   - Read: Books I-V (founding to the Gallic sack) and XXI-XXX (the war with Hannibal).
   - Edition: Tr. Aubrey de Selincourt, The Early History of Rome and The War with Hannibal (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Livy+History+of+Rome) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Livy+History+of+Rome) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Livy+History+of+Rome) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Livy+History+of+Rome) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
3. [ ] **Plutarch — Parallel Lives; Moralia** (~25 h)
   - Read: Paired Lives: Theseus/Romulus, Lycurgus/Numa, Alexander/Caesar, Demosthenes/Cicero. Dip into the Moralia.
   - Edition: Tr. Robin Waterfield, Roman Lives and Greek Lives (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plutarch+Lives+Dryden) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Plutarch+Parallel+Lives) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Plutarch+Parallel+Lives) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plutarch+Parallel+Lives) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
4. [ ] **Lucretius — On the Nature of Things (De Rerum Natura)** (~10 h)
   - Read: The Epicurean alternative: atoms, void, and no fear of death (Book III). Rediscovered in 1417.
   - Edition: Tr. Martin Ferguson Smith (Hackett); or A.E. Stallings (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lucretius+Nature+of+Things) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lucretius+On+the+Nature+of+Things) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lucretius+On+the+Nature+of+Things) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lucretius+On+the+Nature+of+Things) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
5. [ ] **Cicero — On Duties; Tusculan Disputations; On the Nature of the Gods; On Old Age; On Friendship; Letters** (~20 h)
   - Read: On Duties first, then On Friendship and On Old Age; Tusculan Disputations I and V; a selection of the Letters.
   - Edition: On Duties tr. Griffin and Atkins (Cambridge Texts in the History of Political Thought); On Friendship and On Old Age in Cicero: On the Good Life, tr. Michael Grant (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+Offices) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cicero+On+Duties) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cicero+On+Duties) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+On+Duties) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
6. [ ] **Cicero — The Dream of Scipio (Somnium Scipionis)** (~1 h)
   - Read: The closing myth of Cicero's On the Republic. It links Roman public service to Platonic cosmology.
   - Edition: In Cicero: The Republic and The Laws, tr. Niall Rudd (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Dream+of+Scipio) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cicero+Dream+of+Scipio) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cicero+Dream+of+Scipio) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+Dream+of+Scipio) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
7. [ ] **Virgil — The Aeneid** (~20 h)
   - Read: Books 1-6 (Troy to the underworld), then 7-12 (the war in Italy). Compare Book 6 with Odyssey 11.
   - Edition: Tr. Robert Fitzgerald (Vintage) or Robert Fagles (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Virgil+Aeneid+Dryden) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Virgil+Aeneid) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Virgil+Aeneid) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Virgil+Aeneid) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
8. [ ] **Horace — Odes; Satires; Epistles; Ars Poetica** (~8 h)
   - Read: Odes I-III, a few Epistles, and the Ars Poetica.
   - Edition: Tr. David West, The Complete Odes and Epodes (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Horace+Odes) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Horace+Odes) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Horace+Odes) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Horace+Odes) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
9. [ ] **Ovid — Metamorphoses; Fasti; Heroides** (~15 h)
   - Read: The Metamorphoses in full; much later poetry and painting depend on it.
   - Edition: Tr. Allen Mandelbaum (Harcourt) or Charles Martin (Norton)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ovid+Metamorphoses) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ovid+Metamorphoses) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ovid+Metamorphoses) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ovid+Metamorphoses) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
10. [ ] **Seneca — Letters to Lucilius; On the Shortness of Life; Moral Essays** (~12 h)
   - Read: Letters 1-30 and On the Shortness of Life.
   - Edition: Tr. Robin Campbell, Letters from a Stoic (Penguin); On the Shortness of Life tr. C.D.N. Costa (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Seneca+Morals) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Seneca+Letters+to+Lucilius) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Seneca+Letters+to+Lucilius) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Seneca+Letters+to+Lucilius) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
11. [ ] **Tacitus — Annals; Histories; Germania; Agricola** (~20 h)
   - Read: Agricola and Germania (both short), then Annals I-VI on Tiberius.
   - Edition: Agricola and Germania tr. Harold Mattingly (Penguin); Annals tr. A.J. Woodman (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tacitus+Agricola) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tacitus+Annals) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tacitus+Annals) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tacitus+Annals) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
12. [ ] **Suetonius — The Twelve Caesars** (~8 h)
   - Read: Julius, Augustus, Tiberius, Nero. Compare his method with Tacitus.
   - Edition: Tr. Robert Graves, rev. J.B. Rives (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Suetonius+Twelve+Caesars) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Suetonius+Twelve+Caesars) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Suetonius+Twelve+Caesars) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Suetonius+Twelve+Caesars) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
13. [ ] **Epictetus — Enchiridion; Discourses** (~8 h)
   - Read: The Enchiridion, then Discourses Book I. Chapter 1 of the Enchiridion is the core distinction.
   - Edition: Tr. Robin Hard, Discourses, Fragments, Handbook (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Epictetus+Discourses) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Epictetus+Enchiridion) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Epictetus+Enchiridion) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Epictetus+Enchiridion) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
14. [ ] **Marcus Aurelius — Meditations** (~5 h)
   - Read: Read slowly, one book per sitting. Book 1 lists what he learned from each teacher.
   - Edition: Tr. Gregory Hays (Modern Library) or Robin Hard (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marcus+Aurelius+Meditations) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Marcus+Aurelius+Meditations) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Marcus+Aurelius+Meditations) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marcus+Aurelius+Meditations) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
15. [ ] **Apuleius — The Golden Ass** (~8 h)
   - Read: The only Latin novel to survive complete. Book 11 on the Isis mysteries anticipates Phase 4's conversion narratives.
   - Edition: Tr. P.G. Walsh (Oxford World's Classics) or Sarah Ruden (Yale)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Apuleius+Golden+Ass) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Apuleius+Golden+Ass) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Apuleius+Golden+Ass) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Apuleius+Golden+Ass) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

**Synthesis.** Compare the Roman idea of duty in Cicero and Virgil with the Stoic idea of inner freedom in Epictetus and Marcus Aurelius.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Lucan — Pharsalia (De Bello Civili)**: An unfinished Latin epic poem by Lucan (39-65 AD) on the civil war between Julius Caesar and Pompey. Unlike traditional epic, it largely omits divine intervention, focusing instead on political power, fortune, and the collapse of the Roman Republic.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lucan+Pharsalia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lucan+Pharsalia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lucan+Pharsalia) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Statius — Thebaid**: A Latin epic poem by Statius, composed in the first century AD, recounting the mythical war between the sons of Oedipus, Eteocles and Polynices, for control of Thebes. It was widely read in the medieval period, and Dante includes Statius as a character and guide in the Purgatorio.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Statius+Thebaid) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Statius+Thebaid) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Statius+Thebaid) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **St. Venantius Fortunatus — Pange Lingua Gloriosi Proelium Certaminis**: A Latin hymn composed by Venantius Fortunatus in the sixth century, celebrating the cross of the crucifixion. Written in classical meter, it was adopted into the liturgy of the Western Church, particularly for Good Friday and Holy Week services.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Venantius+Fortunatus+Pange+Lingua+Gloriosi+Proelium+Certaminis) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Venantius+Fortunatus+Pange+Lingua+Gloriosi+Proelium+Certaminis) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Venantius+Fortunatus+Pange+Lingua+Gloriosi+Proelium+Certaminis) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Virgil — Eclogues; Georgics**: Earlier works by Virgil preceding the Aeneid. The Eclogues (42-39 BC) are pastoral poems depicting shepherds in idealized rural settings; the Georgics (29 BC) is a didactic poem on agriculture, covering farming, viticulture, animal husbandry, and beekeeping. The Fourth Eclogue's prophecy of a child ushering in a new age was later interpreted by some Christian readers as a prefiguration of Christ.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Virgil+Eclogues) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Virgil+Eclogues) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Virgil+Eclogues) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Catullus — Poems**: A collection of Latin poems by Catullus, mid-first century BC, including short lyric and invective verses as well as longer poems in various meters. Many poems address a woman referred to as "Lesbia," generally identified with Clodia Metelli, and treat themes of love, friendship, and personal rivalry.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Catullus+Poems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Catullus+Poems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Catullus+Poems) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Statius — Silvae; Achilleid**: Additional works by Statius beyond the Thebaid. The Silvae is a collection of occasional poems on subjects including patronage, domestic life, and personal loss; the Achilleid is an unfinished epic on the early life of Achilles, including his time disguised among the daughters of King Lycomedes.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Statius+Silvae) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Statius+Silvae) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Statius+Silvae) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Silius Italicus — Punica**: A Latin epic poem by Silius Italicus, first century AD, the longest surviving poem in Latin, recounting the Second Punic War between Rome and Carthage, including the campaigns of Hannibal.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Silius+Italicus+Punica) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Silius+Italicus+Punica) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Silius+Italicus+Punica) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Valerius Flaccus — Argonautica**: An unfinished Latin epic poem by Valerius Flaccus, first century AD, retelling the story of Jason and the Argonauts' quest for the Golden Fleece. It is modeled on the earlier Greek Argonautica of Apollonius of Rhodes but places greater emphasis on the psychological development of Medea.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Valerius+Flaccus+Argonautica) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Valerius+Flaccus+Argonautica) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Valerius+Flaccus+Argonautica) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Juvenal — Satires**: Sixteen satirical poems by the Roman poet Juvenal, late first to early second century AD, addressing the vices, hypocrisies, and social conditions of imperial Rome. They include the phrases "panem et circenses" (bread and circuses) and "quis custodiet ipsos custodes" (who will guard the guards themselves).
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Juvenal+Satires) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Juvenal+Satires) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Juvenal+Satires) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Persius — Satires**: Six satirical poems by Persius (34-62 AD), a Stoic writer whose dense, allusive Latin addresses themes of moral hypocrisy and self-examination in light of Stoic philosophy.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Persius+Satires) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Persius+Satires) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Persius+Satires) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Martial — Epigrams**: A collection of short Latin epigrams by Martial, first century AD, numbering around 1,500, covering subjects from social satire and personal invective to occasional and commemorative verse, and documenting aspects of daily life in Rome.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Martial+Epigrams) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Martial+Epigrams) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Martial+Epigrams) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Claudian — Poems**: Latin poems by Claudian, late fourth to early fifth century AD, a court poet who wrote panegyrics for the Western Roman emperor Honorius and the general Stilicho, and the unfinished mythological epic The Rape of Proserpina. He is generally regarded as among the last poets writing within the classical Latin epic tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Claudian+Poems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Claudian+Poems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Claudian+Poems) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Prudentius — Psychomachia**: A Latin allegorical poem by the Christian poet Prudentius, late fourth century AD, depicting a battle between personified virtues and vices for possession of the human soul. It is considered an early and influential example of the allegorical genre in Western literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Prudentius+Psychomachia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Prudentius+Psychomachia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Prudentius+Psychomachia) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Sedulius — Carmen Paschale**: A Latin epic poem by Sedulius, fifth century AD, retelling episodes from the Gospels, particularly the miracles of Christ, in the style and meter of classical Latin epic. It was used as an educational text in medieval schools.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sedulius+Carmen+Paschale) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sedulius+Carmen+Paschale) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sedulius+Carmen+Paschale) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Juvencus — Evangeliorum Libri**: A Latin epic poem by Juvencus, composed in the early fourth century AD, versifying the life of Christ, based primarily on the Gospel of Matthew, in Virgilian hexameters. It is considered the earliest Christian Latin epic.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Juvencus+Evangeliorum+Libri) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Juvencus+Evangeliorum+Libri) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Juvencus+Evangeliorum+Libri) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Plautus & Terence — The Comedies**: Roman comic playwrights of the third and second centuries BC, respectively, both adapting plots from Greek New Comedy. Plautus's plays are marked by broad farce, wordplay, and musical elements, while Terence's are noted for more restrained language and psychological characterization. Their surviving plays center on themes including mistaken identity and clever slaves.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Plautus+%26+Terence+Comedies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Plautus+%26+Terence+Comedies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plautus+%26+Terence+Comedies) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Seneca — The Tragedies**: Latin tragedies attributed to the Stoic philosopher and statesman Seneca the Younger, first century AD, including Medea, Phaedra, and Thyestes, dealing with themes of revenge, tyranny, and extreme passion. They influenced the development of Renaissance tragedy, including English drama.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Seneca+Tragedies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Seneca+Tragedies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Seneca+Tragedies) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Ennius — Annales (fragments)**: A fragmentary Latin epic poem by Quintus Ennius (239-169 BC), recounting Roman history from the fall of Troy to Ennius's own time in dactylic hexameter, a meter he introduced to Latin poetry. Only fragments survive, quoted by later authors.
