  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ennius+Annales) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ennius+Annales) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ennius+Annales) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Tibullus & Propertius — The Elegies**: Roman love elegists of the late first century BC. Propertius's elegies, addressed largely to a woman he calls Cynthia, are noted for their mythological allusion and intensity; Tibullus's poems combine themes of love with idealized depictions of rural life.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tibullus+%26+Propertius+Elegies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tibullus+%26+Propertius+Elegies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tibullus+%26+Propertius+Elegies) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Manilius — Astronomica**: A Latin didactic poem by Manilius, early first century AD, on astronomy and astrology, describing the constellations, the zodiac, and a Stoic-influenced view of a cosmos governed by fate.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Manilius+Astronomica) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Manilius+Astronomica) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Manilius+Astronomica) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Ausonius — Mosella; Poems**: Latin poems by Ausonius, fourth century AD, a Gallo-Roman poet and teacher. The Mosella describes the Moselle River and its surrounding landscape; his other poems address teachers, family members, and daily life. He wrote near the end of the classical Latin literary tradition, in the late Roman period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ausonius+Mosella) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ausonius+Mosella) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ausonius+Mosella) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Sallust — The Catiline War; The Jugurthine War**: Two Latin historical monographs by Gaius Sallustius Crispus, written in the mid-1st century BC. The Catiline War covers the conspiracy of Catiline in 63 BC; the Jugurthine War covers Rome's war against the Numidian king Jugurtha. Both combine narrative with moralizing analysis attributing Rome's political troubles to a decline in traditional discipline.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sallust+Catiline+War) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sallust+Catiline+War) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sallust+Catiline+War) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Julius Caesar — The Gallic War; The Civil War**: First-person military narratives authored by Julius Caesar, covering his campaigns in Gaul from 58 to 50 BC and the civil war against Pompey in 49-48 BC. Written in the third person, they combine campaign narrative with justification of Caesar's political and military conduct. They are read both as historical sources and as models of Latin prose style.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Julius+Caesar+Gallic+War) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Julius+Caesar+Gallic+War) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Julius+Caesar+Gallic+War) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Josephus — The Jewish War; Antiquities of the Jews**: Works by Flavius Josephus, a Jewish military commander who defected to Rome during the First Jewish-Roman War. The Jewish War recounts the revolt of 66-73 AD and the destruction of the Second Temple; Antiquities of the Jews is a broader history of the Jewish people from creation to Josephus's own time. His writings are a major source for first-century Judea, including references to early Christianity.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Josephus+Jewish+War) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Josephus+Jewish+War) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Josephus+Jewish+War) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Ammianus Marcellinus — The Later Roman Empire**: A Latin history, the Res Gestae, composed in the late 4th century AD by Ammianus Marcellinus, a former soldier, originally covering Roman history from 96 to 378 AD, of which the surviving portion covers 353-378. It includes an account of the reign of the emperor Julian, whom Ammianus had served. It is a principal narrative source for the later Roman Empire.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ammianus+Marcellinus+Later+Roman+Empire) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ammianus+Marcellinus+Later+Roman+Empire) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ammianus+Marcellinus+Later+Roman+Empire) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Arrian — The Anabasis of Alexander**: A Greek history of Alexander the Great's campaigns, written in the 2nd century AD by Arrian, a Roman provincial governor, based largely on the now-lost accounts of Alexander's officers Ptolemy and Aristobulus. It covers Alexander's campaigns from his accession through his death in 323 BC. It is generally regarded by historians as among the more reliable surviving accounts of Alexander's career.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Arrian+Anabasis+of+Alexander) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Arrian+Anabasis+of+Alexander) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Arrian+Anabasis+of+Alexander) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Quintus Curtius Rufus — History of Alexander**: A Latin history of Alexander the Great, probably written in the 1st century AD, portions of which are lost. It presents a more rhetorical and moralizing account than Arrian's, with attention to the effects of success and power on Alexander's character. Its precise date and the identity of its author remain uncertain.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Quintus+Curtius+Rufus+History+of+Alexander) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Quintus+Curtius+Rufus+History+of+Alexander) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Quintus+Curtius+Rufus+History+of+Alexander) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Diodorus Siculus — Bibliotheca Historica**: A universal history in Greek by Diodorus Siculus, written in the 1st century BC, covering mythological and historical events from the earliest times to Diodorus's own era across forty books, of which roughly a third survives intact. It draws on numerous earlier historians, some of whose works are otherwise lost. It is a significant secondary source for ancient historiography.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Diodorus+Siculus+Bibliotheca+Historica) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Diodorus+Siculus+Bibliotheca+Historica) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Diodorus+Siculus+Bibliotheca+Historica) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Strabo — Geography**: A seventeen-book Greek geographical work by Strabo, completed in the early 1st century AD, describing the physical geography, peoples, and customs of regions known to the Greco-Roman world. It combines firsthand travel observation with material drawn from earlier geographic and historical writers. It is a major source for ancient geography and ethnography.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Strabo+Geography) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Strabo+Geography) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Strabo+Geography) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Pausanias — Description of Greece**: A Greek work by Pausanias, written in the 2nd century AD, describing the topography, monuments, and traditions of mainland Greece region by region. It served travelers as a guide to sites of religious and historical significance. It is an important source for the identification and study of ancient Greek art and architecture.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pausanias+Description+of+Greece) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pausanias+Description+of+Greece) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pausanias+Description+of+Greece) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Xenophon — Anabasis (The Persian Expedition)**: A Greek prose narrative by Xenophon, an Athenian soldier and writer, describing the expedition of Greek mercenaries, the "Ten Thousand," hired by Cyrus the Younger in his bid for the Persian throne, and their retreat after Cyrus's death in battle in 401 BC. Xenophon took part in the retreat and later assumed a leadership role in it. It is considered an early example of extended first-person military narrative.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Xenophon+Anabasis) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Xenophon+Anabasis) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Xenophon+Anabasis) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Cassius Dio — Roman History**: An eighty-book Greek history of Rome by Cassius Dio, a Roman senator active in the late 2nd and early 3rd centuries AD, covering the period from Rome's legendary founding to Dio's own lifetime. Substantial portions survive only in later summaries and excerpts. It is a significant source for the late Republic and early Empire.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cassius+Dio+Roman+History) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cassius+Dio+Roman+History) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cassius+Dio+Roman+History) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Velleius Paterculus — Compendium of Roman History**: A concise Latin history of Rome by Velleius Paterculus, a Roman military officer, written around 30 AD and dedicated to a consul of that year. It surveys Roman history from its origins to the reign of Tiberius, under whom Velleius had served. It is notably brief in comparison with other surviving Roman histories.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Velleius+Paterculus+Compendium+of+Roman+History) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Velleius+Paterculus+Compendium+of+Roman+History) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Velleius+Paterculus+Compendium+of+Roman+History) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Herodian — History of the Empire**: A Greek history covering the Roman Empire from the death of Marcus Aurelius in 180 AD to the accession of Gordian III in 238 AD, written by Herodian in the mid-3rd century. It covers a period marked by frequent civil war and rapid imperial succession. It is a secondary source alongside Cassius Dio and the Historia Augusta for this period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Herodian+History+of+the+Empire) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Herodian+History+of+the+Empire) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Herodian+History+of+the+Empire) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Aulus Gellius — Attic Nights**: A Latin miscellany by Aulus Gellius, compiled in the 2nd century AD, consisting of notes on grammar, philosophy, history, and law gathered from his reading and from conversations. It is arranged in twenty books of short, loosely connected chapters. It preserves quotations and information from earlier works that would otherwise be lost.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aulus+Gellius+Attic+Nights) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aulus+Gellius+Attic+Nights) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aulus+Gellius+Attic+Nights) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Petronius — Satyricon**: A Latin prose work, probably by Gaius Petronius, written in the 1st century AD and surviving only in fragments, combining prose narrative with verse. Its best-preserved section, the Cena Trimalchionis ("Trimalchio's Feast"), depicts a lavish banquet hosted by a wealthy freedman. It is regarded as an early example of prose fiction satirizing contemporary Roman society.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Petronius+Satyricon) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Petronius+Satyricon) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Petronius+Satyricon) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Frontinus — Stratagems**: A Latin work by Sextus Julius Frontinus, a Roman general and administrator of the 1st century AD, cataloging examples of military stratagems drawn from Greek and Roman history. It is organized by category of tactic and intended as a practical supplement to his separate, now-lost treatise on military science. It was used as a reference on tactics in later periods.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Frontinus+Stratagems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Frontinus+Stratagems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Frontinus+Stratagems) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Vegetius — On Military Matters (De Re Militari)**: A Latin treatise on Roman military organization and training, written by Publius Flavius Vegetius Renatus, probably in the late 4th century AD. It describes recruitment, training, camp construction, and tactics, drawing on earlier military writings, with the stated aim of restoring earlier standards of discipline. It was widely copied and read as a military manual throughout the medieval period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Vegetius+On+Military+Matters) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Vegetius+On+Military+Matters) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Vegetius+On+Military+Matters) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Pliny the Younger — Letters**: A collection of Latin letters by Gaius Plinius Caecilius Secundus (Pliny the Younger), a Roman senator and official, written and compiled for publication in the late 1st and early 2nd centuries AD. They include an eyewitness account of the eruption of Vesuvius in 79 AD, which killed his uncle Pliny the Elder, and correspondence with the emperor Trajan concerning the administration of the province of Bithynia, including the treatment of Christians. They provide detailed information on the concerns of the Roman senatorial class.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pliny+the+Younger+Letters) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pliny+the+Younger+Letters) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pliny+the+Younger+Letters) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Varro — On the Latin Language; On Agriculture**: Marcus Terentius Varro was a Roman scholar of the 1st century BC who wrote on a wide range of subjects. On the Latin Language examines etymology and grammar and survives only in part; On Agriculture (Res Rusticae) is a treatise on farming presented in dialogue form. Only a fraction of Varro's extensive output survives.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Varro+On+the+Latin+Language) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Varro+On+the+Latin+Language) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Varro+On+the+Latin+Language) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Columella — On Agriculture (De Re Rustica)**: A Latin treatise on farming in twelve books by Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella, written in the 1st century AD. It covers topics including crop cultivation, livestock, viticulture, and estate management. It is among the most complete surviving Roman agricultural treatises, alongside those of Cato and Varro.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Columella+On+Agriculture) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Columella+On+Agriculture) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Columella+On+Agriculture) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Macrobius — Saturnalia**: A Latin work by Macrobius, probably written in the early 5th century AD, structured as a dialogue set during the Roman festival of Saturnalia among a group of learned men. Its participants discuss antiquarian, literary, and philosophical topics, with substantial attention to the works of Virgil. Macrobius's Commentary on the Dream of Scipio is a related but separate work.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Macrobius+Saturnalia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Macrobius+Saturnalia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Macrobius+Saturnalia) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

<a id="phase-iv"></a>

## Phase IV: Scripture and the Early Church

*c. 1000 BC to 6th c. AD. About 201 hours of core reading.*

The Hebrew Bible and the New Testament, followed by the writers who defined Christian doctrine in contact with Greek philosophy. Plotinus is placed here because Augustine read him before his conversion. Read the Psalms a few at a time across the whole phase.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. How does the God of Genesis and Exodus differ from the gods of Homer and Hesiod?
2. What question does Job ask, and is it answered?
3. What did the Fathers take from Plato, and what did they reject?
4. How does Augustine's City of God answer the fall of Rome?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **The Pentateuch (the Torah) — Genesis to Deuteronomy** (~25 h)
   - Read: Genesis and Exodus closely; Leviticus through Deuteronomy at a steadier pace.
   - Edition: Robert Alter, The Hebrew Bible (Norton); or any study Bible such as the New Oxford Annotated Bible (NRSV)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Pentateuch+Genesis+to+Deuteronomy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Pentateuch+Genesis+to+Deuteronomy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Pentateuch+Genesis+to+Deuteronomy) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=The+Pentateuch+Genesis+to+Deuteronomy)
2. [ ] **The Psalms** (~10 h)
   - Read: Spread across the phase: a few each day.
   - Edition: Robert Alter, The Book of Psalms (Norton); or a study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Psalms) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Psalms) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Psalms) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Psalms)
3. [ ] **The Book of Job** (~5 h)
   - Read: The frame story, the dialogues, and the speeches from the whirlwind (38-41).
   - Edition: Robert Alter, The Wisdom Books (Norton); or a study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Book+of+Job) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Book+of+Job) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Book+of+Job) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Book+of+Job)
4. [ ] **The Wisdom Books — Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, the Song of Songs** (~6 h)
   - Read: Ecclesiastes against Proverbs: the same tradition in two moods.
   - Edition: Robert Alter, The Wisdom Books (Norton); or a study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Wisdom+Books+Proverbs%2C+Ecclesiastes%2C+the+Song+of+Songs) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Wisdom+Books+Proverbs%2C+Ecclesiastes%2C+the+Song+of+Songs) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Wisdom+Books+Proverbs%2C+Ecclesiastes%2C+the+Song+of+Songs) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=The+Wisdom+Books+Proverbs%2C+Ecclesiastes%2C+the+Song+of+Songs)
5. [ ] **The Prophets — Isaiah and the rest** (~20 h)
   - Read: Isaiah, Jeremiah, Amos. Note the demand for justice and the promise of restoration.
   - Edition: Robert Alter, The Hebrew Bible, vol. 2 (Norton); or a study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Prophets+Isaiah+and+the+rest) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Prophets+Isaiah+and+the+rest) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Prophets+Isaiah+and+the+rest) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=The+Prophets+Isaiah+and+the+rest)
6. [ ] **The Four Gospels — Matthew, Mark, Luke, John** (~15 h)
   - Read: Mark first, then Matthew, Luke, and John. Note what John includes that the other three do not.
   - Edition: New Oxford Annotated Bible (NRSV) or the ESV Study Bible; David Bentley Hart, The New Testament (Yale) for a literal rendering
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Four+Gospels+Matthew%2C+Mark%2C+Luke%2C+John) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Four+Gospels+Matthew%2C+Mark%2C+Luke%2C+John) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Four+Gospels+Matthew%2C+Mark%2C+Luke%2C+John) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=The+Four+Gospels+Matthew%2C+Mark%2C+Luke%2C+John)
7. [ ] **The Acts of the Apostles** (~4 h)
   - Read: Paul's speech at Athens (17:16-34) meets Phase 2 directly.
   - Edition: New Oxford Annotated Bible (NRSV) or another study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Acts+of+the+Apostles) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Acts+of+the+Apostles) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Acts+of+the+Apostles) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Acts+of+the+Apostles)
8. [ ] **The Pauline Epistles** (~10 h)
   - Read: Romans, 1 Corinthians (chapters 13 and 15), Galatians, Philippians.
   - Edition: New Oxford Annotated Bible (NRSV) or another study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pauline+Epistles) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pauline+Epistles) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pauline+Epistles) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Pauline+Epistles)
9. [ ] **The Apocalypse (Revelation)** (~3 h)
   - Read: Read after Daniel if possible; the imagery draws on it.
   - Edition: New Oxford Annotated Bible (NRSV) or another study Bible
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=King+James+Bible) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Apocalypse) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Apocalypse) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Apocalypse) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Apocalypse)
10. [ ] **The Didache** (~1 h)
   - Read: The earliest surviving church manual: the Two Ways, baptism, the Eucharist.
   - Edition: In Early Christian Writings: The Apostolic Fathers, tr. Staniforth and Louth (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Didache) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Didache) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Didache) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Didache) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Didache)
11. [ ] **St. Ignatius of Antioch — The Letters** (~2 h)
   - Read: The Letter to the Romans, written on the way to his execution.
   - Edition: In Early Christian Writings: The Apostolic Fathers (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ignatius+Epistles) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ignatius+of+Antioch+Letters) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ignatius+of+Antioch+Letters) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ignatius+of+Antioch+Letters) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Ignatius+of+Antioch+Letters)
12. [ ] **St. Justin Martyr — The First and Second Apologies** (~4 h)
   - Read: The First Apology and its idea of the logos spermatikos in pagan philosophy.
   - Edition: Tr. Leslie William Barnard, The First and Second Apologies (Paulist, Ancient Christian Writers)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Justin+Martyr+Apology) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Justin+Martyr+First+and+Second+Apologies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Justin+Martyr+First+and+Second+Apologies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Justin+Martyr+First+and+Second+Apologies) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Justin+Martyr+First+and+Second+Apologies)
13. [ ] **St. Irenaeus — Against Heresies** (~12 h)
   - Read: Book I on the Gnostic systems, then Books III-IV on apostolic tradition and recapitulation.
   - Edition: Ante-Nicene Fathers vol. 1 (free at New Advent); or Robert M. Grant, Irenaeus of Lyons (Routledge) for selections
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Irenaeus+Against+Heresies) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Irenaeus+Against+Heresies) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Irenaeus+Against+Heresies) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Irenaeus+Against+Heresies) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Irenaeus+Against+Heresies)
14. [ ] **St. Athanasius — On the Incarnation; The Life of Antony** (~6 h)
   - Read: On the Incarnation in full; The Life of Antony founded the genre of the desert saint.
   - Edition: On the Incarnation, tr. John Behr (St Vladimir's Seminary Press); The Life of Antony, tr. Robert C. Gregg (Paulist)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Athanasius+Incarnation) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Athanasius+On+the+Incarnation) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Athanasius+On+the+Incarnation) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Athanasius+On+the+Incarnation) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Athanasius+On+the+Incarnation)
15. [ ] **Plotinus — The Enneads** (~20 h)
   - Read: I.6 (On Beauty), V.1, and VI.9 (On the Good or the One). These are the 'books of the Platonists' Augustine mentions in Confessions VII.
   - Edition: Tr. Stephen MacKenna, abridged by John Dillon (Penguin); or the Gerson edition (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plotinus+Enneads+MacKenna) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Plotinus+Enneads) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Plotinus+Enneads) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plotinus+Enneads) · [Perseus Greco-Roman](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/collection?collection=Perseus:collection:Greco-Roman) · [MIT Internet Classics Archive](https://classics.mit.edu/Browse/index.html) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
16. [ ] **St. Augustine — Confessions** (~12 h)
   - Read: Books I-IX narrative, X on memory, XI on time.
   - Edition: Tr. Henry Chadwick (Oxford World's Classics) or Sarah Ruden (Modern Library)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Augustine+Confessions+Pusey) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Augustine+Confessions) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Augustine+Confessions) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Augustine+Confessions) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Augustine+Confessions)
17. [ ] **St. Augustine — The City of God; On Christian Doctrine; On the Trinity** (~40 h)
   - Read: City of God I-V, XI-XIV, XIX. On Christian Doctrine Book II for how the tradition read texts.
   - Edition: City of God tr. Henry Bettenson (Penguin); On Christian Doctrine tr. R.P.H. Green (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Augustine+City+of+God+Dods) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Augustine+City+of+God) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Augustine+City+of+God) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Augustine+City+of+God) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Augustine+City+of+God)
18. [ ] **St. Benedict of Nursia — The Rule** (~3 h)
   - Read: The Prologue and chapters on obedience, humility (7), and daily order. It governed Western monasticism.
   - Edition: RB 1980: The Rule of St. Benedict, ed. Timothy Fry (Liturgical Press)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Rule+of+Saint+Benedict) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Benedict+of+Nursia+Rule) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Benedict+of+Nursia+Rule) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Benedict+of+Nursia+Rule) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Benedict+of+Nursia+Rule)
19. [ ] **Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite — The Celestial Hierarchy; The Mystical Theology** (~3 h)
   - Read: The Mystical Theology is very short: the source of the negative way used in Phases 5 and 8.
   - Edition: Pseudo-Dionysius: The Complete Works, tr. Colm Luibheid (Paulist, Classics of Western Spirituality)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Dionysius+Areopagite) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pseudo-Dionysius+the+Areopagite+Celestial+Hierarchy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pseudo-Dionysius+the+Areopagite+Celestial+Hierarchy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pseudo-Dionysius+the+Areopagite+Celestial+Hierarchy) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Pseudo-Dionysius+the+Areopagite+Celestial+Hierarchy)

**Synthesis.** Write on Augustine as the meeting point of Phase 2 (Plato and Plotinus), Phase 3 (Rome and Cicero), and Scripture.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **St. Jerome (trans.) — The Vulgate**: A Latin translation of the Bible produced largely by Jerome in the late 4th and early 5th centuries AD, translating the Old Testament directly from Hebrew and revising existing Latin translations of the New Testament. It became the standard biblical text of the Western Church for centuries and was affirmed as authoritative by the Council of Trent in 1546.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jerome+Vulgate) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jerome+Vulgate) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jerome+Vulgate) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Jerome+Vulgate)
- [ ] **The Septuagint**: A Greek translation of the Hebrew Scriptures produced in stages beginning in the 3rd century BC, primarily in Alexandria. It was widely used by Greek-speaking Jewish communities and by early Christians, and it is frequently quoted in the New Testament. It also includes several books not present in the later Hebrew canon.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Septuagint) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Septuagint) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Septuagint) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Septuagint)
- [ ] **The Deuterocanon — Wisdom of Solomon, Sirach, and the rest**: A group of texts included in the Septuagint and the Latin Vulgate but excluded from the Hebrew Bible and, subsequently, from most Protestant biblical canons following the Reformation. They include the Wisdom of Solomon, Sirach, Tobit, Judith, and 1-2 Maccabees, among others, composed mostly between the 3rd and 1st centuries BC. Catholic and Orthodox traditions regard them as canonical scripture, while Protestant traditions generally classify them as apocrypha.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Deuterocanon+Wisdom+of+Solomon%2C+Sirach%2C+and+the+rest) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Deuterocanon+Wisdom+of+Solomon%2C+Sirach%2C+and+the+rest) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Deuterocanon+Wisdom+of+Solomon%2C+Sirach%2C+and+the+rest) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=The+Deuterocanon+Wisdom+of+Solomon%2C+Sirach%2C+and+the+rest)
- [ ] **The Gallican / Roman Psalter**: Latin versions of the Book of Psalms arranged for liturgical recitation in the Western Church. The Gallican Psalter, based on Jerome's revision from the Greek Septuagint, became the version most widely used in medieval monastic worship. These arrangements structured the recitation of the Psalms across a weekly or other fixed cycle in monastic communities.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gallican+%2F+Roman+Psalter) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gallican+%2F+Roman+Psalter) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gallican+%2F+Roman+Psalter) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Gallican+%2F+Roman+Psalter)
- [ ] **The Roman Breviary (the Divine Office)**: The liturgical book containing the texts for the Liturgy of the Hours, the cycle of prayers, psalms, and readings recited by clergy and religious at fixed times throughout the day. Its structure developed over centuries from early Christian and monastic practice, with the Roman Breviary standardized in its modern form after the Council of Trent. Its use remains obligatory for clergy in the Catholic Church, in updated forms following the Second Vatican Council.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Roman+Breviary) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Roman+Breviary) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Roman+Breviary) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Roman+Breviary)
- [ ] **The Roman Missal**: The liturgical book containing the texts and rubrics for the celebration of the Mass in the Roman Rite of the Catholic Church. Its contents developed over many centuries, with major standardized editions issued after the Council of Trent (1570) and following the Second Vatican Council (1970). It includes prayers, scripture-reading assignments, and instructions for the liturgical calendar.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Roman+Missal) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Roman+Missal) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Roman+Missal) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Roman+Missal)
- [ ] **The Roman Martyrology**: The official liturgical martyrology of the Catholic Church, listing saints and martyrs commemorated on each day of the calendar year, along with brief biographical notices. Its current form developed from earlier martyrologies and was revised several times, most recently in 2001 and 2004. It is used in monastic and clerical communities for daily recitation.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Roman+Martyrology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Roman+Martyrology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Roman+Martyrology) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Roman+Martyrology)
- [ ] **The Catholic Epistles — James, Peter, John, Jude**: A group of New Testament letters — James, 1-2 Peter, 1-3 John, and Jude — traditionally described as "catholic" or "general" because they are addressed to broader Christian audiences rather than to specific individual churches. They cover topics including ethical conduct, perseverance under persecution, and warnings against false teaching. Their dates of composition and authorship are debated among scholars.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Catholic+Epistles+James%2C+Peter%2C+John%2C+Jude) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Catholic+Epistles+James%2C+Peter%2C+John%2C+Jude) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Catholic+Epistles+James%2C+Peter%2C+John%2C+Jude) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=The+Catholic+Epistles+James%2C+Peter%2C+John%2C+Jude)
- [ ] **The Book of Daniel**: A book of the Hebrew Bible combining narrative and apocalyptic visions, set during the Babylonian and Persian periods but generally dated by modern scholars to the 2nd century BC in its final form. It includes stories such as Daniel in the lions' den and the three men in the fiery furnace, alongside symbolic visions of successive empires. It contributed imagery, including the phrase "Son of Man," later taken up in the New Testament.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Book+of+Daniel) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Book+of+Daniel) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Book+of+Daniel) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Book+of+Daniel)
- [ ] **The Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom**: The most commonly used eucharistic liturgy in the Eastern Orthodox Church, traditionally attributed to St. John Chrysostom (c. 349-407 AD), though its present form developed over subsequent centuries. It structures the celebration of the Eucharist with a fixed sequence of prayers, litanies, and hymns. It is used on most days of the liturgical year outside of specific fasting periods.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Divine+Liturgy+of+St.+John+Chrysostom) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Divine+Liturgy+of+St.+John+Chrysostom) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Divine+Liturgy+of+St.+John+Chrysostom) · [Bible Gateway](https://www.biblegateway.com/quicksearch/?quicksearch=Divine+Liturgy+of+St.+John+Chrysostom)
- [ ] **St. Gregory the Great — Pastoral Care (Liber Regulae Pastoralis)**: A treatise written around 590-591 AD by Pope Gregory I, addressing the responsibilities and conduct expected of bishops and other pastors. It discusses how spiritual guidance should be adapted to different types of people and circumstances. It was translated into Old English under King Alfred the Great in the 9th century for distribution to English bishops.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gregory+the+Great+Pastoral+Care) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gregory+the+Great+Pastoral+Care) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gregory+the+Great+Pastoral+Care) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Gregory+the+Great+Pastoral+Care)
- [ ] **St. Aldhelm — In Praise of Virginity (De Virginitate)**: A work by the Anglo-Saxon scholar and bishop Aldhelm (c. 639-709 AD), composed in both prose and verse versions, addressing the theme of religious virginity and chastity. It is written in an elaborate, Latinate style characteristic of Aldhelm's works. It is among the earliest substantial Latin literary works produced in Anglo-Saxon England.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aldhelm+In+Praise+of+Virginity) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aldhelm+In+Praise+of+Virginity) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aldhelm+In+Praise+of+Virginity) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Aldhelm+In+Praise+of+Virginity)
- [ ] **St. Clement of Rome — The First Epistle to the Corinthians**: A letter, likely written in the late 1st century AD, sent from the Christian community in Rome to the church at Corinth to address a leadership dispute there. It is among the earliest surviving Christian texts outside the New Testament and references the authority of church leadership established through apostolic succession. It was read as scripture in some early Christian communities, though it was not included in the eventual New Testament canon.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Clement+of+Rome+First+Epistle+to+the+Corinthians) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Clement+of+Rome+First+Epistle+to+the+Corinthians) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Clement+of+Rome+First+Epistle+to+the+Corinthians) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Clement+of+Rome+First+Epistle+to+the+Corinthians)
- [ ] **Tertullian — Apology**: A work written around 197 AD by the North African writer Tertullian, defending Christians against Roman legal persecution and popular accusations. Tertullian is credited with significant contributions to Latin Christian theological vocabulary. He later associated with the Montanist movement, considered heterodox by the broader Church.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tertullian+Apology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tertullian+Apology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tertullian+Apology) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Tertullian+Apology)
- [ ] **St. Clement of Alexandria — The Stromata; The Tutor**: Works by the 2nd-3rd century AD theologian Clement of Alexandria, who taught at the catechetical school in Alexandria. The Stromata ("Miscellanies") discusses the relationship between Greek philosophy and Christian faith; The Tutor (Paedagogus) addresses Christian ethical conduct. Clement argued that philosophy could serve as a preparation for Christian belief.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Clement+of+Alexandria+Stromata) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Clement+of+Alexandria+Stromata) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Clement+of+Alexandria+Stromata) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Clement+of+Alexandria+Stromata)
- [ ] **Origen — On First Principles**: A systematic theological work by the 3rd-century AD scholar Origen of Alexandria, one of the earliest attempts at a comprehensive Christian theology. It addresses topics including the nature of God, creation, free will, and the eventual state of all rational beings. Some of Origen's speculative positions were later condemned as heterodox by church councils, though his broader influence on Christian theology and biblical scholarship was substantial.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Origen+On+First+Principles) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Origen+On+First+Principles) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Origen+On+First+Principles) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Origen+On+First+Principles)
- [ ] **Eusebius — Ecclesiastical History**: A history of the Christian Church from its origins to the early 4th century AD, written by Eusebius of Caesarea and completed in stages, with a final edition after 324 AD. It preserves excerpts from earlier documents, many otherwise lost, including accounts of persecutions and martyrdoms. It is a primary source for the history of early Christianity.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Eusebius+Ecclesiastical+History) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Eusebius+Ecclesiastical+History) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Eusebius+Ecclesiastical+History) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Eusebius+Ecclesiastical+History)
- [ ] **St. Hilary of Poitiers — On the Trinity**: A theological work by Hilary, bishop of Poitiers (c. 310-367 AD), written partly during his exile under the Arian-sympathizing emperor Constantius II. It defends the doctrine of the full divinity of Christ against Arian positions that held the Son to be a created, subordinate being. It is among the earliest major Latin treatments of Trinitarian theology.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hilary+of+Poitiers+On+the+Trinity) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hilary+of+Poitiers+On+the+Trinity) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hilary+of+Poitiers+On+the+Trinity) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Hilary+of+Poitiers+On+the+Trinity)
- [ ] **St. Basil the Great — On the Holy Spirit; Hexaemeron; To Young Men**: Works by Basil of Caesarea (c. 330-379 AD), one of the three Cappadocian Fathers. On the Holy Spirit argues for the divinity of the Holy Spirit; the Hexaemeron is a series of homilies on the six days of creation; To Young Men advises Christian students on how to make selective use of classical Greek literature. Basil also contributed to the organization of communal monastic life in the Eastern Church.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Basil+the+Great+On+the+Holy+Spirit) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Basil+the+Great+On+the+Holy+Spirit) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Basil+the+Great+On+the+Holy+Spirit) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Basil+the+Great+On+the+Holy+Spirit)
- [ ] **St. Gregory of Nazianzus — The Theological Orations**: Five orations delivered by Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329-390 AD) in Constantinople around 380 AD, addressing Trinitarian theology, particularly the divinity of the Son and the Holy Spirit. Gregory, another of the Cappadocian Fathers, is traditionally given the title "the Theologian" in the Eastern Church. The orations were influential in the doctrinal settlement affirmed at the First Council of Constantinople in 381.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gregory+of+Nazianzus+Theological+Orations) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gregory+of+Nazianzus+Theological+Orations) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gregory+of+Nazianzus+Theological+Orations) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Gregory+of+Nazianzus+Theological+Orations)
- [ ] **St. Gregory of Nyssa — The Life of Moses; On the Soul and the Resurrection**: Works by Gregory of Nyssa (c. 335-395 AD), the third of the Cappadocian Fathers. The Life of Moses interprets the biblical account of Moses allegorically as a model of spiritual progress toward God. On the Soul and the Resurrection, structured as a dialogue, discusses the nature of the soul and Christian teaching on resurrection; Gregory developed the concept of epektasis, describing spiritual growth as an unending process.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gregory+of+Nyssa+Life+of+Moses) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gregory+of+Nyssa+Life+of+Moses) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gregory+of+Nyssa+Life+of+Moses) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Gregory+of+Nyssa+Life+of+Moses)
- [ ] **St. John Chrysostom — On the Priesthood; the Homilies**: Works by John Chrysostom (c. 347-407 AD), archbishop of Constantinople, known for his skill as a preacher. On the Priesthood discusses the responsibilities and challenges of pastoral ministry. His numerous surviving homilies offer verse-by-verse scriptural commentary along with moral exhortation, including attention to care for the poor.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+Chrysostom+On+the+Priesthood) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+Chrysostom+On+the+Priesthood) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+Chrysostom+On+the+Priesthood) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=John+Chrysostom+On+the+Priesthood)
- [ ] **St. Ambrose — On the Duties of the Clergy; Hexameron**: Works by Ambrose, bishop of Milan (c. 340-397 AD), a former Roman governor. On the Duties of the Clergy (De Officiis) adapts Cicero's philosophical treatment of ethical duty to a Christian clerical context. Ambrose is known for instructing Augustine of Hippo prior to Augustine's conversion and for requiring the emperor Theodosius I to perform public penance following a massacre at Thessalonica.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ambrose+On+the+Duties+of+the+Clergy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ambrose+On+the+Duties+of+the+Clergy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ambrose+On+the+Duties+of+the+Clergy) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Ambrose+On+the+Duties+of+the+Clergy)
- [ ] **St. Jerome — Letters; On Illustrious Men**: Writings of Jerome (c. 342-420 AD), the scholar-monk best known for translating the Vulgate Bible (see Book 15). His surviving letters address scriptural, ascetic, and controversial topics. On Illustrious Men is a biographical catalog of Christian authors up to Jerome's own time, modeled on earlier Roman biographical works.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jerome+Letters) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jerome+Letters) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jerome+Letters) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Jerome+Letters)
- [ ] **St. John Cassian — Conferences; Institutes**: Works by John Cassian (c. 360-435 AD), who transmitted the monastic practices of the Egyptian desert to the Latin-speaking West. The Conferences record discussions attributed to Egyptian monastic teachers on topics including prayer and spiritual discernment. The Institutes addresses the organization of monastic communities and discusses eight principal vices; both works influenced the later Rule of St. Benedict.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+Cassian+Conferences) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+Cassian+Conferences) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+Cassian+Conferences) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=John+Cassian+Conferences)
- [ ] **St. Cyprian — On the Unity of the Church**: A treatise written around 251 AD by Cyprian, bishop of Carthage, addressing a schism within the North African church. It argues for the unity of the Church under the collective authority of its bishops. Cyprian was executed during the persecution under the emperor Valerian in 258 AD.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cyprian+On+the+Unity+of+the+Church) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cyprian+On+the+Unity+of+the+Church) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cyprian+On+the+Unity+of+the+Church) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Cyprian+On+the+Unity+of+the+Church)
- [ ] **Lactantius — The Divine Institutes**: A work by the early 4th-century Christian writer Lactantius, presenting Christian doctrine in classical Latin prose style for an educated Roman readership. It critiques polytheistic religion and presents Christianity as compatible with, and superior to, classical philosophical ethics. Lactantius later served as tutor to Crispus, son of the emperor Constantine.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lactantius+Divine+Institutes) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lactantius+Divine+Institutes) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lactantius+Divine+Institutes) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Lactantius+Divine+Institutes)
- [ ] **St. Leo the Great — The Tome; the Sermons**: Writings of Pope Leo I (r. 440-461 AD). The Tome of Leo, a letter addressing the theological dispute over the nature of Christ, was influential at the Council of Chalcedon in 451, which affirmed that Christ has two natures, divine and human, in one person. His surviving sermons address doctrinal and liturgical topics; Leo is also noted for meeting Attila the Hun in 452, traditionally credited with persuading him not to advance on Rome.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Leo+the+Great+Tome) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Leo+the+Great+Tome) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Leo+the+Great+Tome) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Leo+the+Great+Tome)
- [ ] **St. Gregory the Great — Dialogues; Moralia in Job**: Works by Pope Gregory I (r. 590-604 AD), also known for the Pastoral Care (above) and for sending missionaries to convert Anglo-Saxon England. The Dialogues recount lives and miracles of Italian saints, including Benedict of Nursia. The Moralia in Job is an extended allegorical and moral commentary on the Book of Job.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gregory+the+Great+Dialogues) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gregory+the+Great+Dialogues) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gregory+the+Great+Dialogues) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Gregory+the+Great+Dialogues)
- [ ] **Cassiodorus — Institutions of Divine and Secular Learning**: A work by the Roman statesman Cassiodorus (c. 485-585 AD), written after he founded the monastery of Vivarium in southern Italy. The Institutions outlines a curriculum combining the study of scripture with the classical liberal arts, intended to guide the monastery's work of copying manuscripts. The monastery's copying activity contributed to the preservation of a number of classical texts.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cassiodorus+Institutions+of+Divine+and+Secular+Learning) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cassiodorus+Institutions+of+Divine+and+Secular+Learning) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cassiodorus+Institutions+of+Divine+and+Secular+Learning) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Cassiodorus+Institutions+of+Divine+and+Secular+Learning)
- [ ] **St. Maximus the Confessor — The Ambigua; Centuries on Love**: Works by the Byzantine monk and theologian Maximus the Confessor (c. 580-662 AD). The Ambigua addresses difficult passages in earlier theological writers, particularly Gregory of Nazianzus. Maximus opposed the Monothelite position that Christ had only a divine will, and he was mutilated and exiled for this opposition; his theological positions were later vindicated at the Third Council of Constantinople in 680-681.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Maximus+the+Confessor+Ambigua) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Maximus+the+Confessor+Ambigua) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Maximus+the+Confessor+Ambigua) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Maximus+the+Confessor+Ambigua)
- [ ] **St. John of Damascus — On the Orthodox Faith**: A theological work by John of Damascus (c. 675-749 AD), part of his larger Fount of Knowledge, summarizing earlier Greek patristic theology into a systematic exposition. John also wrote in defense of the use of religious images during the Byzantine Iconoclast controversy, arguing that the Incarnation justified depicting Christ. He is traditionally regarded as the last of the major Greek Church Fathers.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+of+Damascus+On+the+Orthodox+Faith) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+of+Damascus+On+the+Orthodox+Faith) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+of+Damascus+On+the+Orthodox+Faith) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=John+of+Damascus+On+the+Orthodox+Faith)
- [ ] **The Shepherd of Hermas**: An early Christian text, likely composed in Rome during the 1st or 2nd century AD, presented as a series of visions, mandates, and parables concerning repentance and church discipline. It was widely read in the early Church, and some early sources considered it for inclusion in the New Testament canon, though it was ultimately excluded.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Shepherd+of+Hermas) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Shepherd+of+Hermas) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Shepherd+of+Hermas) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Shepherd+of+Hermas)
- [ ] **St. Ephrem the Syrian — The Hymns on Paradise and the Nativity**: Hymns composed by Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306-373 AD), a deacon and theologian who wrote in Syriac. The Hymns on Paradise use imagery to explore biblical and theological themes concerning creation and paradise; the Nativity hymns address the birth of Christ. Ephrem's poetic hymns were used for both liturgical and catechetical purposes in the Syriac Christian tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ephrem+the+Syrian+Hymns+on+Paradise+and+the+Nativity) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ephrem+the+Syrian+Hymns+on+Paradise+and+the+Nativity) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ephrem+the+Syrian+Hymns+on+Paradise+and+the+Nativity) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Ephrem+the+Syrian+Hymns+on+Paradise+and+the+Nativity)
- [ ] **St. Vincent of Lerins — The Commonitory**: A treatise written around 434 AD by Vincent, a monk at the monastery of Lerins, proposing criteria for distinguishing authentic Christian doctrine from heresy. It is known for articulating the standard of belief "held everywhere, always, and by all" (quod ubique, quod semper, quod ab omnibus). It addresses the concept of legitimate doctrinal development within continuity of tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Vincent+of+Lerins+Commonitory) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Vincent+of+Lerins+Commonitory) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Vincent+of+Lerins+Commonitory) · [New Advent: Fathers](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Vincent+of+Lerins+Commonitory)

<a id="phase-v"></a>

## Phase V: The Middle Ages

*6th to 15th c.. About 232 hours of core reading.*

Three strands interleaved: the northern heroic literature (Anglo-Saxon, Norse, Celtic), the theology of the schools and cloisters, and the romance and epic that culminated in Dante and Chaucer. Boethius opens the phase because every medieval writer knew him.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. How does the heroic code of Beowulf and the sagas change once Christianity arrives?
2. What is scholastic method, and why does Aquinas write in questions and objections?
3. How does courtly love in the romances relate to love of God in the mystics?
4. What does Dante's structure (three realms, 100 cantos) say about his view of order?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **St. Boethius — The Consolation of Philosophy** (~6 h)
   - Read: Written in prison awaiting execution. Book II on Fortune's wheel, Book V on providence and free will.
   - Edition: Tr. P.G. Walsh (Oxford World's Classics) or Joel Relihan (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Boethius+Consolation) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Boethius+Consolation+of+Philosophy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Boethius+Consolation+of+Philosophy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Boethius+Consolation+of+Philosophy) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Boethius+Consolation+of+Philosophy)
2. [ ] **St. Bede — Ecclesiastical History of the English People** (~12 h)
   - Read: Book II.13 (the sparrow in the hall) and IV.24 (Caedmon).
   - Edition: Tr. Leo Sherley-Price, rev. Farmer (Penguin); or Colgrave and McClure (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bede+Ecclesiastical+History) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bede+Ecclesiastical+History+of+the+English+People) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bede+Ecclesiastical+History+of+the+English+People) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bede+Ecclesiastical+History+of+the+English+People)
3. [ ] **Anon. — Beowulf** (~5 h)
   - Read: The poem's three fights and its Christian narrator commenting on a pagan past.
   - Edition: Tr. Seamus Heaney (Norton); or Roy Liuzza (Broadview), with notes
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Beowulf+Gummere) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Beowulf) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Beowulf) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Beowulf)
4. [ ] **The Anglo-Saxon Elegies — The Wanderer; The Seafarer; The Dream of the Rood; The Battle of Maldon** (~2 h)
   - Read: The Wanderer and The Seafarer first; The Dream of the Rood recasts the Crucifixion in heroic diction.
   - Edition: In The Word Exchange: Anglo-Saxon Poems in Translation, ed. Delanty and Matto (Norton); or Kevin Crossley-Holland, The Anglo-Saxon World (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Anglo-Saxon+Elegies+Wanderer) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Anglo-Saxon+Elegies+Wanderer) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Anglo-Saxon+Elegies+Wanderer)
5. [ ] **The Poetic Edda** (~10 h)
   - Read: Voluspa (the creation and end of the world) and Havamal (the sayings of Odin), then the heroic poems.
   - Edition: Tr. Carolyne Larrington (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Poetic+Edda+Bellows) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Poetic+Edda) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Poetic+Edda) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Poetic+Edda)
6. [ ] **Njal's Saga; Egil's Saga; Laxdaela Saga** (~15 h)
   - Read: Njal's Saga first. Law, feud, and the conversion of Iceland.
   - Edition: Njal's Saga tr. Robert Cook (Penguin); The Sagas of Icelanders, ed. Ornolfur Thorsson (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Njal+Dasent) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Njal%27s+Saga) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Njal%27s+Saga) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Njal%27s+Saga)
7. [ ] **Tain Bo Cuailnge (The Cattle Raid of Cooley)** (~8 h)
   - Read: Cu Chulainn's defense of Ulster; the central epic of the Ulster Cycle.
   - Edition: Tr. Thomas Kinsella, The Tain (Oxford); or Ciaran Carson (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tain+Bo+Cuailnge) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tain+Bo+Cuailnge) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tain+Bo+Cuailnge) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tain+Bo+Cuailnge)
8. [ ] **The Mabinogion** (~8 h)
   - Read: The Four Branches first, then Culhwch and Olwen for the earliest Arthur.
   - Edition: Tr. Sioned Davies (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Mabinogion+Guest) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Mabinogion) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Mabinogion) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Mabinogion)
9. [ ] **The Song of Roland** (~4 h)
   - Read: Compare Roland's pride with Achilles'. Oliver is the counterweight.
   - Edition: Tr. Glyn Burgess (Penguin) or Dorothy L. Sayers (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Song+of+Roland) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Song+of+Roland) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Song+of+Roland) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Song+of+Roland)
10. [ ] **St. Anselm — Proslogion; Monologion; Cur Deus Homo** (~5 h)
   - Read: Proslogion 2-4 (the ontological argument) and Cur Deus Homo.
   - Edition: Anselm of Canterbury: The Major Works, ed. Davies and Evans (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Anselm+Proslogium) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Anselm+Proslogion) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Anselm+Proslogion) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Anselm+Proslogion) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Anselm+Proslogion)
11. [ ] **Peter Abelard — Sic et Non; Historia Calamitatum** (~4 h)
   - Read: Historia Calamitatum; the prologue to Sic et Non sets out the method of reconciling authorities.
   - Edition: The Letters of Abelard and Heloise, tr. Betty Radice, rev. Clanchy (Penguin), includes Historia Calamitatum
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Abelard+Historia+Calamitatum) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Peter+Abelard+Sic+et+Non) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Peter+Abelard+Sic+et+Non) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Peter+Abelard+Sic+et+Non) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Peter+Abelard+Sic+et+Non)
12. [ ] **St. Bernard of Clairvaux — On Loving God; Sermons on the Song of Songs** (~5 h)
   - Read: On Loving God: the four degrees of love.
   - Edition: Bernard of Clairvaux: Selected Works, tr. G.R. Evans (Paulist, Classics of Western Spirituality)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bernard+Loving+God) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bernard+of+Clairvaux+On+Loving+God) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bernard+of+Clairvaux+On+Loving+God) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bernard+of+Clairvaux+On+Loving+God) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Bernard+of+Clairvaux+On+Loving+God)
13. [ ] **Chretien de Troyes — Perceval; Lancelot; Yvain; Erec and Enide** (~12 h)
   - Read: Yvain and Lancelot, then Perceval, the first Grail story.
   - Edition: Tr. William W. Kibler and Carleton W. Carroll, Arthurian Romances (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Chretien+de+Troyes) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Chretien+de+Troyes+Perceval) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Chretien+de+Troyes+Perceval) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Chretien+de+Troyes+Perceval)
14. [ ] **Anon. — Sir Gawain and the Green Knight** (~4 h)
   - Read: The three temptations and the three blows.
   - Edition: Tr. Simon Armitage (Norton) or J.R.R. Tolkien (Del Rey / HarperCollins)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sir+Gawain+Green+Knight) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sir+Gawain+and+the+Green+Knight) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sir+Gawain+and+the+Green+Knight) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sir+Gawain+and+the+Green+Knight)
15. [ ] **St. Thomas Aquinas — Summa Theologiae; Summa contra Gentiles; On Kingship** (~40 h)
   - Read: Summa Theologiae I qq.1-13 (God, including the five ways in q.2), I-II qq.90-97 (law), selections on the virtues in II-II; On Kingship.
   - Edition: Summa Theologiae: A Concise Translation, ed. Timothy McDermott (Christian Classics); On Law, Morality, and Politics, ed. Regan and Baumgarth (Hackett); full Dominican Fathers translation free at New Advent
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aquinas+Summa+Theologica) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Thomas+Aquinas+Summa+Theologiae) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Thomas+Aquinas+Summa+Theologiae) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Thomas+Aquinas+Summa+Theologiae) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Thomas+Aquinas+Summa+Theologiae)
16. [ ] **St. Bonaventure — The Journey of the Mind to God** (~3 h)
   - Read: A short itinerary of the mind's ascent in six stages, set against Aquinas.
   - Edition: Bonaventure: The Soul's Journey into God, tr. Ewert Cousins (Paulist); or tr. Philotheus Boehner (Franciscan Institute)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bonaventure+Journey+of+the+Mind+to+God) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bonaventure+Journey+of+the+Mind+to+God) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bonaventure+Journey+of+the+Mind+to+God) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Bonaventure+Journey+of+the+Mind+to+God)
17. [ ] **Dante Alighieri — The Divine Comedy; De Monarchia** (~30 h)
   - Read: The Divine Comedy in order. Many readers stop after Inferno; Purgatorio and Paradiso are where the argument resolves.
   - Edition: Tr. Robert and Jean Hollander (Anchor, facing Italian with notes); or John Ciardi (Signet); or Allen Mandelbaum (Everyman)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Dante+Divine+Comedy+Longfellow) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Dante+Alighieri+Divine+Comedy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Dante+Alighieri+Divine+Comedy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Dante+Alighieri+Divine+Comedy)
18. [ ] **Geoffrey Chaucer — The Canterbury Tales; Troilus and Criseyde** (~20 h)
   - Read: The General Prologue, then the Knight's, Miller's, Wife of Bath's, Pardoner's, and Nun's Priest's Tales.
   - Edition: Middle English: The Riverside Chaucer (Oxford) or the Norton Critical Edition; modern English tr. Nevill Coghill (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Chaucer+Canterbury+Tales) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Geoffrey+Chaucer+Canterbury+Tales) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Geoffrey+Chaucer+Canterbury+Tales) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Geoffrey+Chaucer+Canterbury+Tales)
19. [ ] **Anon. — The Cloud of Unknowing** (~4 h)
   - Read: The negative way from Pseudo-Dionysius put into English practice.
   - Edition: Tr. A.C. Spearing (Penguin); or ed. James Walsh (Paulist)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cloud+of+Unknowing) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cloud+of+Unknowing) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cloud+of+Unknowing) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cloud+of+Unknowing) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Cloud+of+Unknowing)
20. [ ] **Julian of Norwich — Revelations of Divine Love** (~5 h)
   - Read: The first woman known to have written a book in English. The parable of the lord and the servant (ch. 51).
   - Edition: Tr. Elizabeth Spearing (Penguin); or Colledge and Walsh (Paulist)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Revelations+of+Divine+Love) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Julian+of+Norwich+Revelations+of+Divine+Love) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Julian+of+Norwich+Revelations+of+Divine+Love) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Julian+of+Norwich+Revelations+of+Divine+Love) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Julian+of+Norwich+Revelations+of+Divine+Love)
21. [ ] **Sir Thomas Malory — Le Morte d'Arthur** (~25 h)
   - Read: The Tale of the Sankgreal and the last two tales: the fall of the Round Table.
