   - Edition: Ed. Stephen H.A. Shepherd (Norton Critical Edition); or Helen Cooper's abridgment (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Malory+Morte+d%27Arthur) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sir+Thomas+Malory+Le+Morte+d%27Arthur) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sir+Thomas+Malory+Le+Morte+d%27Arthur) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sir+Thomas+Malory+Le+Morte+d%27Arthur)
22. [ ] **Thomas a Kempis — The Imitation of Christ** (~5 h)
   - Read: Books I-II. It closes the phase as the late-medieval devotional handbook.
   - Edition: Tr. Leo Sherley-Price (Penguin); or William Creasy (Ave Maria Press)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Imitation+of+Christ) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Thomas+a+Kempis+Imitation+of+Christ) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Thomas+a+Kempis+Imitation+of+Christ) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Thomas+a+Kempis+Imitation+of+Christ) · [CCEL](https://www.ccel.org/search?qu=Thomas+a+Kempis+Imitation+of+Christ)

**Synthesis.** Compare a Germanic hero (Beowulf or Njal) with a romance knight (Gawain or Lancelot) and with Dante the pilgrim.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Snorri Sturluson — The Prose Edda; Heimskringla**: Works by the thirteenth-century Icelandic chieftain and writer Snorri Sturluson. The Prose Edda is a handbook of Norse mythology and poetic technique, intended to preserve knowledge needed to compose traditional skaldic verse; Heimskringla is a history of the kings of Norway from legendary origins to the twelfth century.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Snorri+Sturluson+Prose+Edda) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Snorri+Sturluson+Prose+Edda) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Snorri+Sturluson+Prose+Edda)
- [ ] **The Nibelungenlied**: An anonymous Middle High German epic poem, composed around 1200, telling of the hero Siegfried, his murder, and the subsequent revenge taken by his widow Kriemhild against her own kin. It draws on earlier Germanic legend and later influenced works including Richard Wagner's Ring cycle.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Nibelungenlied) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Nibelungenlied) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Nibelungenlied)
- [ ] **The Saga of the Volsungs**: An Old Norse prose saga, composed around the thirteenth century, retelling the legend of the Volsung and Niflung families, including the hero Sigurd, the cursed treasure of the dragon Fafnir, and the downfall of the Burgundian kings. It parallels, and shares sources with, the Nibelungenlied.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Saga+of+the+Volsungs) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Saga+of+the+Volsungs) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Saga+of+the+Volsungs)
- [ ] **Caedmon's Hymn**: A short Old English poem of nine lines praising God as creator, attributed by Bede to the herdsman Caedmon in the seventh century. Bede's account describes Caedmon receiving the ability to compose religious verse in a vision. It is among the earliest attested pieces of English poetry.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Caedmon%27s+Hymn) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Caedmon%27s+Hymn) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Caedmon%27s+Hymn)
- [ ] **The Kalevala**: A Finnish epic compiled by Elias Lonnrot in the nineteenth century from Karelian and Finnish oral folk poetry, centering on the wizard-hero Vainamoinen and a magical artifact called the Sampo. It is regarded as the Finnish national epic and influenced later works, including music by Jean Sibelius.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Kalevala) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Kalevala) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kalevala)
- [ ] **Grettir's Saga (The Saga of Grettir the Strong)**: An Icelandic saga composed in the fourteenth century, recounting the life of the outlaw Grettir Asmundarson, including his fights against supernatural adversaries and his years as a fugitive. It is one of the later works in the Icelanders' sagas tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Grettir%27s+Saga) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Grettir%27s+Saga) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Grettir%27s+Saga)
- [ ] **The Vinland Sagas (The Greenlanders' Saga; Erik the Red's Saga)**: Two Icelandic sagas describing Norse voyages from Greenland to North America around 1000 AD. They are considered, alongside archaeological evidence at L'Anse aux Meadows, historical sources for pre-Columbian Norse contact with North America.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Vinland+Sagas+%28The+Greenlanders%27+Saga) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Vinland+Sagas+%28The+Greenlanders%27+Saga) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Vinland+Sagas+%28The+Greenlanders%27+Saga)
- [ ] **Hervarar Saga (The Saga of King Heidrek the Wise)**: A legendary Icelandic saga incorporating older heroic poetry, centering on a cursed sword named Tyrfing and including a section of riddle contests between King Heidrek and Odin in disguise. J.R.R. Tolkien, who edited and translated the saga, drew on its riddle contest when writing The Hobbit.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hervarar+Saga) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hervarar+Saga) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hervarar+Saga)
- [ ] **Widsith; Deor**: Two short Old English poems preserved in the Exeter Book. Widsith presents itself as the catalog of peoples and rulers visited by a legendary wandering minstrel; Deor is spoken by a poet recounting past misfortunes, each followed by the refrain "that passed away, so may this."
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Widsith) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Widsith) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Widsith)
- [ ] **The Poem of the Cid**: A Spanish epic poem probably composed in the mid-12th century, recounting the campaigns of Rodrigo Diaz de Vivar, known as El Cid, after his exile by King Alfonso VI of Castile. It describes his military successes, including the conquest of Valencia, and his eventual reconciliation with the king. It is one of the earliest and best-preserved works of medieval Spanish literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Poem+of+the+Cid) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Poem+of+the+Cid) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Poem+of+the+Cid)
- [ ] **Wolfram von Eschenbach — Parzival**: A Middle High German verse romance composed in the early 13th century, adapting and extending Chretien de Troyes's unfinished Perceval. It follows the knight Parzival from naive youth through failure to his eventual attainment of the Grail. It later served as the primary literary source for Richard Wagner's opera Parsifal.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Wolfram+von+Eschenbach+Parzival) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Wolfram+von+Eschenbach+Parzival) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Wolfram+von+Eschenbach+Parzival)
- [ ] **Gottfried von Strassburg — Tristan**: An unfinished Middle High German verse romance from the early 13th century, retelling the legend of Tristan and Isolde. It centers on the love potion that binds the two despite Isolde's marriage to King Mark of Cornwall. It is one of the principal medieval German treatments of the Tristan story.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gottfried+von+Strassburg+Tristan) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gottfried+von+Strassburg+Tristan) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gottfried+von+Strassburg+Tristan)
- [ ] **Anon. — The Quest of the Holy Grail**: A 13th-century French prose romance, part of the Vulgate (Lancelot-Grail) Cycle, concerning the Arthurian knights' pursuit of the Holy Grail. It emphasizes spiritual purity, and only Galahad, Percival, and Bors are shown achieving the Grail. It recasts the earlier chivalric romance tradition in explicitly religious terms.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Quest+of+the+Holy+Grail) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Quest+of+the+Holy+Grail) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Quest+of+the+Holy+Grail)
- [ ] **Giovanni Boccaccio — The Decameron**: A collection of 100 novellas in Italian prose, written around 1349-1353, framed as stories told by ten young Florentines sheltering outside the city during the Black Death. The tales range across comic, tragic, and erotic subjects drawn from varied sources. It influenced later European prose fiction, including Chaucer.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Giovanni+Boccaccio+Decameron) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Giovanni+Boccaccio+Decameron) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Giovanni+Boccaccio+Decameron)
- [ ] **Guillaume de Lorris & Jean de Meun — The Romance of the Rose**: A French allegorical poem in two parts. The first, by Guillaume de Lorris (c. 1230), presents a dream-vision of the pursuit of courtly love, personified as a rose; the second, by Jean de Meun (c. 1275), adds extensive philosophical, satirical, and encyclopedic digressions. It was widely read across medieval Europe and partly translated into English, in a version once attributed to Chaucer.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Guillaume+de+Lorris+%26+Jean+de+Meun+Romance+of+the+Rose) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Guillaume+de+Lorris+%26+Jean+de+Meun+Romance+of+the+Rose) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Guillaume+de+Lorris+%26+Jean+de+Meun+Romance+of+the+Rose)
- [ ] **Ludovico Ariosto — Orlando Furioso**: An Italian epic poem first published in 1516 and completed in its final form in 1532, continuing the narrative begun in Boiardo's Orlando Innamorato. It follows the knight Orlando's madness brought on by unrequited love for Angelica, alongside numerous interwoven episodes involving Charlemagne's paladins and their Saracen adversaries. It is written in ottava rima.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ludovico+Ariosto+Orlando+Furioso) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ludovico+Ariosto+Orlando+Furioso) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ludovico+Ariosto+Orlando+Furioso)
- [ ] **Torquato Tasso — Jerusalem Delivered**: An Italian epic poem published in 1581, presenting a fictionalized account of the First Crusade and the 1099 siege of Jerusalem. It combines historical military narrative with episodes of romance and supernatural enchantment. It is considered a major work of the later Italian Renaissance epic tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Torquato+Tasso+Jerusalem+Delivered) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Torquato+Tasso+Jerusalem+Delivered) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Torquato+Tasso+Jerusalem+Delivered)
- [ ] **Edmund Spenser — The Faerie Queene**: An English allegorical epic poem; its first three books were published in 1590 and further books in 1596, with the work left unfinished at Spenser's death. Each completed book follows a knight representing a particular virtue in service to the Faerie Queene, a figure generally understood to represent Queen Elizabeth I. It is written in a nine-line stanza form now called the Spenserian stanza.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Edmund+Spenser+Faerie+Queene) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Edmund+Spenser+Faerie+Queene) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Edmund+Spenser+Faerie+Queene)
- [ ] **Jean Froissart — Chronicles**: A French-language chronicle covering events in Western Europe, chiefly during the Hundred Years' War, from the 1320s into the early 15th century. Froissart drew on eyewitness testimony and his own travels among the nobility of England, France, and the Low Countries. The Chronicles are a major narrative source for the political and military history of the period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jean+Froissart+Chronicles) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jean+Froissart+Chronicles) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jean+Froissart+Chronicles)
- [ ] **Francois Villon — The Testament**: A poem written in 1461 by Francois Villon, a French poet whose recorded life included periods of crime and imprisonment. Framed as a mock last will and testament, it addresses mortality, poverty, and the passage of time, combining formal ballades with satirical and autobiographical passages. It is among the best-known works of later medieval French poetry.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Francois+Villon+Testament) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Francois+Villon+Testament) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Francois+Villon+Testament)
- [ ] **Marie de France — The Lais**: A collection of twelve narrative poems in Old French, composed in the late 12th century by a poet identified only as Marie, believed to have been active at the English court. The lais are short verse tales concerning love, often incorporating supernatural or Breton folkloric elements. Marie de France is one of the few named women writers of the medieval period.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Marie+de+France+Lais) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Marie+de+France+Lais) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marie+de+France+Lais)
- [ ] **Beroul & Thomas — The Romance of Tristan**: Two separate 12th-century Old French verse versions of the Tristan and Isolde legend, attributed respectively to Beroul and to a poet known as Thomas of Britain, both surviving only in fragments. They are among the earliest literary treatments of the Tristan story. They were drawn on by later versions, including Gottfried von Strassburg's Tristan.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Beroul+%26+Thomas+Romance+of+Tristan) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Beroul+%26+Thomas+Romance+of+Tristan) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Beroul+%26+Thomas+Romance+of+Tristan)
- [ ] **Amadis of Gaul**: A prose chivalric romance of Iberian origin, published in its best-known Spanish form by Garci Rodriguez de Montalvo in 1508, though earlier versions likely circulated before then. It follows the adventures of the knight Amadis and was widely popular across 16th-century Europe. It is referenced in Cervantes's Don Quixote as one of the chivalric romances read by the protagonist.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Amadis+of+Gaul) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Amadis+of+Gaul) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Amadis+of+Gaul)
- [ ] **Aucassin et Nicolette**: An anonymous French work of the late 12th or early 13th century, combining alternating passages of sung verse and spoken prose, a form known as a chantefable. It tells of the love between the nobleman Aucassin and Nicolette, an enslaved woman, and includes elements parodying conventions of contemporary romance. It survives in a single manuscript.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aucassin+et+Nicolette) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aucassin+et+Nicolette) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aucassin+et+Nicolette)
- [ ] **Lebor Gabala Erenn (The Book of Invasions)**: A medieval Irish compilation of prose and verse, surviving in several recensions from around the 11th century onward, presenting a pseudo-historical account of Ireland's successive legendary settlements. It traces the ancestry of the Irish to the Gaels, described as the last of the invading peoples to arrive. It combines native mythological material with elements adapted from biblical and classical history.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lebor+Gabala+Erenn) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lebor+Gabala+Erenn) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lebor+Gabala+Erenn)
- [ ] **The Ulster Cycle — Bricriu's Feast; The Destruction of Da Derga's Hostel; The Wooing of Etain**: A group of early Irish narratives centered on the warriors of King Conchobar mac Nessa and the hero Cu Chulainn. Bricriu's Feast concerns a contest over a champion's portion at a feast; The Destruction of Da Derga's Hostel recounts the death of King Conaire; The Wooing of Etain involves the otherworldly figure Midir. They survive chiefly in medieval manuscripts such as the Book of the Dun Cow.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Ulster+Cycle+Bricriu%27s+Feast) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Ulster+Cycle+Bricriu%27s+Feast) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Ulster+Cycle+Bricriu%27s+Feast)
- [ ] **The Mythological Cycle — Cath Maige Tuired**: A body of early Irish narrative concerning the Tuatha De Danann, a supernatural race said to have inhabited Ireland before the arrival of the Gaels. Cath Maige Tuired recounts their conflict with the Fomorians for control of Ireland. It survives in medieval manuscripts reflecting older traditional material.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Mythological+Cycle+Cath+Maige+Tuired) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Mythological+Cycle+Cath+Maige+Tuired) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Mythological+Cycle+Cath+Maige+Tuired)
- [ ] **The Fenian Cycle — Acallam na Senorach; The Pursuit of Diarmuid and Grainne**: A body of early Irish literature centered on the warrior Finn mac Cumaill and his band, the Fianna. Acallam na Senorach, composed around the 12th century, frames surviving members of the Fianna recounting earlier tales to St. Patrick. The Pursuit of Diarmuid and Grainne tells of the elopement of Grainne, betrothed to Finn, with the warrior Diarmuid.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Fenian+Cycle+Acallam+na+Senorach) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Fenian+Cycle+Acallam+na+Senorach) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Fenian+Cycle+Acallam+na+Senorach)
- [ ] **The Immrama — The Voyage of Bran; The Voyage of Mael Duin**: Early Irish narratives belonging to the immram, or "voyage," genre, describing sea journeys to supernatural otherworld islands. They are among the earliest surviving examples of the genre.

- [ ] **The Annals of the Four Masters; The Annals of Ulster**: Chronological compilations of Irish history. The Annals of the Four Masters were compiled in the 17th century from earlier sources, covering events from legendary antiquity to 1616. The Annals of Ulster cover a comparable span and were compiled from the 15th century onward using earlier annalistic material.

- [ ] **Senchas Mar (the Brehon law tracts)**: A collection of early Irish legal texts, compiled around the 8th century, forming part of the native Irish body of customary law known as Brehon law. It addresses areas including contract, status, and compensation, administered through mechanisms distinct from later feudal or common law systems. It is among the earliest vernacular law codes recorded in Europe.

- [ ] **Lebor na hUidre; The Book of Leinster**: Two principal medieval Irish manuscripts. Lebor na hUidre ("Book of the Dun Cow"), compiled around 1100, is the oldest surviving manuscript written wholly in Irish. The Book of Leinster dates from the 12th century. - [ ] **The Welsh Triads (Trioedd Ynys Prydein)**: A collection of medieval Welsh texts organizing traditional lore into groups of three related figures or events, used as a mnemonic device by poets and storytellers. - [ ] **Aneirin — Y Gododdin**: An early Welsh poem attributed to the poet Aneirin, commemorating warriors of the Gododdin kingdom killed at the Battle of Catraeth, traditionally dated to around 600 AD. - [ ] **The Book of Taliesin**: A 14th-century Welsh manuscript containing poems attributed to the 6th-century bard Taliesin, though scholars date much of the material to later centuries. The collection includes praise poetry alongside poems with mythological and riddling content, some employing a shape-shifting first-person voice. It is a primary source for early Welsh bardic poetry.

- [ ] **The Black Book of Carmarthen**: A Welsh manuscript dating to around the mid-13th century, considered the earliest surviving manuscript written wholly in Welsh. - [ ] **The Red Book of Hergest**: A large Welsh manuscript compiled in the late 14th or early 15th century, containing prose tales including the Mabinogion along with extensive poetry. - [ ] **St. Patrick — Confessio; Letter to Coroticus**: Two Latin texts generally accepted as the authentic writings of St. Patrick, the missionary credited with a central role in Ireland's conversion to Christianity, probably written in the 5th century. - [ ] **The Voyage of St. Brendan (Navigatio Sancti Brendani)**: A Latin narrative, probably composed in the 9th or 10th century, recounting a legendary sea voyage by the Irish abbot St. Brendan in search of a paradise called the Promised Land of the Saints. - [ ] **Nennius — Historia Brittonum**: A Latin compilation of British history and legend traditionally attributed to Nennius, dating to around the early 9th century. - [ ] **Buile Suibhne (The Frenzy of Sweeney)**: A medieval Irish prose and verse narrative, surviving in a manuscript of around the 17th century though reflecting an older story, telling of King Suibhne, who is cursed with madness and transformed into a bird-like wanderer after a battle. It has been the subject of modern translations and adaptations, including by Seamus Heaney and Flann O'Brien.

- [ ] **Early Irish Nature Lyrics (incl. "Pangur Ban")**: A body of short lyric poems composed by monastic scribes in medieval Ireland, often recorded in manuscript margins, addressing subjects such as hermit life, the natural world, and the seasons. - [ ] **Sanas Cormaic (Cormac's Glossary)**: An early Irish glossary traditionally attributed to Cormac mac Cuilennain, bishop-king of Munster, compiled around the 9th or 10th century. - [ ] **Dafydd ap Gwilym — Poems**: A Welsh poet active in the 14th century whose surviving corpus consists mainly of love poetry and poems addressing the natural world. - [ ] **Orosius — History Against the Pagans**: A Latin work by Paulus Orosius, written around 417-418 AD at the encouragement of Augustine of Hippo, presenting a universal history intended to argue that pre-Christian history included calamities comparable to those following Rome's decline. - [ ] **Einhard — The Life of Charlemagne**: A Latin biography of the emperor Charlemagne written by Einhard, a member of his court, in the 820s or 830s, modeled explicitly on Suetonius's Lives of the Caesars. - [ ] **The Anglo-Saxon Chronicle**: A set of related annalistic chronicles in Old English, originating during the reign of Alfred the Great in the late 9th century and continued at various monastic centers, with some versions extending into the 12th century. - [ ] **Gildas — On the Ruin of Britain**: A Latin text by the British monk Gildas, probably written in the 6th century, combining historical narrative with a polemical condemnation of contemporary British rulers, whom Gildas blames for inviting Saxon settlement and conquest. - [ ] **Jordanes — Getica**: A Latin history of the Goths written by Jordanes in the mid-6th century, based substantially on the lost Gothic history of Cassiodorus. - [ ] **Paul the Deacon — History of the Lombards**: A Latin history of the Lombards, a Germanic people who established a kingdom in Italy, written by Paul the Deacon in the late 8th century at the Carolingian court. - [ ] **William of Malmesbury — Deeds of the Kings of the English**: A Latin history of the kings of England from the 5th century to William's own time, written by the monk William of Malmesbury in the 1120s.
