   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Yamamoto+Tsunetomo+Hagakure) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Yamamoto+Tsunetomo+Hagakure) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Yamamoto+Tsunetomo+Hagakure) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)

**Synthesis.** Compare the Bhagavad Gita's teaching on action with the Stoics (Phase 3) and with the Hagakure.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Inazo Nitobe — Bushido: The Soul of Japan**: A book written in English and published in 1900 by the Japanese educator and diplomat Inazo Nitobe, intended to explain samurai ethical principles to a Western readership. It presents bushido as a code comparable to European chivalry, covering virtues such as loyalty, honor, and self-control. It was influential in shaping early 20th-century Western perceptions of Japanese culture.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Inazo+Nitobe+Bushido%3A+The+Soul+of+Japan) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Inazo+Nitobe+Bushido%3A+The+Soul+of+Japan) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Inazo+Nitobe+Bushido%3A+The+Soul+of+Japan) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **The Kojiki**: The oldest extant chronicle of Japan, compiled in 712 AD, recording mythological accounts of the creation of Japan and its gods alongside a genealogy of the early imperial line. It is a primary source for Shinto mythology and early Japanese history.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Kojiki) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Kojiki) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kojiki) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Omar Khayyam — The Rubaiyat**: A collection of quatrains (rubaiyat) attributed to the Persian polymath Omar Khayyam (1048-1131), addressing themes of mortality, fate, and the pleasures of the present moment. The attribution and authenticity of many individual verses are disputed among scholars. It became widely known in English through Edward FitzGerald's 1859 translation, which adapted rather than literally rendered the original Persian.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Omar+Khayyam+Rubaiyat) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Omar+Khayyam+Rubaiyat) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Omar+Khayyam+Rubaiyat) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **The Four Books — The Great Learning; The Doctrine of the Mean**: Two short texts, originally chapters of the Book of Rites, later compiled with the Analects and the Mencius into the "Four Books" of Confucianism by the Song-dynasty scholar Zhu Xi. They address self-cultivation, moral development, and the relationship between the individual and the well-ordered state. The Four Books formed the basis of the Chinese imperial civil service examination curriculum for several centuries.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Four+Books+Great+Learning) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Four+Books+Great+Learning) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Four+Books+Great+Learning) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Sei Shonagon — The Pillow Book**: A collection of observations, lists, and anecdotes written around 1000 AD by Sei Shonagon, a lady-in-waiting at the Japanese imperial court during the Heian period. It combines diary-like entries with categorized lists of impressions and preferences. It is considered an early example of the zuihitsu ("following the brush") genre of Japanese literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sei+Shonagon+Pillow+Book) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sei+Shonagon+Pillow+Book) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sei+Shonagon+Pillow+Book) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Kakuzo Okakura — The Book of Tea**: A short book written in English and published in 1906 by the Japanese scholar Kakuzo Okakura, explaining the aesthetic and philosophical principles behind the Japanese tea ceremony to Western readers. It discusses the influence of Zen Buddhism and Taoism on Japanese approaches to art and daily life. It was written partly in response to Western misunderstandings of Japanese culture at the time.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Kakuzo+Okakura+Book+of+Tea) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Kakuzo+Okakura+Book+of+Tea) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kakuzo+Okakura+Book+of+Tea) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Qu Yuan — The Songs of Chu (Li Sao)**: A collection of poems associated with the statesman and poet Qu Yuan (c. 340-278 BC) of the state of Chu, along with later additions by other poets. Qu Yuan is traditionally said to have drowned himself after being exiled on charges of disloyalty. The Dragon Boat Festival is traditionally linked to his death.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Qu+Yuan+Songs+of+Chu) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Qu+Yuan+Songs+of+Chu) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Qu+Yuan+Songs+of+Chu) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Tao Yuanming — Collected Poems**: The poetry of Tao Yuanming (also known as Tao Qian, 365-427 AD), a Chinese official who retired from government service to live as a farmer. His verse depicts rural life, wine, and contentment with a simple existence, often centered on imagery of chrysanthemums and the countryside. He is regarded as an early and influential figure in Chinese nature poetry.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tao+Yuanming+Collected+Poems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tao+Yuanming+Collected+Poems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tao+Yuanming+Collected+Poems) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Li Bai (Li Po) — Poems**: The poetry of Li Bai (701-762 AD), a Tang-dynasty poet known for verse on themes of nature, friendship, and wine. His work is associated with the high point of Tang-dynasty poetry and is often discussed alongside that of his contemporary Du Fu. Roughly a thousand of his poems survive.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Li+Bai+Poems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Li+Bai+Poems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Li+Bai+Poems) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Wang Wei — Poems**: The poetry of Wang Wei (699-759 AD), a Tang-dynasty poet, painter, and government official known for landscape poetry influenced by Buddhist contemplation. His verse frequently depicts natural scenes with an emphasis on stillness and quiet observation. He was also a noted landscape painter, and later critics linked the two aspects of his work.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Wang+Wei+Poems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Wang+Wei+Poems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Wang+Wei+Poems) · [Chinese Text Project](https://ctext.org/)
- [ ] **Adi Shankara — Crest-Jewel of Discrimination; the Upanishad commentaries**: Adi Shankara (traditionally dated to the 8th century AD) was a philosopher associated with the Advaita (non-dualist) school of Vedanta, holding that Brahman alone is ultimately real and that the perceived world of multiplicity is due to ignorance (avidya). He wrote commentaries on the Upanishads, the Brahma Sutras, and the Bhagavad Gita.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Adi+Shankara+Crest-Jewel+of+Discrimination) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Adi+Shankara+Crest-Jewel+of+Discrimination) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Adi+Shankara+Crest-Jewel+of+Discrimination)
- [ ] **The Laws of Manu (Manusmriti)**: A Sanskrit legal and religious text, compiled roughly between the 2nd century BC and 2nd century AD, addressing social duty (dharma) organized by caste (varna) and stage of life (ashrama), along with cosmology and ritual matters. It has been an influential, and in the modern period a debated, text in the history of Hindu law.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Laws+of+Manu) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Laws+of+Manu) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Laws+of+Manu)
- [ ] **Kautilya — The Arthashastra**: A Sanskrit treatise on statecraft, economics, and military strategy, traditionally attributed to Kautilya (also known as Chanakya), a minister associated with the Maurya court around the 4th century BC, though its final compilation may be later. It addresses administration, taxation, diplomacy, and espionage.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Kautilya+Arthashastra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Kautilya+Arthashastra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kautilya+Arthashastra)
- [ ] **Kalidasa — Shakuntala; The Cloud Messenger; The Birth of Kumara**: Kalidasa (active c. 4th-5th century AD) was a Sanskrit poet and dramatist. Shakuntala (Abhijnanashakuntalam) is a play recounting the love story of King Dushyanta and Shakuntala; The Cloud Messenger (Meghaduta) is a lyric poem in which an exiled figure sends a message via a cloud; The Birth of Kumara (Kumarasambhava) narrates the birth of the god Kartikeya.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Kalidasa+Shakuntala) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Kalidasa+Shakuntala) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kalidasa+Shakuntala)
- [ ] **The Panchatantra**: A Sanskrit collection of interlinked animal fables, compiled in its earliest known form around the 3rd century AD, intended to instruct princes in practical wisdom and statecraft. It was widely translated and adapted, including as the Arabic Kalila wa Dimna.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Panchatantra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Panchatantra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Panchatantra)
- [ ] **Bhartrhari — The Three Centuries (Shatakatraya)**: A collection of Sanskrit epigrams attributed to Bhartrhari (dates uncertain, possibly 5th century AD), traditionally divided into three sets of roughly one hundred verses each, on worldly conduct, love, and renunciation.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bhartrhari+Three+Centuries) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bhartrhari+Three+Centuries) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bhartrhari+Three+Centuries)
- [ ] **Jayadeva — The Gitagovinda**: A twelfth-century Sanskrit lyric poem by Jayadeva describing the love of the god Krishna and the cowherd Radha. It has been read both as erotic poetry and as religious allegory and has influenced later devotional literature, music, and dance traditions.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jayadeva+Gitagovinda) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jayadeva+Gitagovinda) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jayadeva+Gitagovinda)
- [ ] **Rabindranath Tagore — Gitanjali; The Home and the World; selected poems**: Tagore (1861-1941) was a Bengali poet, writer, and composer. Gitanjali ("Song Offerings") is a collection of devotional poems for which he received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1913, the first non-European recipient of the prize; The Home and the World is a novel addressing nationalism and personal relationships set during the Swadeshi movement.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Rabindranath+Tagore+Gitanjali) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Rabindranath+Tagore+Gitanjali) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Rabindranath+Tagore+Gitanjali)
- [ ] **Tiruvalluvar — Tirukkural**: A Tamil text of couplets, of uncertain date (estimates range from roughly the 1st century BC to the 5th century AD), traditionally attributed to Tiruvalluvar, addressing virtue (aram), wealth and governance (porul), and love (inbam).
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tiruvalluvar+Tirukkural) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tiruvalluvar+Tirukkural) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tiruvalluvar+Tirukkural)
- [ ] **Sudraka — The Little Clay Cart (Mrcchakatika)**: A Sanskrit drama traditionally attributed to a king or author named Sudraka, of uncertain date (estimates range from around the 2nd century BC to the 5th century AD), depicting the relationship between an impoverished merchant, Charudatta, and a courtesan, Vasantasena, against a backdrop of political intrigue.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sudraka+Little+Clay+Cart) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sudraka+Little+Clay+Cart) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sudraka+Little+Clay+Cart)
- [ ] **Tulsidas — Ramcharitmanas**: A sixteenth-century retelling of the Ramayana in Awadhi (a dialect of Hindi) by the poet Tulsidas, presenting the story of Rama within a devotional (bhakti) framework. It is widely read and performed in northern India, including in the annual Ramlila dramatic tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tulsidas+Ramcharitmanas) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tulsidas+Ramcharitmanas) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tulsidas+Ramcharitmanas)
- [ ] **Kabir — Poems (the Bijak; the dohas)**: Kabir (traditionally dated to around the 15th century) was a North Indian poet-saint associated with the bhakti and Sant traditions, whose verses, preserved in collections such as the Bijak, criticize ritual formalism in both Hindu and Muslim practice and emphasize direct devotion to a formless God (nirguna).
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Kabir+Poems+%28the+Bijak) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Kabir+Poems+%28the+Bijak) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kabir+Poems+%28the+Bijak)
- [ ] **Yagyu Munenori — The Life-Giving Sword (Heiho Kadensho)**: A treatise on swordsmanship written in 1632 by Yagyu Munenori, sword instructor to the Tokugawa shoguns. It discusses technique alongside Zen-influenced concepts of perception, timing, and the mental state of the swordsman. It is a foundational text of the Yagyu Shinkage-ryu school of swordsmanship.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Yagyu+Munenori+Life-Giving+Sword) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Yagyu+Munenori+Life-Giving+Sword) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Yagyu+Munenori+Life-Giving+Sword) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Yagyu+Munenori+Life-Giving+Sword)
- [ ] **Bodhidharma — The Two Entrances and Four Practices; The Bloodstream Sermon**: Texts attributed to Bodhidharma, a monk traditionally credited with transmitting Chan (Zen) Buddhism from India to China in the 5th or 6th century. They describe entering the Buddhist path through principle and through practice, emphasizing direct insight into one's nature over reliance on scripture. Bodhidharma is traditionally regarded as the first patriarch of Chan Buddhism and is associated with the Shaolin Monastery.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Bodhidharma+Two+Entrances+and+Four+Practices) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Bodhidharma+Two+Entrances+and+Four+Practices) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bodhidharma+Two+Entrances+and+Four+Practices) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Bodhidharma+Two+Entrances+and+Four+Practices)
- [ ] **Yuanwu — The Blue Cliff Record (Hekiganroku)**: A collection of one hundred koans with commentary, compiled in 12th-century China by the Chan master Yuanwu Keqin, based on earlier selections by Xuedou Zhongxian. It combines case narratives, verse commentary, and prose commentary. It is used as a teaching text in the Rinzai Zen tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Yuanwu+Blue+Cliff+Record) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Yuanwu+Blue+Cliff+Record) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Yuanwu+Blue+Cliff+Record) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Yuanwu+Blue+Cliff+Record)
- [ ] **Hakuin — Wild Ivy; the koan teachings**: Writings including the spiritual autobiography of Hakuin Ekaku, an 18th-century Japanese monk who revived the Rinzai school of Zen. He developed new koans, including the well-known "what is the sound of one hand clapping," and reorganized koan training. Wild Ivy recounts his meditative experiences and periods of illness and doubt.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hakuin+Wild+Ivy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hakuin+Wild+Ivy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hakuin+Wild+Ivy) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Hakuin+Wild+Ivy)
- [ ] **Eugen Herrigel — Zen in the Art of Archery**: A 1948 memoir by the German philosopher Eugen Herrigel describing his study of Japanese archery (kyūdō) under a Zen-influenced teacher in the 1920s. It describes the practice of shooting without deliberate aiming or self-conscious effort. It became a widely read introduction to Zen concepts for Western readers.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Eugen+Herrigel+Zen+in+the+Art+of+Archery) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Eugen+Herrigel+Zen+in+the+Art+of+Archery) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Eugen+Herrigel+Zen+in+the+Art+of+Archery) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Eugen+Herrigel+Zen+in+the+Art+of+Archery)
- [ ] **D.T. Suzuki — Zen and Japanese Culture**: A study by the scholar D.T. Suzuki examining the influence of Zen Buddhism on Japanese art forms, including swordsmanship, tea ceremony, haiku, and garden design. First published in expanded form in 1959. Suzuki's writings introduced Zen concepts to a wide English-speaking readership in the 20th century.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=D.T.+Suzuki+Zen+and+Japanese+Culture) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=D.T.+Suzuki+Zen+and+Japanese+Culture) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=D.T.+Suzuki+Zen+and+Japanese+Culture) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=D.T.+Suzuki+Zen+and+Japanese+Culture)
- [ ] **Shunryu Suzuki — Zen Mind, Beginner's Mind**: A 1970 collection of talks by Shunryū Suzuki, a Sōtō Zen priest who founded the San Francisco Zen Center. It presents basic Zen practice and attitudes toward meditation for a general audience, including the phrase "beginner's mind." It is widely used as an introductory text on Zen practice in the West.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Shunryu+Suzuki+Zen+Mind%2C+Beginner%27s+Mind) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Shunryu+Suzuki+Zen+Mind%2C+Beginner%27s+Mind) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Shunryu+Suzuki+Zen+Mind%2C+Beginner%27s+Mind) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Shunryu+Suzuki+Zen+Mind%2C+Beginner%27s+Mind)
- [ ] **The Lotus Sutra**: A Mahayana Buddhist sutra composed roughly between the 1st century BCE and 2nd century CE, teaching that all beings are capable of attaining buddhahood. It uses parables, including that of a father luring children from a burning house, to illustrate the Buddha's use of skillful means (upaya). It has been particularly influential in East Asian Buddhist traditions, including Tiantai and Nichiren Buddhism.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lotus+Sutra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lotus+Sutra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lotus+Sutra) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Lotus+Sutra)
- [ ] **The Vimalakirti Sutra**: A Mahayana sutra in which the layman Vimalakīrti debates Buddhist doctrine with bodhisattvas and disciples of the Buddha. A key passage has Vimalakīrti respond to a question about non-duality with silence. The text emphasizes that lay practitioners can attain deep spiritual realization.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Vimalakirti+Sutra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Vimalakirti+Sutra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Vimalakirti+Sutra) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Vimalakirti+Sutra)
- [ ] **The Lankavatara Sutra**: A Mahayana sutra addressing the doctrine that phenomena are manifestations of mind (cittamatra, or "mind-only"). It was influential in early Chinese Chan Buddhism and is associated with the transmission of Zen from Bodhidharma. It discusses perception, consciousness, and the nature of reality.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lankavatara+Sutra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lankavatara+Sutra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lankavatara+Sutra) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Lankavatara+Sutra)
- [ ] **The Sutta Nipata; The Udana**: Two texts included in the Khuddaka Nikaya of the Pali Canon, considered among its oldest strata. The Sutta Nipāta is a collection of verse discourses, and the Udāna is a collection of the Buddha's inspired utterances paired with narrative context. Both are associated with early Theravada Buddhist teaching.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sutta+Nipata) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sutta+Nipata) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sutta+Nipata) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Sutta+Nipata)
- [ ] **Buddhaghosa — The Visuddhimagga (The Path of Purification)**: A comprehensive manual of Theravada Buddhist doctrine and meditation practice, composed in Pali by the 5th-century scholar-monk Buddhaghosa in Sri Lanka. It organizes the path to liberation into stages of ethical conduct, concentration, and wisdom. It remains an authoritative reference text in the Theravada tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Buddhaghosa+Visuddhimagga) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Buddhaghosa+Visuddhimagga) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Buddhaghosa+Visuddhimagga) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Buddhaghosa+Visuddhimagga)
- [ ] **The Milindapanha (The Questions of King Milinda)**: A text presenting a dialogue between the Indo-Greek king Menander I (Milinda) and the Buddhist monk Nagasena, likely composed in the early centuries CE. It addresses questions of Buddhist doctrine, including the nature of the self and rebirth, using the analogy of a chariot to illustrate the concept of no-self (anatta). It is regarded as a significant text in Theravada Buddhist literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Milindapanha) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Milindapanha) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Milindapanha) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Milindapanha)
- [ ] **The Jataka Tales**: A collection of several hundred stories describing the previous lives of the Buddha, included in the Pali Canon's Khuddaka Nikaya. Each tale illustrates a moral or virtue associated with the bodhisattva path. The stories have also circulated widely as folk literature across South and Southeast Asia.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jataka+Tales) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jataka+Tales) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jataka+Tales) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Jataka+Tales)
- [ ] **The Tibetan Book of the Dead (Bardo Thodol)**: A Tibetan Buddhist text traditionally attributed to Padmasambhava and said to be discovered in the 14th century by Karma Lingpa, used as a guide for the dying and recently deceased. It describes the intermediate state (bardo) between death and rebirth and practices for recognizing the nature of mind during this period. It was first translated into English in 1927 by Walter Evans-Wentz.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tibetan+Book+of+the+Dead) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tibetan+Book+of+the+Dead) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tibetan+Book+of+the+Dead) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Tibetan+Book+of+the+Dead)
- [ ] **The Life of Milarepa; The Hundred Thousand Songs**: Texts recounting the life and teachings of Milarepa, an 11th-12th century Tibetan yogi of the Kagyu lineage. The biography describes his early practice of sorcery, subsequent remorse, and ascetic training under his teacher Marpa; the songs record his spontaneous verse teachings. He is a widely known figure in Tibetan Buddhist literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Life+of+Milarepa) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Life+of+Milarepa) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Life+of+Milarepa) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Life+of+Milarepa)
- [ ] **Gampopa — The Jewel Ornament of Liberation**: A systematic exposition of the Buddhist path composed by Gampopa, a 12th-century disciple of Milarepa who integrated monastic and yogic traditions. It presents stages of practice from initial motivation through to buddhahood. It is a foundational text of the Kagyu school of Tibetan Buddhism.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gampopa+Jewel+Ornament+of+Liberation) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gampopa+Jewel+Ornament+of+Liberation) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gampopa+Jewel+Ornament+of+Liberation) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Gampopa+Jewel+Ornament+of+Liberation)
- [ ] **Patrul Rinpoche — The Words of My Perfect Teacher**: A 19th-century manual of the preliminary practices (ngöndro) of the Nyingma school of Tibetan Buddhism, written by Patrul Rinpoche. It combines doctrinal instruction with stories and anecdotes. It remains widely used as an introductory text in Nyingma and Dzogchen practice.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Patrul+Rinpoche+Words+of+My+Perfect+Teacher) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Patrul+Rinpoche+Words+of+My+Perfect+Teacher) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Patrul+Rinpoche+Words+of+My+Perfect+Teacher) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Patrul+Rinpoche+Words+of+My+Perfect+Teacher)
- [ ] **Tsongkhapa — The Great Treatise on the Stages of the Path (Lamrim Chenmo)**: A systematic presentation of the Buddhist path to enlightenment composed in 1402 by Tsongkhapa, founder of the Gelug school of Tibetan Buddhism. It organizes teachings into a graduated sequence of stages (lamrim) drawing on Indian Buddhist sources. The Gelug school later became associated with the lineage of the Dalai Lamas.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tsongkhapa+Great+Treatise+on+the+Stages+of+the+Path) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tsongkhapa+Great+Treatise+on+the+Stages+of+the+Path) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tsongkhapa+Great+Treatise+on+the+Stages+of+the+Path) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Tsongkhapa+Great+Treatise+on+the+Stages+of+the+Path)
- [ ] **Ashvaghosha — Buddhacarita (The Life of the Buddha)**: An epic poem in Sanskrit recounting the life of the Buddha, composed by the poet Aśvaghoṣa in the 1st or 2nd century CE. It follows Siddhartha Gautama from his birth and sheltered upbringing through renunciation, enlightenment, and teaching. It is among the earliest surviving complete biographies of the Buddha in Sanskrit literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ashvaghosha+Buddhacarita) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ashvaghosha+Buddhacarita) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ashvaghosha+Buddhacarita) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Ashvaghosha+Buddhacarita)
- [ ] **The Avatamsaka (Flower Garland) Sutra**: A large Mahayana Buddhist sutra, likely compiled in Central Asia or China by the 4th century CE from earlier component texts. It presents a vision of interpenetrating reality, illustrated by the image of Indra's net, in which each element reflects all others. It became a foundational text of the Huayan school of Chinese Buddhism.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Avatamsaka+Sutra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Avatamsaka+Sutra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Avatamsaka+Sutra) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Avatamsaka+Sutra)
- [ ] **The Surangama Sutra**: A Mahayana Buddhist sutra addressing the nature of mind, perception, and meditative attainment, of disputed origin and possibly composed in China. It discusses various meditative states and the causes of delusion. It was studied within Chan (Zen) Buddhist circles for its treatment of consciousness.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Surangama+Sutra) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Surangama+Sutra) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Surangama+Sutra) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Surangama+Sutra)
- [ ] **The Record of Linji (Rinzai Roku)**: A collection of sayings and sermons attributed to Linji Yixuan, a 9th-century Chinese Chan master and founder of what became known as the Linji (Rinzai) school. It is known for its direct, iconoclastic teaching style, including the instruction to "kill the Buddha" if one is encountered as an obstacle. It is regarded as a foundational text of the Rinzai Zen tradition.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Record+of+Linji) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Record+of+Linji) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Record+of+Linji) · [SuttaCentral](https://www.suttacentral.net/search?query=Record+of+Linji)

<a id="phase-viii"></a>

## Phase VIII: Renaissance and the New Science

*14th to 17th c.. About 251 hours of core reading.*

The recovery of ancient texts (including the Hermetic writings, then believed older than Moses), the new political realism, the essay and the stage, and the reordering of the cosmos by Copernicus, Kepler, Galileo, and Newton. The phase ends with the philosophers who tried to rebuild knowledge on new foundations.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. What did Renaissance thinkers believe they were reviving?
2. Is Machiavelli describing politics or recommending it?
3. What changed in how people knew things between Aquinas and Descartes?
4. How do Hobbes and Locke differ on the state of nature?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Petrarch — Canzoniere** (~5 h)
   - Read: The sonnet sequence that set the European lyric. A selection is enough.
   - Edition: Tr. Mark Musa (Indiana) or J.G. Nichols (Carcanet)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Petrarch+Canzoniere) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Petrarch+Canzoniere) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Petrarch+Canzoniere)
2. [ ] **Hermes Trismegistus — The Corpus Hermeticum; The Emerald Tablet; Asclepius** (~6 h)
   - Read: Corpus Hermeticum I (Poimandres) and XIII; the Emerald Tablet. Ficino translated them in 1463.
   - Edition: Tr. Brian Copenhaver, Hermetica (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Divine+Pymander) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hermes+Trismegistus+Corpus+Hermeticum) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hermes+Trismegistus+Corpus+Hermeticum) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hermes+Trismegistus+Corpus+Hermeticum) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
3. [ ] **Pico della Mirandola — Oration on the Dignity of Man; the 900 Theses** (~3 h)
   - Read: The Oration: the human being as the creature with no fixed place.
   - Edition: Ed. Borghesi, Papio and Riva, Oration on the Dignity of Man: A New Translation and Commentary (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pico+Oration+Dignity) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pico+della+Mirandola+Oration+on+the+Dignity+of+Man) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pico+della+Mirandola+Oration+on+the+Dignity+of+Man) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pico+della+Mirandola+Oration+on+the+Dignity+of+Man) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
4. [ ] **Niccolo Machiavelli — The Prince; Discourses on Livy** (~15 h)
   - Read: The Prince, then Discourses Book I. The Discourses show his republicanism.
   - Edition: The Prince tr. Harvey Mansfield (Chicago); Discourses tr. Mansfield and Tarcov (Chicago)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Machiavelli+Prince) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Niccolo+Machiavelli+Prince) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Niccolo+Machiavelli+Prince) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Niccolo+Machiavelli+Prince)
5. [ ] **Thomas More — Utopia** (~4 h)
   - Read: Book I (the debate on counsel) matters as much as Book II (the island).
   - Edition: Ed. George Logan and Robert Adams (Cambridge Texts in the History of Political Thought)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=More+Utopia) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Thomas+More+Utopia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Thomas+More+Utopia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Thomas+More+Utopia)
6. [ ] **Michel de Montaigne — Essays** (~25 h)
   - Read: 'That to philosophize is to learn to die', 'Of cannibals', 'Of experience', and the 'Apology for Raymond Sebond'.
   - Edition: Tr. M.A. Screech, The Complete Essays (Penguin); or Donald Frame (Stanford)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Montaigne+Essays+Cotton) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Michel+de+Montaigne+Essays) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Michel+de+Montaigne+Essays) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Michel+de+Montaigne+Essays)
7. [ ] **Miguel de Cervantes — Don Quixote** (~35 h)
   - Read: Both parts. Part II (1615) responds to Part I's readers and to a spurious sequel.
   - Edition: Tr. Edith Grossman (Ecco)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Don+Quixote+Ormsby) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Miguel+de+Cervantes+Don+Quixote) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Miguel+de+Cervantes+Don+Quixote) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Miguel+de+Cervantes+Don+Quixote)
8. [ ] **William Shakespeare — The Tragedies and Histories** (~40 h)
   - Read: Richard II, Henry IV Parts 1-2, Henry V; then Hamlet, Othello, King Lear, Macbeth.
   - Edition: The Arden Shakespeare, Folger Shakespeare Library, or Oxford World's Classics single volumes; Folger texts are free online
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Shakespeare) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=William+Shakespeare+Tragedies+and+Histories) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=William+Shakespeare+Tragedies+and+Histories) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+Shakespeare+Tragedies+and+Histories)
9. [ ] **Giordano Bruno — On the Infinite Universe and Worlds; The Ash Wednesday Supper** (~5 h)
   - Read: An infinite universe with innumerable worlds. Burned in Rome in 1600.
   - Edition: Cause, Principle and Unity, tr. Robert de Lucca (Cambridge); The Ash Wednesday Supper, tr. Gosselin and Lerner (Toronto); On the Infinite Universe in Dorothea Waley Singer, Giordano Bruno: His Life and Thought (Schuman)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Giordano+Bruno+On+the+Infinite+Universe+and+Worlds) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Giordano+Bruno+On+the+Infinite+Universe+and+Worlds) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Giordano+Bruno+On+the+Infinite+Universe+and+Worlds) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
10. [ ] **Nicolaus Copernicus — On the Revolutions of the Heavenly Spheres** (~4 h)
   - Read: The dedication to Pope Paul III and Book I.
   - Edition: Tr. Edward Rosen, On the Revolutions (Johns Hopkins)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Nicolaus+Copernicus+On+the+Revolutions+of+the+Heavenly+Spheres) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Nicolaus+Copernicus+On+the+Revolutions+of+the+Heavenly+Spheres) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Nicolaus+Copernicus+On+the+Revolutions+of+the+Heavenly+Spheres)
11. [ ] **Johannes Kepler — Astronomia Nova; Harmonices Mundi** (~4 h)
   - Read: Selections on the ellipse (Astronomia Nova) and the third law (Harmonices Mundi V).
   - Edition: New Astronomy tr. William Donahue (Cambridge); The Harmony of the World tr. Aiton, Duncan and Field (American Philosophical Society)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Johannes+Kepler+Astronomia+Nova) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Johannes+Kepler+Astronomia+Nova) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Johannes+Kepler+Astronomia+Nova)
12. [ ] **Galileo Galilei — Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems** (~12 h)
   - Read: The First and Second Days.
   - Edition: Tr. Stillman Drake, Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems (Modern Library)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Galileo+Galilei+Dialogue+Concerning+the+Two+Chief+World+Systems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Galileo+Galilei+Dialogue+Concerning+the+Two+Chief+World+Systems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Galileo+Galilei+Dialogue+Concerning+the+Two+Chief+World+Systems)
13. [ ] **Francis Bacon — Novum Organum; Essays** (~8 h)
   - Read: Novum Organum Book I (the four idols) and a handful of the Essays.
   - Edition: The New Organon, ed. Jardine and Silverthorne (Cambridge); Essays (Oxford World's Classics / Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Bacon+Novum+Organum) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Francis+Bacon+Novum+Organum) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Francis+Bacon+Novum+Organum) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Francis+Bacon+Novum+Organum)
14. [ ] **Rene Descartes — Meditations on First Philosophy; Discourse on Method** (~6 h)
   - Read: Discourse on Method, then all six Meditations.
   - Edition: Tr. John Cottingham, Meditations on First Philosophy (Cambridge); Discourse on Method tr. Donald Cress (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Descartes+Meditations) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Rene+Descartes+Meditations+on+First+Philosophy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Rene+Descartes+Meditations+on+First+Philosophy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Rene+Descartes+Meditations+on+First+Philosophy)
15. [ ] **Thomas Hobbes — Leviathan** (~20 h)
   - Read: Parts I and II. Chapter 13 on the state of nature.
   - Edition: Ed. Edwin Curley (Hackett); or Richard Tuck (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hobbes+Leviathan) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Thomas+Hobbes+Leviathan) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Thomas+Hobbes+Leviathan) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Thomas+Hobbes+Leviathan)
16. [ ] **Blaise Pascal — Pensees** (~8 h)
   - Read: The wager (Lafuma 418), the two infinities, and the 'thinking reed'.
   - Edition: Tr. A.J. Krailsheimer, Pensees (Penguin), Lafuma numbering
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pascal+Pensees) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Blaise+Pascal+Pensees) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Blaise+Pascal+Pensees) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Blaise+Pascal+Pensees)
17. [ ] **John Milton — Paradise Lost** (~15 h)
   - Read: Books I-IV and IX-XII. Compare the epic invocation with Homer and Virgil.
   - Edition: Ed. Gordon Teskey (Norton Critical Edition); or John Leonard (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Milton+Paradise+Lost) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+Milton+Paradise+Lost) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+Milton+Paradise+Lost) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+Milton+Paradise+Lost)
18. [ ] **Baruch Spinoza — Ethics** (~15 h)
   - Read: Parts I and V with a guide; the geometric method from Phase 1 applied to God and ethics.
   - Edition: A Spinoza Reader, tr. Edwin Curley (Princeton)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Spinoza+Ethics) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Baruch+Spinoza+Ethics) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Baruch+Spinoza+Ethics) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Baruch+Spinoza+Ethics)
19. [ ] **Isaac Newton — The Principia** (~6 h)
   - Read: Definitions and laws of motion, the Rules of Reasoning at the start of Book III, and the General Scholium.
   - Edition: Tr. I. Bernard Cohen and Anne Whitman, The Principia (California)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Newton+Principia) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Isaac+Newton+Principia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Isaac+Newton+Principia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Isaac+Newton+Principia)
20. [ ] **John Locke — An Essay Concerning Human Understanding; Two Treatises of Government** (~15 h)
   - Read: Second Treatise of Government in full; Essay Book II on ideas.
   - Edition: Two Treatises ed. Peter Laslett (Cambridge); Essay ed. Roger Woolhouse (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Locke+Second+Treatise) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+Locke+An+Essay+Concerning+Human+Understanding) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+Locke+An+Essay+Concerning+Human+Understanding) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+Locke+An+Essay+Concerning+Human+Understanding)

**Synthesis.** Choose one work from Phase 2 and follow its revival and transformation in this phase.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Plato — Republic; Laws**: Plato's Republic (4th century BC) constructs an ideal city ruled by philosopher-kings as an analogy for the structure of a just soul. The Laws, a later dialogue, proposes a detailed legal code as the basis of government rather than rule by an individual sage. Both works address questions of justice, education, and the organization of the state.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Plato+Republic) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Plato+Republic) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Plato+Republic)
- [ ] **Aristotle — Politics; The Constitution of Athens**: Aristotle's Politics (4th century BC) classifies forms of government by who holds power and in whose interest it is exercised, and describes humans as naturally social or "political" beings. The Constitution of Athens describes the historical development and structure of the Athenian government. Aristotle favored a mixed constitution with a substantial middle class.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aristotle+Politics) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aristotle+Politics) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aristotle+Politics)
- [ ] **Cicero — On the Republic; On the Laws**: Dialogues by the Roman statesman and philosopher (106-43 BC), modeled on Plato's works, discussing the ideal mixed constitution and the basis of law in nature and reason. Cicero argues that true law is grounded in reason common to gods and humans, rather than in the decrees of rulers. Portions of both texts survive incompletely.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Cicero+On+the+Republic) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Cicero+On+the+Republic) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Cicero+On+the+Republic)
- [ ] **Augustine — The City of God**: A theological and philosophical work composed by Augustine of Hippo in the early 5th century AD, following the sack of Rome by the Visigoths in 410. It contrasts the "earthly city," organized around self-interest, with the "City of God," organized around love of God, and addresses the relationship between Christianity and Roman history. It spans twenty-two books.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Augustine+City+of+God) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Augustine+City+of+God) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Augustine+City+of+God)
- [ ] **Justinian — The Institutes (Corpus Juris Civilis)**: A legal textbook compiled under the Byzantine emperor Justinian I and issued in 533 AD, part of the larger Corpus Juris Civilis codification of Roman law. It was intended as an introductory manual for law students. Rediscovered and studied intensively in medieval Europe, it became a basis for civil law systems in much of continental Europe.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Justinian+Institutes) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Justinian+Institutes) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Justinian+Institutes)
- [ ] **John of Salisbury — Policraticus**: A 12th-century treatise by an English scholar and bishop, presenting the state as a body politic with the ruler as its head. It discusses the duties of rulers and officials and includes a discussion of the conditions under which resistance to a tyrannical ruler may be justified. It is among the earliest medieval works of systematic political theory.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+of+Salisbury+Policraticus) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+of+Salisbury+Policraticus) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+of+Salisbury+Policraticus)
- [ ] **Marsilius of Padua — Defensor Pacis**: A political treatise written in 1324 arguing that legitimate political authority derives from the people rather than from ecclesiastical office, and that the Church should not hold coercive temporal power. It was condemned by Pope John XXII shortly after its circulation. Historians frequently cite it as an early statement of ideas associated with popular sovereignty.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Marsilius+of+Padua+Defensor+Pacis) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Marsilius+of+Padua+Defensor+Pacis) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marsilius+of+Padua+Defensor+Pacis)
- [ ] **Jean Bodin — Six Books of the Commonwealth**: A political treatise published in 1576 by the French jurist Jean Bodin, written during the French Wars of Religion. It develops the concept of sovereignty as the supreme, indivisible authority within a state. The work influenced later theories of the modern nation-state.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jean+Bodin+Six+Books+of+the+Commonwealth) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jean+Bodin+Six+Books+of+the+Commonwealth) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jean+Bodin+Six+Books+of+the+Commonwealth)
- [ ] **Hugo Grotius — On the Law of War and Peace**: A legal and philosophical treatise published in 1625 by the Dutch jurist Hugo Grotius. It argues for a natural law governing relations between states, including standards for the conduct of war, applicable independently of any single nation's laws. The work is regarded as a foundational text of international law.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hugo+Grotius+On+the+Law+of+War+and+Peace) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hugo+Grotius+On+the+Law+of+War+and+Peace) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hugo+Grotius+On+the+Law+of+War+and+Peace)
- [ ] **The Chaldean Oracles**: A collection of fragmentary verse attributed to 2nd-century figures Julian the Chaldean and Julian the Theurgist, surviving only through quotations in later Neoplatonist writings. The fragments describe cosmological and ritual teachings associated with theurgy, a practice aimed at union with the divine. They were studied by later Neoplatonist philosophers including Proclus.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Chaldean+Oracles) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Chaldean+Oracles) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Chaldean+Oracles) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Orphic Hymns**: A collection of eighty-seven short hymns to various deities, attributed to the mythical poet Orpheus and likely composed in the 2nd or 3rd century CE, possibly for use in ritual contexts. They are associated with Orphism, a set of religious beliefs and practices in ancient Greece concerning the soul and the afterlife. They survive as part of a broader corpus of Orphic literature.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Orphic+Hymns) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Orphic+Hymns) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Orphic+Hymns) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Iamblichus — On the Mysteries**: A treatise by the Neoplatonist philosopher Iamblichus, written in the early 4th century CE, defending theurgy — ritual practice aimed at communion with the divine — as necessary alongside philosophical contemplation. It responds to a letter attributed to Porphyry questioning such practices. It is a primary source for the study of late antique theurgic ritual.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Iamblichus+On+the+Mysteries) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Iamblichus+On+the+Mysteries) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Iamblichus+On+the+Mysteries) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Proclus — The Elements of Theology; Platonic Theology**: Works by Proclus, a 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who headed the Platonic Academy in Athens. The Elements of Theology presents Neoplatonist metaphysics in a series of logically ordered propositions describing reality as emanating from and returning to a single first principle, the One. His systematic approach influenced later Christian, Islamic, and Jewish philosophical and mystical thought.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Proclus+Elements+of+Theology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Proclus+Elements+of+Theology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Proclus+Elements+of+Theology) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Porphyry — On the Cave of the Nymphs; Letter to Anebo**: Works by Porphyry, a 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Plotinus. On the Cave of the Nymphs offers an allegorical reading of a passage in Homer's Odyssey concerning the soul's descent into the material world. The Letter to Anebo raises critical questions about Egyptian ritual and theurgic practice, prompting Iamblichus's response in On the Mysteries.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Porphyry+On+the+Cave+of+the+Nymphs) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Porphyry+On+the+Cave+of+the+Nymphs) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Porphyry+On+the+Cave+of+the+Nymphs) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Nag Hammadi Library — incl. The Gospel of Thomas; Pistis Sophia**: A collection of Gnostic and related texts discovered near Nag Hammadi, Egypt, in 1945, dating from roughly the 2nd to 4th centuries CE. The texts include the Gospel of Thomas, a collection of sayings attributed to Jesus, and Pistis Sophia, a later Gnostic treatise. They reflect a range of early Christian and Gnostic teachings emphasizing salvation through esoteric knowledge (gnosis).
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=The+Nag+Hammadi+Library+incl.+The+Gospel+of+Thomas) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=The+Nag+Hammadi+Library+incl.+The+Gospel+of+Thomas) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=The+Nag+Hammadi+Library+incl.+The+Gospel+of+Thomas) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Zosimos of Panopolis — On the Apparatus and Furnaces**: Writings by Zosimos of Panopolis, a Greco-Egyptian alchemist active around 300 CE, among the earliest known alchemical authors. His texts combine descriptions of chemical apparatus and processes with visionary and allegorical material relating transmutation to spiritual transformation. His work is a primary source for the early history of alchemy.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Zosimos+of+Panopolis+On+the+Apparatus+and+Furnaces) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Zosimos+of+Panopolis+On+the+Apparatus+and+Furnaces) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Zosimos+of+Panopolis+On+the+Apparatus+and+Furnaces) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Sefer Yetzirah (The Book of Formation)**: An early Jewish mystical text of uncertain date, variously placed between the 2nd and 6th centuries CE, describing the creation of the world through combinations of the ten sefirot and the twenty-two letters of the Hebrew alphabet. It is among the earliest texts associated with the Kabbalistic tradition. It has been the subject of extensive later commentary. (Cross-listed in Book 18.)
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sefer+Yetzirah) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sefer+Yetzirah) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sefer+Yetzirah) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Zohar (The Book of Splendor)**: The central text of Kabbalah, a mystical commentary on the Torah written primarily in Aramaic, largely attributed to the 13th-century Spanish writer Moses de León though presented as the teaching of the 2nd-century sage Shimon bar Yochai. It discusses the sefirot, divine emanations through which the infinite (Ein Sof) relates to creation, and the mystical significance of scripture. It has been foundational to later Kabbalistic thought. (Cross-listed in Book 18.)
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Zohar) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Zohar) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Zohar) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Picatrix (Ghayat al-Hakim)**: A treatise on astral magic and talismans, originally composed in Arabic, likely in the 10th or 11th century, and later translated into Latin as the Picatrix. It describes methods for drawing on planetary influences through talismans, ritual, and astrology. It was influential in the transmission of Arabic magical and astrological texts to medieval and Renaissance Europe.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Picatrix) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Picatrix) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Picatrix) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Marsilio Ficino — Three Books on Life; the Hermetica translations**: Works by Marsilio Ficino, a 15th-century Florentine philosopher who translated Plato's dialogues and the Corpus Hermeticum into Latin. Three Books on Life addresses the health of scholars, combining medical, astrological, and magical recommendations. Ficino's translations were central to the revival of Platonic and Hermetic philosophy in Renaissance Italy.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Marsilio+Ficino+Three+Books+on+Life) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Marsilio+Ficino+Three+Books+on+Life) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marsilio+Ficino+Three+Books+on+Life) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa — Three Books of Occult Philosophy**: A systematic treatise on magic by the German scholar Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, published in 1533. It organizes magical practice into three domains — natural, celestial, and ceremonial — drawing on classical, Hermetic, and Kabbalistic sources. It became one of the most widely referenced texts of Renaissance occult philosophy.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Heinrich+Cornelius+Agrippa+Three+Books+of+Occult+Philosophy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Heinrich+Cornelius+Agrippa+Three+Books+of+Occult+Philosophy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Heinrich+Cornelius+Agrippa+Three+Books+of+Occult+Philosophy) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Paracelsus — Selected Writings**: Writings of Paracelsus, a 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist who challenged the prevailing Galenic medical tradition in favor of chemical treatment and direct observation. He developed the concept of the human body as a microcosm corresponding to the wider natural world. His work influenced the development of both toxicology and iatrochemistry, the application of chemistry to medicine.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Paracelsus+Selected+Writings) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Paracelsus+Selected+Writings) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Paracelsus+Selected+Writings) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Robert Fludd — Utriusque Cosmi Historia**: An illustrated treatise by the English physician and Rosicrucian sympathizer Robert Fludd, published in the 1610s-1620s, presenting a detailed correspondence between the macrocosm (the universe) and the microcosm (the human being). It draws on Hermetic, alchemical, and musical theory. Its engraved diagrams are frequently reproduced as examples of early modern esoteric illustration.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Robert+Fludd+Utriusque+Cosmi+Historia) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Robert+Fludd+Utriusque+Cosmi+Historia) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Robert+Fludd+Utriusque+Cosmi+Historia) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Rosicrucian Manifestos (Fama Fraternitatis; Confessio Fraternitatis)**: Anonymous pamphlets published in Germany in 1614 and 1615, announcing the existence of a secret brotherhood dedicated to spiritual and intellectual reform, associated with the legendary figure Christian Rosenkreuz. Their publication generated widespread discussion across early 17th-century Europe. No organized Rosicrucian brotherhood has been historically confirmed to have existed prior to the manifestos.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Rosicrucian+Manifestos+%28Fama+Fraternitatis) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Rosicrucian+Manifestos+%28Fama+Fraternitatis) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Rosicrucian+Manifestos+%28Fama+Fraternitatis) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **The Key of Solomon (Clavicula Salomonis)**: A grimoire, or magical text, of uncertain authorship, circulating in various manuscript versions from the medieval and Renaissance periods, traditionally attributed to King Solomon. It contains instructions, seals, and rituals for invoking and commanding spirits. It is among the most widely copied Western ceremonial magic texts.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Key+of+Solomon) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Key+of+Solomon) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Key+of+Solomon) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Jakob Bohme — Aurora; The Signature of All Things**: Works by Jakob Böhme, a German shoemaker and mystic writing in the early 17th century. Aurora, his first work, describes a theological vision of God's self-revelation through the interplay of opposing forces. His writings influenced later German idealist philosophy and Christian mystical traditions, including Romanticism.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jakob+Bohme+Aurora) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jakob+Bohme+Aurora) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jakob+Bohme+Aurora) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)
- [ ] **Emanuel Swedenborg — Heaven and Hell**: A 1758 work by the Swedish scientist and theologian Emanuel Swedenborg, describing his accounts of the afterlife based on visionary experiences he reported having from the 1740s onward. It presents a doctrine of "correspondences" linking the natural and spiritual worlds. His writings influenced later figures including William Blake and the Swedenborgian church movement.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Emanuel+Swedenborg+Heaven+and+Hell) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Emanuel+Swedenborg+Heaven+and+Hell) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Emanuel+Swedenborg+Heaven+and+Hell) · [ToposText](https://topostext.org/texts)

<a id="phase-ix"></a>

## Phase IX: Enlightenment to the Twentieth Century

*18th to 20th c.. About 335 hours of core reading.*

The critique of reason and its limits, the political revolutions and their critics, evolution, the unconscious, and the physics of the twentieth century. The phase ends with the mid-century thinkers who wrote after the world wars.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. What does Kant mean by a Copernican revolution in philosophy?
2. Burke and Tocqueville both study revolution. What does each fear?
3. What does Nietzsche mean by the death of God, and who else in this phase is answering the same problem?
4. How did Darwin and the new physics change the questions philosophers asked?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz — Monadology** (~2 h)
   - Read: Ninety short sections; the best possible world.
   - Edition: In Philosophical Essays, tr. Ariew and Garber (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Leibniz+Monadology) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gottfried+Wilhelm+Leibniz+Monadology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gottfried+Wilhelm+Leibniz+Monadology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gottfried+Wilhelm+Leibniz+Monadology)
2. [ ] **Giambattista Vico — The New Science** (~12 h)
   - Read: The Elements (axioms) and the three ages of gods, heroes, and men.
   - Edition: Tr. Bergin and Fisch (Cornell); or David Marsh (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Giambattista+Vico+New+Science) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Giambattista+Vico+New+Science) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Giambattista+Vico+New+Science)
3. [ ] **David Hume — A Treatise of Human Nature; Enquiry Concerning Human Understanding** (~12 h)
   - Read: Enquiry Concerning Human Understanding in full; Treatise Book I Part IV on personal identity.
   - Edition: Enquiry ed. Tom Beauchamp (Oxford); Treatise ed. Norton and Norton (Oxford)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hume+Enquiry+Human+Understanding) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=David+Hume+A+Treatise+of+Human+Nature) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=David+Hume+A+Treatise+of+Human+Nature) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=David+Hume+A+Treatise+of+Human+Nature)
4. [ ] **Montesquieu — The Spirit of the Laws** (~12 h)
   - Read: Books I-VIII on the three forms of government and Book XI on the separation of powers.
   - Edition: Tr. Cohler, Miller and Stone (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Montesquieu+Spirit+of+Laws) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Montesquieu+Spirit+of+the+Laws) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Montesquieu+Spirit+of+the+Laws) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Montesquieu+Spirit+of+the+Laws)
5. [ ] **Voltaire — Candide; Philosophical Dictionary** (~4 h)
   - Read: Candide, written in answer to Leibniz.
   - Edition: Candide tr. Theo Cuffe (Penguin) or Robert M. Adams (Norton)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Voltaire+Candide) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Voltaire+Candide) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Voltaire+Candide) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Voltaire+Candide)
6. [ ] **Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Social Contract; Emile; Confessions** (~12 h)
   - Read: The Social Contract; the first books of Emile and the Confessions.
   - Edition: The Social Contract and Other Later Political Writings, ed. Victor Gourevitch (Cambridge); Emile tr. Allan Bloom (Basic Books)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Rousseau+Social+Contract) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jean-Jacques+Rousseau+Social+Contract) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jean-Jacques+Rousseau+Social+Contract) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jean-Jacques+Rousseau+Social+Contract)
7. [ ] **Adam Smith — The Theory of Moral Sentiments; The Wealth of Nations** (~20 h)
   - Read: Theory of Moral Sentiments Part I first, then Wealth of Nations Book I.
   - Edition: Theory of Moral Sentiments (Penguin / Liberty Fund); Wealth of Nations (Oxford World's Classics / Liberty Fund)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Adam+Smith+Wealth+of+Nations) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Adam+Smith+Theory+of+Moral+Sentiments) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Adam+Smith+Theory+of+Moral+Sentiments) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Adam+Smith+Theory+of+Moral+Sentiments)
8. [ ] **Immanuel Kant — Critique of Pure Reason; of Practical Reason; of Judgment; Groundwork** (~30 h)
   - Read: Groundwork first; then the two Prefaces, Introduction, and Transcendental Aesthetic of the first Critique.
   - Edition: Groundwork tr. Mary Gregor (Cambridge); Critique of Pure Reason tr. Guyer and Wood (Cambridge) or Werner Pluhar (Hackett)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Kant+Critique+Pure+Reason) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Immanuel+Kant+Critique+of+Pure+Reason) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Immanuel+Kant+Critique+of+Pure+Reason) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Immanuel+Kant+Critique+of+Pure+Reason)
9. [ ] **Edmund Burke — Reflections on the Revolution in France** (~8 h)
   - Read: Written in 1790, before the Terror. His argument on inherited institutions.
   - Edition: Ed. J.G.A. Pocock (Hackett); or L.G. Mitchell (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Burke+Reflections+Revolution+France) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Edmund+Burke+Reflections+on+the+Revolution+in+France) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Edmund+Burke+Reflections+on+the+Revolution+in+France) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Edmund+Burke+Reflections+on+the+Revolution+in+France)
10. [ ] **Alexis de Tocqueville — Democracy in America** (~25 h)
   - Read: Volume I Part II ch. 7 (tyranny of the majority); Volume II Part IV on soft despotism.
   - Edition: Tr. Harvey Mansfield and Delba Winthrop (Chicago)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tocqueville+Democracy+America) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Alexis+de+Tocqueville+Democracy+in+America) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Alexis+de+Tocqueville+Democracy+in+America) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Alexis+de+Tocqueville+Democracy+in+America)
11. [ ] **Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel — The Phenomenology of Spirit; Science of Logic; Philosophy of Right** (~25 h)
   - Read: The Preface and the lordship-and-bondage section (paras. 178-196) of the Phenomenology, then the Philosophy of Right.
   - Edition: Phenomenology tr. A.V. Miller (Oxford), paragraph numbers as cited; Philosophy of Right ed. Allen Wood (Cambridge)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Georg+Wilhelm+Friedrich+Hegel+Phenomenology+of+Spirit) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Georg+Wilhelm+Friedrich+Hegel+Phenomenology+of+Spirit) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Georg+Wilhelm+Friedrich+Hegel+Phenomenology+of+Spirit)
12. [ ] **Arthur Schopenhauer — The World as Will and Representation; Essays and Aphorisms** (~12 h)
   - Read: World as Will Book IV, then a selection of the Essays.
   - Edition: Tr. E.F.J. Payne, The World as Will and Representation (Dover, 2 vols.); Essays and Aphorisms tr. R.J. Hollingdale (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Schopenhauer+Essays) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Arthur+Schopenhauer+World+as+Will+and+Representation) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Arthur+Schopenhauer+World+as+Will+and+Representation) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Arthur+Schopenhauer+World+as+Will+and+Representation)
13. [ ] **Soren Kierkegaard — Fear and Trembling; Either/Or; The Sickness Unto Death** (~10 h)
   - Read: Fear and Trembling on Abraham and Isaac (Genesis 22 from Phase 4).
   - Edition: Tr. Alastair Hannay, Fear and Trembling (Penguin); or Hong and Hong (Princeton)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Soren+Kierkegaard+Fear+and+Trembling) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Soren+Kierkegaard+Fear+and+Trembling) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Soren+Kierkegaard+Fear+and+Trembling)
14. [ ] **Karl Marx — Capital; Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts** (~15 h)
   - Read: The 1844 Manuscripts on estranged labor; Capital Volume I, chapter 1 and Part VIII.
   - Edition: The Marx-Engels Reader, ed. Robert Tucker (Norton); Capital vol. 1 tr. Ben Fowkes (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marx+Capital) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Karl+Marx+Capital) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Karl+Marx+Capital) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Karl+Marx+Capital)
15. [ ] **Charles Darwin — On the Origin of Species; The Voyage of the Beagle** (~15 h)
   - Read: Origin chapters 1-4 and 14 (recapitulation and conclusion).
   - Edition: On the Origin of Species, 1st edition (1859), Penguin Classics or Oxford World's Classics
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Darwin+Origin+of+Species) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Charles+Darwin+On+the+Origin+of+Species) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Charles+Darwin+On+the+Origin+of+Species) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Charles+Darwin+On+the+Origin+of+Species)
16. [ ] **Friedrich Nietzsche — The Birth of Tragedy; On the Genealogy of Morals; The Gay Science** (~12 h)
   - Read: Birth of Tragedy (it returns to Phase 2), Genealogy essay I, Gay Science 125 (the madman).
   - Edition: Basic Writings of Nietzsche, tr. Walter Kaufmann (Modern Library); The Gay Science tr. Kaufmann (Vintage)
