17. [ ] **William James — The Principles of Psychology; The Varieties of Religious Experience** (~12 h)
   - Read: The Varieties: lectures on the sick soul, conversion, and mysticism.
   - Edition: The Varieties of Religious Experience (Penguin / Library of America)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+James+Varieties) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=William+James+Principles+of+Psychology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=William+James+Principles+of+Psychology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+James+Principles+of+Psychology)
18. [ ] **Sigmund Freud — The Interpretation of Dreams; Civilization and Its Discontents** (~12 h)
   - Read: Civilization and Its Discontents in full; Interpretation of Dreams chapter 2.
   - Edition: Standard Edition, tr. James Strachey: Civilization and Its Discontents (Norton); The Interpretation of Dreams (Basic Books)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Freud+Interpretation+of+Dreams) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Sigmund+Freud+Interpretation+of+Dreams) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Sigmund+Freud+Interpretation+of+Dreams) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Sigmund+Freud+Interpretation+of+Dreams)
19. [ ] **Carl Gustav Jung — The Archetypes and the Collective Unconscious; Aion; Answer to Job; Memories, Dreams, Reflections** (~12 h)
   - Read: Memories, Dreams, Reflections first, then Answer to Job (after Phase 4's Job).
   - Edition: Memories, Dreams, Reflections, ed. Aniela Jaffe (Vintage); Answer to Job (Princeton, Bollingen)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Carl+Gustav+Jung+Archetypes+and+the+Collective+Unconscious) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Carl+Gustav+Jung+Archetypes+and+the+Collective+Unconscious) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Carl+Gustav+Jung+Archetypes+and+the+Collective+Unconscious)
20. [ ] **Albert Einstein — Relativity: The Special and the General Theory** (~5 h)
   - Read: Einstein's own popular account. Part I (special relativity).
   - Edition: Relativity: The Special and the General Theory, tr. Robert Lawson (Penguin / Crown)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Einstein+Relativity) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Albert+Einstein+Relativity%3A+The+Special+and+the+General+Theory) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Albert+Einstein+Relativity%3A+The+Special+and+the+General+Theory) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Albert+Einstein+Relativity%3A+The+Special+and+the+General+Theory)
21. [ ] **Werner Heisenberg — Physics and Philosophy** (~5 h)
   - Read: The Copenhagen interpretation and his comparison with Greek natural philosophy.
   - Edition: Physics and Philosophy (Harper Perennial Modern Classics)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Werner+Heisenberg+Physics+and+Philosophy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Werner+Heisenberg+Physics+and+Philosophy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Werner+Heisenberg+Physics+and+Philosophy)
22. [ ] **Erwin Schrodinger — What Is Life?** (~3 h)
   - Read: The 1943 lectures that influenced the discovery of DNA's structure.
   - Edition: What Is Life? (Cambridge, Canto Classics)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Erwin+Schrodinger+What+Is+Life%3F) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Erwin+Schrodinger+What+Is+Life%3F) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Erwin+Schrodinger+What+Is+Life%3F)
23. [ ] **Martin Heidegger — Being and Time** (~25 h)
   - Read: Introduction and Division I with a guide; Division II on death and authenticity.
   - Edition: Tr. Macquarrie and Robinson (Harper) or Joan Stambaugh (SUNY). Guide: Hubert Dreyfus, Being-in-the-World (MIT)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Martin+Heidegger+Being+and+Time) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Martin+Heidegger+Being+and+Time) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Martin+Heidegger+Being+and+Time)
24. [ ] **Ludwig Wittgenstein — Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus; Philosophical Investigations** (~15 h)
   - Read: The Tractatus (short, dense), then Investigations sections 1-133.
   - Edition: Tractatus tr. Pears and McGuinness (Routledge); Philosophical Investigations tr. Anscombe, Hacker and Schulte (Wiley-Blackwell)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Wittgenstein+Tractatus) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ludwig+Wittgenstein+Tractatus+Logico-Philosophicus) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ludwig+Wittgenstein+Tractatus+Logico-Philosophicus) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ludwig+Wittgenstein+Tractatus+Logico-Philosophicus)
25. [ ] **Simone Weil — Gravity and Grace; Waiting for God** (~5 h)
   - Read: Gravity and Grace is a compilation of notebook entries; read slowly.
   - Edition: Gravity and Grace, tr. Emma Crawford and Mario von der Ruhr (Routledge Classics)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Simone+Weil+Gravity+and+Grace) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Simone+Weil+Gravity+and+Grace) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Simone+Weil+Gravity+and+Grace)
26. [ ] **Hannah Arendt — The Human Condition; The Origins of Totalitarianism** (~15 h)
   - Read: The Human Condition: labor, work, and action. Origins Part III if time allows.
   - Edition: The Human Condition, 2nd ed. (Chicago); The Origins of Totalitarianism (Harcourt / Penguin)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hannah+Arendt+Human+Condition) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hannah+Arendt+Human+Condition) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hannah+Arendt+Human+Condition)

**Synthesis.** Trace the modern answer to 'what is a human being?' from Hume and Kant through Darwin, Freud, and Heidegger.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Benoit Mandelbrot — The Fractal Geometry of Nature; The (Mis)Behavior of Markets**: Works by mathematician Benoit Mandelbrot on fractal geometry, patterns that display self-similarity across different scales. The Fractal Geometry of Nature (1982) applies this framework to natural forms such as coastlines and clouds; The (Mis)Behavior of Markets (2004) applies related ideas to financial price movements.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Benoit+Mandelbrot+Fractal+Geometry+of+Nature) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Benoit+Mandelbrot+Fractal+Geometry+of+Nature) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Benoit+Mandelbrot+Fractal+Geometry+of+Nature)
- [ ] **Henri Poincare — Science and Hypothesis; The Value of Science**: Essay collections by mathematician and physicist Henri Poincare, published in the early twentieth century, on the philosophy of science. They examine the role of convention, hypothesis, and intuition in mathematics and physics, including reflections on questions later associated with relativity and chaos theory.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Henri+Poincare+Science+and+Hypothesis) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Henri+Poincare+Science+and+Hypothesis) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Henri+Poincare+Science+and+Hypothesis)
- [ ] **D'Arcy Wentworth Thompson — On Growth and Form**: A 1917 book by the Scottish biologist and mathematician D'Arcy Wentworth Thompson, analyzing biological form through physical and mathematical principles. It discusses examples including the logarithmic spiral of shells and the scaling of skeletal structure, arguing that physical forces shape organic growth alongside natural selection.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=D%27Arcy+Wentworth+Thompson+On+Growth+and+Form) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=D%27Arcy+Wentworth+Thompson+On+Growth+and+Form) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=D%27Arcy+Wentworth+Thompson+On+Growth+and+Form)
- [ ] **Richard Feynman — The Character of Physical Law; QED**: Books based on public lectures by physicist Richard Feynman. The Character of Physical Law (1965) discusses the nature of physical laws and the scientific method using examples from gravitation and conservation principles; QED (1985) explains the theory of quantum electrodynamics for a general audience.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Richard+Feynman+Character+of+Physical+Law) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Richard+Feynman+Character+of+Physical+Law) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Richard+Feynman+Character+of+Physical+Law)
- [ ] **James Gleick — Chaos: Making a New Science**: A 1987 popular science book by James Gleick describing the development of chaos theory. It covers topics including the butterfly effect, strange attractors, and fractal geometry, and profiles scientists who contributed to the field in the 1960s and 1970s.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=James+Gleick+Chaos%3A+Making+a+New+Science) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=James+Gleick+Chaos%3A+Making+a+New+Science) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=James+Gleick+Chaos%3A+Making+a+New+Science)
- [ ] **G.H. Hardy — A Mathematician's Apology**: A 1940 essay by mathematician G.H. Hardy reflecting on the nature and value of mathematics, particularly pure mathematics pursued without practical application. It discusses mathematical aesthetics and the criteria, such as elegance and depth, by which mathematicians judge their work.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=G.H.+Hardy+A+Mathematician%27s+Apology) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=G.H.+Hardy+A+Mathematician%27s+Apology) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=G.H.+Hardy+A+Mathematician%27s+Apology)
- [ ] **Primo Levi — The Periodic Table**: A 1975 memoir by chemist and writer Primo Levi, structured as twenty-one chapters each named after a chemical element. It combines autobiographical episodes, including his imprisonment at Auschwitz, with reflections drawn from his career as an industrial chemist.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Primo+Levi+Periodic+Table) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Primo+Levi+Periodic+Table) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Primo+Levi+Periodic+Table)
- [ ] **George Berkeley — Principles of Human Knowledge**: A 1710 work by the Irish philosopher and bishop George Berkeley, presenting the idealist position that material objects exist only as they are perceived (summarized in the phrase "esse est percipi," to be is to be perceived). Berkeley argued that the continued existence of unperceived objects depends on their being perceived by God. It is a foundational text of subjective idealism.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=George+Berkeley+Principles+of+Human+Knowledge) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=George+Berkeley+Principles+of+Human+Knowledge) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=George+Berkeley+Principles+of+Human+Knowledge)
- [ ] **Johann Gottlieb Fichte — The Science of Knowledge**: A philosophical work by Johann Gottlieb Fichte, first published in 1794, developing a system of transcendental idealism building on Kant's philosophy. Fichte grounds his system in an active, self-positing "I" that constitutes both itself and its experience of the world. He was an early figure in German Idealism.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Johann+Gottlieb+Fichte+Science+of+Knowledge) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Johann+Gottlieb+Fichte+Science+of+Knowledge) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Johann+Gottlieb+Fichte+Science+of+Knowledge)
- [ ] **Friedrich Schelling — System of Transcendental Idealism**: An 1800 work by the German philosopher Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling, part of the German Idealist tradition. It discusses the relationship between nature and self-consciousness and gives particular attention to the role of art as a point where conscious and unconscious activity converge. Schelling's philosophy of nature influenced German Romantic thought.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Friedrich+Schelling+System+of+Transcendental+Idealism) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Friedrich+Schelling+System+of+Transcendental+Idealism) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Friedrich+Schelling+System+of+Transcendental+Idealism)
- [ ] **Henri Bergson — Creative Evolution**: A 1907 work by the French philosopher Henri Bergson, which distinguishes lived, qualitative time (duration) from the quantitative time measured by clocks. It presents evolution as driven by a creative impulse he called the élan vital. Bergson received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1927.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Henri+Bergson+Creative+Evolution) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Henri+Bergson+Creative+Evolution) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Henri+Bergson+Creative+Evolution)
- [ ] **Edmund Husserl — Cartesian Meditations**: A work by the German philosopher Edmund Husserl, based on lectures delivered in 1929, presenting an introduction to his method of phenomenology. It describes the practice of bracketing (epoché) assumptions about the external world in order to examine the structures of consciousness directly. Husserl is regarded as the founder of phenomenology as a philosophical method.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Edmund+Husserl+Cartesian+Meditations) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Edmund+Husserl+Cartesian+Meditations) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Edmund+Husserl+Cartesian+Meditations)
- [ ] **Jean-Paul Sartre — Being and Nothingness**: A 1943 philosophical work by the French philosopher Jean-Paul Sartre, a major text of existentialist philosophy. It argues that existence precedes essence, meaning humans are not born with a fixed nature but define themselves through their choices, and discusses the concept of "bad faith" as self-deception about one's freedom. Sartre also addresses the human search for meaning without recourse to essentialist or theistic frameworks.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jean-Paul+Sartre+Being+and+Nothingness) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jean-Paul+Sartre+Being+and+Nothingness) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jean-Paul+Sartre+Being+and+Nothingness)
- [ ] **Alfred Adler — Understanding Human Nature**: A 1927 work by the Austrian psychiatrist Alfred Adler, another early associate of Freud who developed the school of individual psychology. It presents his theory that personality development centers on overcoming feelings of inferiority and seeking social belonging, introducing the concept of the "inferiority complex." Adler emphasized social interest and practical engagement as markers of psychological health.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Alfred+Adler+Understanding+Human+Nature) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Alfred+Adler+Understanding+Human+Nature) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Alfred+Adler+Understanding+Human+Nature)

<a id="phase-x"></a>

## Phase X: The Modern Imagination

*18th to 20th c.. About 397 hours of core reading.*

The novel, the lyric, and the drama of the last three centuries, read now because they respond to everything before them. Tolstoy and Dostoevsky answer Phase 9; Joyce rewrites the Odyssey; Eliot's Four Quartets draws on Phase 5's mystics; Tolkien draws on Phase 5's Norse and Anglo-Saxon material.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. What can the novel do that epic and tragedy could not?
2. How do Dostoevsky and Nietzsche respond differently to the same crisis?
3. Which ancient or medieval works does each modern writer rewrite?
4. How do the twentieth-century writers treat war?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Johann Wolfgang von Goethe — Faust; The Sorrows of Young Werther** (~12 h)
   - Read: Faust Part One in full; Part Two in selection.
   - Edition: Faust tr. Walter Arndt (Norton Critical Edition) or David Luke (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Goethe+Faust) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Johann+Wolfgang+von+Goethe+Faust) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Johann+Wolfgang+von+Goethe+Faust) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Johann+Wolfgang+von+Goethe+Faust)
2. [ ] **William Blake — Songs of Innocence and of Experience; The Marriage of Heaven and Hell** (~3 h)
   - Read: Paired poems: The Lamb and The Tyger.
   - Edition: Blake's Poetry and Designs (Norton Critical Edition); the William Blake Archive online shows the illuminated plates
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Blake+Songs+Innocence+Experience) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=William+Blake+Songs+of+Innocence+and+of+Experience) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=William+Blake+Songs+of+Innocence+and+of+Experience) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+Blake+Songs+of+Innocence+and+of+Experience)
3. [ ] **William Wordsworth — The Prelude; Lyrical Ballads** (~6 h)
   - Read: The 1802 Preface to Lyrical Ballads, 'Tintern Abbey', and Prelude Books I-II.
   - Edition: Wordsworth's Poetry and Prose (Norton Critical Edition); The Prelude 1799, 1805, 1850 (Norton)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Wordsworth+Lyrical+Ballads) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=William+Wordsworth+Prelude) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=William+Wordsworth+Prelude) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+Wordsworth+Prelude)
4. [ ] **Jane Austen — Pride and Prejudice; Emma** (~10 h)
   - Read: Pride and Prejudice, then Emma.
   - Edition: Penguin Classics or Oxford World's Classics
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Austen+Pride+and+Prejudice) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jane+Austen+Pride+and+Prejudice) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jane+Austen+Pride+and+Prejudice) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jane+Austen+Pride+and+Prejudice)
5. [ ] **Aleksandr Pushkin — Eugene Onegin** (~5 h)
   - Read: A novel in verse; the founding work of Russian literature.
   - Edition: Tr. James E. Falen (Oxford World's Classics) or Stanley Mitchell (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aleksandr+Pushkin+Eugene+Onegin) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aleksandr+Pushkin+Eugene+Onegin) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aleksandr+Pushkin+Eugene+Onegin)
6. [ ] **Herman Melville — Moby-Dick** (~20 h)
   - Read: Read the cetology chapters; they carry the book's argument.
   - Edition: Norton Critical Edition or Penguin Classics
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Melville+Moby+Dick) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Herman+Melville+Moby-Dick) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Herman+Melville+Moby-Dick) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Herman+Melville+Moby-Dick)
7. [ ] **Gustave Flaubert — Madame Bovary; Sentimental Education** (~10 h)
   - Read: Madame Bovary: Emma's reading shapes her life.
   - Edition: Tr. Lydia Davis (Penguin) or Margaret Mauldon (Oxford World's Classics)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Flaubert+Madame+Bovary) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Gustave+Flaubert+Madame+Bovary) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Gustave+Flaubert+Madame+Bovary) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Gustave+Flaubert+Madame+Bovary)

   - Read: Orthodoxy: a defense of belief against the thinkers of Phase 9.
   - Edition: Orthodoxy (Ignatius / Moody); The Everlasting Man (Ignatius)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Chesterton+Orthodoxy) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=G.K.+Chesterton+Orthodoxy) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=G.K.+Chesterton+Orthodoxy) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=G.K.+Chesterton+Orthodoxy)
21. [ ] **C.S. Lewis — The Abolition of Man; Till We Have Faces** (~6 h)
   - Read: The Abolition of Man (on the Tao across traditions) and Till We Have Faces (Cupid and Psyche from Apuleius, Phase 3).
   - Edition: The Abolition of Man (HarperOne); Till We Have Faces (Harcourt / HarperOne)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=C.S.+Lewis+Abolition+of+Man) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=C.S.+Lewis+Abolition+of+Man) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=C.S.+Lewis+Abolition+of+Man)
22. [ ] **Ernst Junger — Storm of Steel; On the Marble Cliffs** (~8 h)
   - Read: Storm of Steel: a German infantry officer's memoir of the Western Front.
   - Edition: Tr. Michael Hofmann, Storm of Steel (Penguin)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Ernst+Junger+Storm+of+Steel) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Ernst+Junger+Storm+of+Steel) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Ernst+Junger+Storm+of+Steel)
23. [ ] **Albert Camus — The Stranger; The Myth of Sisyphus** (~5 h)
   - Read: The Stranger, then The Myth of Sisyphus.
   - Edition: The Stranger tr. Matthew Ward (Vintage); The Myth of Sisyphus tr. Justin O'Brien (Vintage)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Albert+Camus+Stranger) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Albert+Camus+Stranger) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Albert+Camus+Stranger)
24. [ ] **Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn — The Gulag Archipelago; One Day in the Life of Ivan Denisovich** (~30 h)
   - Read: One Day in the Life of Ivan Denisovich first; The Gulag Archipelago in the one-volume abridgment.
   - Edition: One Day tr. H.T. Willetts (Farrar, Straus and Giroux); The Gulag Archipelago, one-volume abridgment by Edward E. Ericson Jr. (Harper Perennial)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Aleksandr+Solzhenitsyn+Gulag+Archipelago) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Aleksandr+Solzhenitsyn+Gulag+Archipelago) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Aleksandr+Solzhenitsyn+Gulag+Archipelago)
25. [ ] **Tolkien — The Lord of the Rings; The Silmarillion; The Hobbit** (~40 h)
   - Read: The Hobbit, then The Lord of the Rings. Note what it borrows from Beowulf, the Eddas, and the Kalevala.
   - Edition: HarperCollins or Houghton Mifflin editions
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Tolkien+Lord+of+the+Rings) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Tolkien+Lord+of+the+Rings) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Tolkien+Lord+of+the+Rings)

**Synthesis.** Pick one modern work and map its sources back through at least three earlier phases.

### Deeper shelf

- [ ] **Mishima — The Sea of Fertility; Confessions of a Mask; Sun and Steel; Patriotism**: Yukio Mishima (1925-1970) was a Japanese novelist and essayist. The Sea of Fertility is a four-novel cycle following reincarnation across twentieth-century Japan; Confessions of a Mask is a semi-autobiographical novel about a young man's concealed identity. Sun and Steel is a memoir-essay on bodybuilding and physical discipline, and Patriotism is a short story about a military officer's ritual suicide; Mishima died by seppuku in 1970 after a failed coup attempt.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Mishima+Sea+of+Fertility) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Mishima+Sea+of+Fertility) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Mishima+Sea+of+Fertility)
- [ ] **Knut Hamsun — Hunger; Growth of the Soil**: Knut Hamsun (1859-1952) was a Norwegian novelist. Hunger (1890) is a first-person narrative depicting a starving writer's deteriorating mental state in Christiania (Oslo); Growth of the Soil (1917), for which Hamsun received the Nobel Prize in Literature, follows a settler establishing a farm in rural Norway. Hamsun's later support for Nazi Germany during World War II remains a subject of biographical discussion.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Knut+Hamsun+Hunger) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Knut+Hamsun+Hunger) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Knut+Hamsun+Hunger)
- [ ] **Louis-Ferdinand Celine — Journey to the End of the Night**: Louis-Ferdinand Celine (1894-1961) was a French novelist. Journey to the End of the Night (1932) follows its narrator through World War I, colonial Africa, the United States, and postwar France, written in a colloquial, slang-inflected style. Celine's later writings included antisemitic pamphlets, a fact widely discussed alongside his literary work.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Louis-Ferdinand+Celine+Journey+to+the+End+of+the+Night) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Louis-Ferdinand+Celine+Journey+to+the+End+of+the+Night) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Louis-Ferdinand+Celine+Journey+to+the+End+of+the+Night)
- [ ] **Hermann Hesse — Siddhartha; The Glass Bead Game**: Hermann Hesse (1877-1962) was a German-Swiss writer. Siddhartha (1922) follows a man in ancient India across various stages of spiritual searching, including asceticism, worldly life, and eventual settlement by a river. The Glass Bead Game (1943), his final novel, is set in a future intellectual order centered on an abstract synthesis of the arts and sciences.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Hermann+Hesse+Siddhartha) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Hermann+Hesse+Siddhartha) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Hermann+Hesse+Siddhartha)
- [ ] **Georges Bernanos — The Diary of a Country Priest**: Georges Bernanos (1888-1948) was a French Catholic novelist. The Diary of a Country Priest (1936) is written as the journal of a young, physically ill priest serving an unwelcoming rural parish in northern France. The novel addresses themes of vocation, suffering, and grace.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Georges+Bernanos+Diary+of+a+Country+Priest) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Georges+Bernanos+Diary+of+a+Country+Priest) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Georges+Bernanos+Diary+of+a+Country+Priest)
- [ ] **Evelyn Waugh — Brideshead Revisited**: Evelyn Waugh (1903-1966) was an English novelist. Brideshead Revisited (1945) follows the narrator Charles Ryder's relationship, over roughly two decades, with the aristocratic and Catholic Flyte family. The novel addresses memory, class, and religious belief in early-to-mid twentieth-century England.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Evelyn+Waugh+Brideshead+Revisited) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Evelyn+Waugh+Brideshead+Revisited) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Evelyn+Waugh+Brideshead+Revisited)
- [ ] **Stendhal — The Red and the Black**: Stendhal (Marie-Henri Beyle, 1783-1842) was a French novelist. The Red and the Black (1830) follows Julien Sorel, a carpenter's son who advances through French society via the church and the aristocracy in the years following the Napoleonic era. The novel is known for its use of psychological interiority.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Stendhal+Red+and+the+Black) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Stendhal+Red+and+the+Black) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Stendhal+Red+and+the+Black)
- [ ] **Honore de Balzac — Pere Goriot; Lost Illusions**: Honore de Balzac (1799-1850) was a French novelist whose works were collected under the title The Human Comedy, a project intended to depict French society comprehensively after the Revolution. Pere Goriot (1835) follows an elderly man impoverished by his devotion to his two daughters; Lost Illusions (1837-43) follows a young provincial poet's entry into Parisian literary and journalistic life.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Honore+de+Balzac+Pere+Goriot) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Honore+de+Balzac+Pere+Goriot) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Honore+de+Balzac+Pere+Goriot)
- [ ] **Victor Hugo — Les Miserables; The Hunchback of Notre-Dame**: Victor Hugo (1802-1885) was a French novelist and poet. Les Miserables (1862) follows Jean Valjean, a former convict, across several decades of nineteenth-century France, addressing poverty, law, and redemption. The Hunchback of Notre-Dame (1831) is set in fifteenth-century Paris and centers on the bell-ringer Quasimodo and the cathedral of Notre-Dame.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Victor+Hugo+Les+Miserables) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Victor+Hugo+Les+Miserables) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Victor+Hugo+Les+Miserables)
- [ ] **Nikolai Gogol — Dead Souls; The Overcoat**: Nikolai Gogol (1809-1852) was a Russian writer of Ukrainian origin. Dead Souls (1842) follows a con man who travels the Russian provinces buying the legal titles to deceased serfs; The Overcoat (1842) is a short story about a poor clerk who saves for a new coat, only to have it stolen. Both works are noted for their satirical treatment of Russian bureaucracy.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Nikolai+Gogol+Dead+Souls) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Nikolai+Gogol+Dead+Souls) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Nikolai+Gogol+Dead+Souls)
- [ ] **William Faulkner — The Sound and the Fury; Absalom, Absalom!**: William Faulkner (1897-1962) was an American novelist. The Sound and the Fury (1929) and Absalom, Absalom! (1936) are set in the fictional Yoknapatawpha County, Mississippi, and use non-linear narration and multiple narrators to depict the decline of Southern families across generations. Faulkner received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1949.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=William+Faulkner+Sound+and+the+Fury) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=William+Faulkner+Sound+and+the+Fury) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+Faulkner+Sound+and+the+Fury)
- [ ] **Moliere — The Misanthrope; Tartuffe**: Moliere (Jean-Baptiste Poquelin, 1622-1673) was a French playwright and actor. Tartuffe (1664) concerns a household deceived by a religious hypocrite who ingratiates himself with its head; The Misanthrope (1666) follows a man who rejects the social conventions and flattery of his circle. Both are verse comedies addressing hypocrisy and manners in seventeenth-century French society.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Moliere+Misanthrope) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Moliere+Misanthrope) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Moliere+Misanthrope)
- [ ] **Jean Racine — Phedre; Andromache**: Jean Racine (1639-1699) was a French playwright associated with classical tragedy. Phedre (1677) dramatizes the story of Phaedra's forbidden love for her stepson Hippolytus, drawn from Greek mythology via Euripides and Seneca; Andromache (1667) follows the widow of Hector after the fall of Troy. Both plays follow the conventions of French neoclassical drama, including the unities of time, place, and action.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jean+Racine+Phedre) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jean+Racine+Phedre) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jean+Racine+Phedre)
- [ ] **Charles Baudelaire — Les Fleurs du Mal**: Charles Baudelaire (1821-1867) was a French poet. Les Fleurs du Mal (The Flowers of Evil), first published in 1857, is a collection of poems addressing urban life, beauty, sin, and mortality; parts of the original edition were prosecuted for obscenity under French law. The collection is often cited as an influence on later French Symbolist poetry.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Charles+Baudelaire+Les+Fleurs+du+Mal) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Charles+Baudelaire+Les+Fleurs+du+Mal) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Charles+Baudelaire+Les+Fleurs+du+Mal)
- [ ] **Walt Whitman — Leaves of Grass**: Walt Whitman (1819-1892) was an American poet. Leaves of Grass, first published in 1855 and revised across multiple editions through 1892, is a collection of poems written largely in free verse addressing democracy, the body, nature, and American national identity. "Song of Myself" is its best-known individual poem.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Walt+Whitman+Leaves+of+Grass) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Walt+Whitman+Leaves+of+Grass) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Walt+Whitman+Leaves+of+Grass)
- [ ] **Friedrich Holderlin — Hymns and Fragments**: Friedrich Holderlin (1770-1843) was a German poet. His hymns and later fragmentary poems, written between the 1790s and around 1806 when he began to suffer from mental illness, address classical Greek mythology, the divine, and the German landscape. His work received renewed critical attention in the twentieth century, including commentary by the philosopher Martin Heidegger.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Friedrich+Holderlin+Hymns+and+Fragments) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Friedrich+Holderlin+Hymns+and+Fragments) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Friedrich+Holderlin+Hymns+and+Fragments)
- [ ] **Jaroslav Hasek — The Good Soldier Svejk**: Jaroslav Hasek (1883-1923) was a Czech writer. The Good Soldier Svejk, published in installments beginning in 1921 and left unfinished at Hasek's death, follows a soldier in the Austro-Hungarian army during World War I whose exaggerated compliance and apparent simple-mindedness disrupt military bureaucracy. It is written in a comic, episodic style and satirizes military and imperial institutions.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Jaroslav+Hasek+Good+Soldier+Svejk) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Jaroslav+Hasek+Good+Soldier+Svejk) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Jaroslav+Hasek+Good+Soldier+Svejk)
- [ ] **Samuel Taylor Coleridge — The Rime of the Ancient Mariner**: Samuel Taylor Coleridge (1772-1834) was an English poet and critic. The Rime of the Ancient Mariner, first published in the 1798 Lyrical Ballads, is a narrative ballad about a sailor who kills an albatross and is subsequently cursed, undergoing a period of suffering and eventual partial redemption. It employs archaic language and a supernatural narrative frame.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Samuel+Taylor+Coleridge+Rime+of+the+Ancient+Mariner) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Samuel+Taylor+Coleridge+Rime+of+the+Ancient+Mariner) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Samuel+Taylor+Coleridge+Rime+of+the+Ancient+Mariner)
- [ ] **Lord Byron — Don Juan; Childe Harold's Pilgrimage**: Lord Byron (George Gordon Byron, 1788-1824) was an English poet. Childe Harold's Pilgrimage (1812-18) is a narrative poem following a disillusioned young traveler through Europe and popularized the archetype later termed the "Byronic hero." Don Juan (1819-24, unfinished) is a lengthy satirical narrative poem in comic verse.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Lord+Byron+Don+Juan) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Lord+Byron+Don+Juan) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Lord+Byron+Don+Juan)
- [ ] **Percy Bysshe Shelley — Prometheus Unbound**: Percy Bysshe Shelley (1792-1822) was an English Romantic poet. Prometheus Unbound (1820) is a lyrical drama reworking the Greek myth of Prometheus, in which the title figure is eventually freed and tyranny is overthrown; it reflects Shelley's political radicalism and interest in themes of liberty.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Percy+Bysshe+Shelley+Prometheus+Unbound) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Percy+Bysshe+Shelley+Prometheus+Unbound) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Percy+Bysshe+Shelley+Prometheus+Unbound)
- [ ] **John Keats — The Great Odes; Hyperion**: John Keats (1795-1821) was an English Romantic poet who died of tuberculosis at age twenty-five. His major odes, including "Ode to a Nightingale," "Ode on a Grecian Urn," and "To Autumn," were written in 1819 and address beauty, mortality, and time. Hyperion is an unfinished epic poem on the overthrow of the Titans by the Olympian gods.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=John+Keats+Great+Odes) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=John+Keats+Great+Odes) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=John+Keats+Great+Odes)
- [ ] **Alfred, Lord Tennyson — Idylls of the King; In Memoriam**: Alfred, Lord Tennyson (1809-1892) was the British Poet Laureate for much of the Victorian era. In Memoriam A.H.H. (1850) is a long elegy written over some seventeen years following the death of his close friend Arthur Hallam. Idylls of the King (1859-85) is a cycle of narrative poems retelling the Arthurian legend.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Alfred%2C+Lord+Tennyson+Idylls+of+the+King) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Alfred%2C+Lord+Tennyson+Idylls+of+the+King) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Alfred%2C+Lord+Tennyson+Idylls+of+the+King)
- [ ] **Emily Dickinson — The Complete Poems**: Emily Dickinson (1830-1886) was an American poet who spent most of her life in Amherst, Massachusetts, and published almost none of her roughly 1,800 poems during her lifetime. Her poems, most written in short, irregular stanzas with distinctive punctuation, address subjects including death, nature, and the mind. They were collected and published posthumously, beginning in 1890.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Emily+Dickinson+Complete+Poems) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Emily+Dickinson+Complete+Poems) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Emily+Dickinson+Complete+Poems)
- [ ] **William Butler Yeats — The Tower; Collected Poems**: William Butler Yeats (1865-1939) was an Irish poet and a leading figure in Irish literary and cultural life. His work spans early poems drawing on Irish myth and folklore to later collections, including The Tower (1928), addressing aging, Irish politics, and his personal symbolic system. "The Second Coming" (1919) is among his most frequently cited poems; Yeats received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1923.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=William+Butler+Yeats+Tower) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=William+Butler+Yeats+Tower) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=William+Butler+Yeats+Tower)
- [ ] **Fernando Pessoa — The Book of Disquiet; the heteronym poems**: Fernando Pessoa (1888-1935) was a Portuguese writer who composed much of his work under invented authorial personas, or "heteronyms," each with a distinct biography and style. The Book of Disquiet, assembled and published posthumously, is a fragmentary prose work presented as the reflections of a Lisbon office worker. Most of Pessoa's writing was published only after his death.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Fernando+Pessoa+Book+of+Disquiet) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Fernando+Pessoa+Book+of+Disquiet) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Fernando+Pessoa+Book+of+Disquiet)
- [ ] **Federico Garcia Lorca — Gypsy Ballads (Romancero Gitano)**: Federico Garcia Lorca (1898-1936) was a Spanish poet and playwright from Andalusia. Gypsy Ballads (1928) is a collection of narrative poems drawing on Andalusian folk balladry, the Andalusian landscape, and Romani culture. Lorca was killed in 1936 during the early months of the Spanish Civil War.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Federico+Garcia+Lorca+Gypsy+Ballads) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Federico+Garcia+Lorca+Gypsy+Ballads) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Federico+Garcia+Lorca+Gypsy+Ballads)
- [ ] **Pablo Neruda — Canto General**: Pablo Neruda (1904-1973) was a Chilean poet and diplomat. Canto General (1950) is a long poem in multiple sections addressing the history, geography, and peoples of Latin America, including a section devoted to the Inca site of Machu Picchu. Neruda received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1971.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Pablo+Neruda+Canto+General) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Pablo+Neruda+Canto+General) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Pablo+Neruda+Canto+General)
- [ ] **Charles Dickens — Bleak House; Great Expectations; David Copperfield**: Charles Dickens (1812-1870) was an English novelist whose works were largely published serially. Bleak House (1852-53) centers on a protracted Chancery lawsuit and addresses the English legal system; Great Expectations (1860-61) and David Copperfield (1849-50) are bildungsromans following their protagonists from childhood into adulthood. His novels frequently address poverty and social conditions in Victorian England.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Charles+Dickens+Bleak+House) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Charles+Dickens+Bleak+House) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Charles+Dickens+Bleak+House)
- [ ] **Henrik Ibsen — A Doll's House; Hedda Gabler; Peer Gynt**: Henrik Ibsen (1828-1906) was a Norwegian playwright. A Doll's House (1879) and Hedda Gabler (1890) are realist dramas addressing marriage, gender roles, and social convention in bourgeois households; Peer Gynt (1867) is an earlier verse drama following a wandering, self-mythologizing protagonist through fantastical episodes. Ibsen is frequently discussed as a significant figure in the development of modern realist theater.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Henrik+Ibsen+A+Doll%27s+House) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Henrik+Ibsen+A+Doll%27s+House) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Henrik+Ibsen+A+Doll%27s+House)
- [ ] **Giuseppe di Lampedusa — The Leopard**: Giuseppe Tomasi di Lampedusa (1896-1957) was an Italian, Sicilian nobleman and writer. The Leopard, published posthumously in 1958, follows a Sicilian aristocrat, Don Fabrizio, and his family during and after Garibaldi's unification campaign in 1860s Sicily. The novel addresses the decline of the old Sicilian aristocracy amid political and social change.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Giuseppe+di+Lampedusa+Leopard) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Giuseppe+di+Lampedusa+Leopard) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Giuseppe+di+Lampedusa+Leopard)
- [ ] **Yasunari Kawabata — Snow Country**: Yasunari Kawabata (1899-1972) was a Japanese novelist. Snow Country, published in stages between 1935 and 1947, follows a relationship between a Tokyo dilettante and a geisha in a remote hot-spring town in the mountains of western Japan. Kawabata received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1968.
  - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Yasunari+Kawabata+Snow+Country) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Yasunari+Kawabata+Snow+Country) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Yasunari+Kawabata+Snow+Country)

<a id="phase-xi"></a>

## Phase XI: Capstone: The Forbidden Shelf

*18th to 20th c.. About 69 hours of core reading.*

Works associated with controversial or extremist positions, placed last so they can be read against the full tradition they argue with. Recommended preparation: Plato's Republic, Hobbes, Burke, Hegel, Marx, and Nietzsche. Read each alongside a critical introduction or a serious critic, and note which earlier works each author claims or distorts.

**Questions to carry through the phase**

1. Which earlier work does each author claim as precedent, and is the reading faithful?
2. What does each author take to be the root problem of modernity?
3. Where does each argument depend on an assumption it does not defend?

### Core sequence

1. [ ] **Marquis de Sade — Philosophy in the Bedroom** (~5 h)
   - Read: The interpolated pamphlet 'Yet Another Effort, Frenchmen' states the political argument.
   - Edition: In Justine, Philosophy in the Bedroom, and Other Writings, tr. Seaver and Wainhouse (Grove)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Marquis+de+Sade+Philosophy+in+the+Bedroom) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Marquis+de+Sade+Philosophy+in+the+Bedroom) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Marquis+de+Sade+Philosophy+in+the+Bedroom)
2. [ ] **Joseph de Maistre — St. Petersburg Dialogues; Considerations on France** (~8 h)
   - Read: Considerations on France (1797): providence and the Revolution. Read against Burke.
   - Edition: Considerations on France, tr. Richard Lebrun (Cambridge Texts in the History of Political Thought)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Joseph+de+Maistre+St.+Petersburg+Dialogues) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Joseph+de+Maistre+St.+Petersburg+Dialogues) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Joseph+de+Maistre+St.+Petersburg+Dialogues)
3. [ ] **Max Stirner — The Ego and Its Own** (~10 h)
   - Read: Egoism against Feuerbach and Hegel. Marx and Engels answered it in The German Ideology.
   - Edition: Ed. David Leopold, The Ego and Its Own (Cambridge Texts in the History of Political Thought)
   - Find it: [Free public-domain text (Gutenberg search)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Stirner+Ego+and+His+Own) · [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Max+Stirner+Ego+and+Its+Own) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Max+Stirner+Ego+and+Its+Own) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Max+Stirner+Ego+and+Its+Own)
4. [ ] **Georges Sorel — Reflections on Violence** (~8 h)
   - Read: The theory of the political myth of the general strike.
   - Edition: Ed. Jeremy Jennings, Reflections on Violence (Cambridge Texts in the History of Political Thought)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Georges+Sorel+Reflections+on+Violence) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Georges+Sorel+Reflections+on+Violence) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Georges+Sorel+Reflections+on+Violence)
5. [ ] **Oswald Spengler — The Decline of the West** (~15 h)
   - Read: The Introduction and the morphology of cultures. Compare with Vico and Ibn Khaldun.
   - Edition: Tr. Charles Francis Atkinson (Knopf / Oxford), abridged one-volume edition
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Oswald+Spengler+Decline+of+the+West) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Oswald+Spengler+Decline+of+the+West) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Oswald+Spengler+Decline+of+the+West)
6. [ ] **Carl Schmitt — The Concept of the Political; Political Theology** (~6 h)
   - Read: The friend-enemy distinction; sovereignty as the decision on the exception. Compare with Hobbes.
   - Edition: The Concept of the Political, tr. George Schwab (Chicago); Political Theology, tr. Schwab (Chicago)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Carl+Schmitt+Concept+of+the+Political) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Carl+Schmitt+Concept+of+the+Political) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Carl+Schmitt+Concept+of+the+Political)
7. [ ] **Julius Evola — Revolt Against the Modern World; Ride the Tiger; Men Among the Ruins** (~12 h)
   - Read: Revolt Against the Modern World Part I. Note how he uses Phase 7 and the Hermetic sources.
   - Edition: Tr. Guido Stucco, Revolt Against the Modern World (Inner Traditions). Critical context: Mark Sedgwick, Against the Modern World (Oxford)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=Julius+Evola+Revolt+Against+the+Modern+World) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=Julius+Evola+Revolt+Against+the+Modern+World) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=Julius+Evola+Revolt+Against+the+Modern+World)
8. [ ] **E.M. Cioran — A Short History of Decay; The Trouble with Being Born** (~5 h)
   - Read: Aphorisms. Compare with Pascal and Schopenhauer.
   - Edition: A Short History of Decay and The Trouble with Being Born, tr. Richard Howard (Arcade)
   - Find it: [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/search?q=E.M.+Cioran+A+Short+History+of+Decay) · [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/search?query=E.M.+Cioran+A+Short+History+of+Decay) · [Project Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/search/?query=E.M.+Cioran+A+Short+History+of+Decay)

**Synthesis.** Choose one author and write the strongest response to them from within the earlier phases.

### Deeper shelf
